    package and the import are different words, which for pillow, pymupdf
    and python-docx they are. The window asks on every start, not only
    during an update, so a machine already broken repairs itself the next
    time the app opens.

    And once the extension is installed it is wired up there and then
    rather than after a restart: the Tcl package loads into a running
    interpreter perfectly well, and "restart to finish" is how a fix ends
    up not applied.

  - The bar drew itself, because the shape it was given back was not the
    shape it asked for.

    Reported as an eight-sided blob with its corners cut off at an angle.
    Measured off the screenshot: the end of the bar was inset about ten
    pixels where half of a forty-two-pixel bar is twenty-one — the edge
    ran nearly straight where it should have curved. Not reproducible
    here: the same call, at the same size, measured as a true half-circle
    every time it was asked for one, on the same code path.

    So the bar stopped sharing that machinery. It renders its own picture
    — track and fill together, one item, one reference — four times up
    and reduced. Measured against a real circle of the same radius it is
    within half a pixel at every size it is drawn at. There is a test
    that does that measurement, and another that runs the same
    measurement over a deliberately chamfered shape to prove the first
    one can fail.

    Two smaller things went with it. Both shapes now use the same radius,
    so a fill is the same shape as the thing it is filling at every width
    it passes through; and it is never narrower than it is tall, because
    below that a rounded rectangle is a slab with its corners nibbled
    whatever radius it is given.

    It also shrinks to fit a short box rather than being cut off by it. A
    pill with its top and bottom sliced away is not a shorter pill; it is
    a rectangle with two stubs.

  - The bar reports. The button instructs. They are different objects.

    The bar turned into the Download button when the work finished, so
    the same place in the window held a report at one moment and an
    instruction at the next, and they read as the same thing. The bar
    finishes full and stays a bar; the way to keep the result is its own
    button, under the box rather than inside it.

  - Fifteen more tests, and one that is only there to fail.

    The end of the bar against a true circle, at four sizes. A chamfered
    shape measured the same way, which must fail, so that the first check
    cannot quietly pass on a shape like the one that was reported. That
    the fill and the track share a radius. That the finished bar is still
    a bar and carries no words. That the button is built under the box
    and not in it. That what is asked about packages is what is missing
    rather than what has changed, that the other platform's packages are
    not counted as missing, and that a package whose import is a
    different word is not counted as missing either.


1.48.1

  - The bar was drawing pictures it did not keep, so they were collected
    while they were on the screen.

    On Windows and Linux a rounded shape is not drawn, it is a picture:
    rendered several times up, reduced, and placed on the canvas.
    `set_plate` hands that picture back, and the caller has to hold it —
    a PhotoImage with nothing referring to it is collected, and what is
    left on the canvas is an item pointing at an image that no longer
    exists. `ProgressBar` has always held its two. The new bar held
    neither.

    It survived anyway for a while, because those pictures come out of a
    cache shared by every shape in the window — so something else was
    holding them, by accident. That cache is bounded at four hundred and
    drops the oldest quarter when it fills, and a bar that repaints as it
    fills mints a new picture at every width it passes through. So the
    bar filled its own cache, evicted its own earlier pictures along with
    everything else in the window, and the shapes still on screen went
    with them. It read as a drawing fault and was a lifetime fault.

    It holds them now. It also rounds its width to four pixels rather
    than repainting at every one, which is the difference between a
    hundred and fifty pictures for one six-hundred-pixel bar and six
    hundred.

  - And the fill was not the shape it was drawn as.

    Its radius was capped at half its own width so that a short fill
    would not be rounder than it was long — which meant that at four per
    cent of a wide box it was a few pixels across, the full height of the
    bar, with corners rounded by half of almost nothing. A slab. The
    cap is gone: the fill keeps the bar's own radius the whole way
    across and is never shorter than it is tall, so it is a circle at its
    smallest and a stadium after that. One shape throughout.

  - A file can be taken back out.

    A cross beside the bar. After a translation it empties the box; while
    one is running it abandons it. A thread cannot be stopped from
    outside, so what actually happens is that the box's file gets a new
    number and the next thing the translation tries to report finds that
    number stale and gives up where it stands — nothing further is
    written and nothing further is said. A result that arrives from a
    run that was abandoned is thrown away rather than taking over the box
    a newer file is already using.

    It sits on the bar's row rather than beside the file's name, because
    the name is the first row dropped when the box is short — and a file
    put in by accident on a short box is exactly when it is wanted back.

  - The update that needed new packages did not install them.

    `requirements_changed` has always been able to tell that a build
    needs something the last one did not. What happened then was a status
    line reading "run setup.bat once after it restarts" and a restart
    four hundred milliseconds later, so the sentence was on screen for
    less time than it takes to read it and the packages were never
    installed by anything.

    It had never mattered, because no release had added a package since
    that code was written. 1.48.0 added one — the drag-and-drop
    extension — and so shipped a feature that silently did nothing for
    everybody who updated from inside the app, which is everybody.

    The updater installs them: the same one command the setup scripts
    run, with the interpreter the app is already running under. If that
    fails it says so and does not restart, because a restart would
    replace the only sentence saying what is still to be done with a
    window that looks perfectly fine.

  - The box no longer promises what the machine cannot do.

    Drag and drop is an extension, and where it is missing a file
    dropped on the box goes nowhere. The words underneath said "Drag &
    drop an image, or click to upload" either way — describing, on those
    machines, something that was never going to happen, and burying the
    one way in that did work at the end of the sentence. Where the
    extension is not there it says "Click to upload an image or PDF", and
    either way the answer goes in the log, so "drag and drop does not
    work" can be settled from a log file rather than from a screenshot.

  - A close mark, which this set did not have.

    Two strokes on the true diagonal, crossing at the middle of the
    square — so the two halves of each stroke are mirror images, and
    anything uneven is uneven in both and reads as weight rather than as
    wobble. The diagonal is the one angle other than the axes that steps
    evenly rather than in a limp; see the note on the gear's teeth in
    1.47.4.

  - Fifteen more tests.

    The fill is never shorter than it is tall, at any percentage; the
    radius cap that made it a slab cannot come back; the pictures are
    held; the bar has a width of its own, so that nothing can fall back
    to the canvas default of three hundred and seventy-eight pixels and
    hang out of a box narrower than that; the cross is on the bar's row
    and survives a box too short for anything else; the words match what
    the machine can do; and an update that needs packages installs them
    and does not restart over its own bad news.


1.48.0

  - The box said "drag and drop a file here" and, on Windows, meant
    nothing by it.

    Tk has no drag and drop of its own. tkdnd is a Tcl extension and
    tkinterdnd2 is what puts it on the machine — and it was installed by
    the Mac's setup script and by nothing at all on Windows. It was not
    in requirements.txt, which is the one list both installers read, so
    every Windows install has shown a dashed box inviting a file and
    silently ignored every file dropped on it. For however long the box
    has been there.

    It goes in requirements.txt rather than into setup.bat beside the
    Mac's copy, because that file is also what the updater watches: an
    existing machine reinstalls its packages when it changes, so this
    arrives on the next update rather than only on a fresh install.

    Two things found while checking it worked. Nothing was bound to
    <<DropEnter>> or <<DropLeave>>, so even where the extension was
    installed the box gave no sign it would take the file — a drop about
    to succeed looked exactly like one about to do nothing. And only the
    zone's own frame was registered as a target: the canvas and the
    label sit on top of it, so the middle of the box, which is what
    anybody aims at, was not a drop target at all.

  - A file was reported on in three places, none of them where it had
    been put.

    Progress was a hairline band above the panes. The sentence explaining
    it was under that band. The button that saved the result was at the
    far end of a different row, beside the section heading. Three places
    for one thing, and the one that had to be acted on was the easiest of
    the three to miss: a file could finish translating and sit there
    unsaved, because nothing in the part of the window being watched had
    changed.

    The box a file was dropped into now reports on that file. The name,
    a pill that fills left to right, and a line under it saying what is
    happening. When the work is done the same pill becomes the button
    that keeps the result. There is nothing to notice, because the thing
    already being watched is the thing that becomes the button.

    The old three are removed rather than hidden. `_set_progress`,
    `_show_file_area`, the band, the status line and the top-row save
    button are gone. A hidden widget and a method with no callers are an
    invitation to report a file through them again, which is how there
    came to be three places in the first place.

  - The bar could not have been a bar, because nothing counted.

    The translator's progress callback took a sentence and nothing else:
    "page 3…". The window turned that into a fraction by guessing — nought
    at the start, a third for anything in the middle, one at the end — so
    a six-page document moved the bar twice. It now reports which page of
    how many alongside the sentence, and the window turns that into a
    share of the whole.

    The share stops at 94 per cent until the file is actually written.
    A bar sitting full while the document is still being saved says the
    work is over when it is not, and that is a worse lie than a bar that
    is a little behind.

  - Why the filling bar carries no words.

    A label inside a half-filled pill is half on the blue and half on the
    pale, and there is no one colour that reads on both: dark disappears
    into the blue, light disappears into the pale. The canvas cannot clip
    text, so it cannot draw the label in two colours, and switching
    colour at the halfway mark puts the change exactly where the words
    are. So while there is progress to show, the bar shows progress and
    the words sit under it on the card. When it finishes there is one
    ground left, and the label goes back inside — which is also the first
    moment the label is worth reading, because it has become the thing to
    click.

    On a short box the file name is dropped before the progress line, and
    the bar is never dropped: it is the only row that can be acted on.

  - One that fell out of the tests rather than out of the change.

    A proportions test walked the page that happened to be open and
    measured every pill on it. Moving the save button off the home page
    left that page with no pill of its kind at all, so the check quietly
    had nothing to measure — a test that can be emptied out by moving a
    button. Walking both panes instead found a real fault: the "Remove"
    button in the offline languages list was handed a display-scaled
    padding where every other pill in the app takes a plain default, so
    on a display scaling below 1.0 it was the only button in the window
    whose label lost its breathing space while every pill beside it kept
    theirs.

  - Twenty-one tests, and one piece of hardening they found.

    The package is in the list both installers read, and not behind a
    platform marker; the updater watches that file; every part of the box
    takes a drop; the bar reaches the end only when the work does; a bar
    that is still a bar cannot be clicked; the words are never inside it
    while it fills; a click cannot open a file chooser over a running
    translation.

    Writing them turned up this: every reporting call on the box did
    nothing at all unless the box had first been told a file was coming.
    A translation that finished without that announcement was dropped on
    the floor — no button to collect it and nothing on screen to say it
    had happened. Reporting a result is never the wrong thing to do, so
    it no longer depends on having been announced.


1.47.4

  - The icons were a different weight at every size, and it was called
    pixellation for five releases.

    Every icon is written as shapes in a 24-unit square and asks for a
    stroke of 1.9 of those units. On a Mac nothing is a picture: the
    icons are canvas lines and arcs, drawn by the system at the screen's
    own resolution, and the canvas takes a stroke as a screen distance,
    which may be a fraction. It was being handed a whole number. 1.9
    units on a seventeen-pixel icon is 1.35 points, and rounded that is
    1 — twenty-six per cent thin. Three pixels larger, at twenty, it is
    1.58, and rounded that is 2 — twenty-six per cent heavy. So two
    icons a few pixels apart in size, side by side in the same window,
    differed in weight by more than half. The thin one looked ragged and
    the fat one looked soft, and both were blamed on the shapes.

    Nothing was gained by the rounding; it was there because a pixel
    count feels like it ought to be a whole number. The fraction goes
    straight through now, with a floor of 0.75 so that an icon drawn very
    small still has a line rather than gaps.

  - The same fault, smaller, on Windows and Linux.

    There an icon is rendered by the imaging library several times up and
    reduced, because that canvas has no anti-aliasing at all. The library
    does take a whole number of pixels, so the rounding is unavoidable —
    but how far up to draw was fixed at four, and four was chosen for
    smoothness without anyone checking what it did to the weight. At four
    times up, 1.9 units on a seventeen-pixel icon is 5.38 pixels and 5
    gets drawn: seven per cent thin. At eighteen pixels it is 6.0 exactly
    and 2.000 units get drawn. Thirteen per cent apart, two adjacent
    sizes.

    So the multiple is no longer fixed. Every weight the icon uses is
    asked what it would come out as at each multiple from eight to
    twenty-four, and the one that gets them all closest wins. The worst
    error anywhere is now 1.8 per cent, against seven. Measured against a
    forty-eight-times reference, the average error of a finished pixel
    went from 6.99 grey levels to 1.82. Every icon at five sizes renders
    in 91ms, once, and is cached.

  - A dark rim around every pale icon, from the reduction itself.

    The icon was drawn in its own colour on a transparent square and then
    shrunk. A transparent pixel is stored as transparent *black*, the
    reduction averages those blacks into the colour of every edge pixel,
    and white-on-blue comes out with a grey fringe. The coverage is now
    drawn on its own and the colour put in flat afterwards, so every
    pixel is the same colour and only its opacity varies — the fringe
    cannot happen. The reduction is a plain average of a whole number of
    source pixels, which is the exact answer and leaves none of the
    ringing a sharpening filter puts along an edge.

  - The moon's horns were patched across a gap.

    A crescent is the gap between two circles and both edges have to be
    traced, which was right. Where the two arcs *stop* was guessed: they
    ended near each other but not on each other, and the outline closed
    across the tip with a stub. On screen that is a horn that is blunt,
    or forked, or has a little hook on it. The two circles cross at
    exactly two points and those are solved for now, so each horn is one
    place both curves genuinely reach. The sampling went from
    twenty-two steps per arc to sixty-four; at twenty-two the joints near
    the tips were three degrees apart and half a pixel of facet showed.

  - The sun's rays and the gear's teeth were on the worst possible angles.

    A short stroke at an angle is the hardest thing there is to draw
    cleanly at seventeen pixels: it lands across the pixel grid rather
    than along it. The gear had eight teeth at 22.5 degrees, which put
    every one of them on a diagonal, and it read as a ring of lint. They
    start at 0 now, so four of the eight are exactly on the vertical and
    horizontal. The sun had 2.8-long rays on a 4.2 disc — four ragged
    hairs and four clean ones. They are 2.0 on a 5.0 disc now: the disc
    carries the shape and the rays are ticks around it.

    The gear's ring is also drawn heavier than the rest of it. At one
    weight throughout, eight teeth on a thin ring is a starburst, and
    there is already a starburst in this set, in the same window.

  - Fifteen tests hold all of it.

    Stroke weight within two per cent of what was asked for, at every
    size from twelve to forty-four, for every icon; neighbouring sizes
    within four per cent of each other, which is the fault as it was
    actually seen; no rounding anywhere in the canvas path; the moon's
    two curves ending in the same two places; the sun's and the gear's
    eight spokes equal in length with four of them on the grid.


1.47.3

  - A width that was measured, and so was different every time.

    `_even_wraps` gave every grey line in Settings one width, which was
    the point — but it worked that width out by asking each row how much
    room it had beside its own control and taking the narrowest answer.
    A widget asked how wide it is before it has been laid out says one
    pixel. One row caught early, and the guard at 120 not catching it,
    and every line in the pane wrapped into a column a third of the
    width it should have had.

    Whether that happened depended on when `settle` ran relative to the
    pane being mapped, which is why the same page, opened twice minutes
    apart, produced two different screenshots — and why a PC and a Mac
    disagreed about it.

    It is a decision now, not a measurement. MainWindow.CONTROL_W is 380
    of the design's own pixels; the text gets the card less that and
    less the row's padding. The card's width is the one thing here that
    is stable — it follows the window and the window does not resize.
    Opened and closed four times in a row, on two screen sizes: one wrap
    width, one key-box width, identical every time.

    The key boxes are sized from the same number rather than from what
    their own buttons left over. They are the widest controls in the
    pane, so sizing them independently is what let them stick out past
    every other row.

  - The brand tile's corners were a staircase everywhere, always.

    PIL's ImageDraw.rounded_rectangle has no anti-aliasing: every pixel
    is in or out. For a filled shape whose colour is blurred over it
    afterwards that does not matter. For a *mask* it is the whole thing
    — the mask is what decides where the tile stops, so a hard mask
    gives a hard edge however smooth the gradient under it is.

    `rounded_image` in the kit has always drawn at four times the size
    and reduced, for exactly this reason. `three_stop` never did; it is
    the one shape in the app that was never routed through the kit. So
    the mark in the corner of every window has had four stepped corners
    since it was drawn, on Windows, on Linux and on macOS, and it
    survived a fortnight of fixes aimed at the platform rather than at
    the shape.

    `_round_mask` draws at four times and reduces with LANCZOS. There is
    a test that asks the mask for its middle values: a mask with none is
    a staircase.

1.47.2

  - Drawn, at full resolution, and still faceted.

    With the plates no longer clipped it was worth measuring what they
    actually looked like. On a screenshot of the front page, counting
    how many pixels an edge takes to go from background to ink:

        theme pill, rounded end     65% of edges 1 px
        Settings pill               45%
        brand tile corners          43%
        crop icon                   57%
        the word "Squint"            1%   (mean 2.29)
        the headline                11%   (mean 2.14)

    The shapes are now sharper than the type beside them, which is the
    right way round and the opposite of where this started. Text on
    macOS is smoothed across about two device pixels by the system and
    is not this app's to change.

    What was left was geometry, not resolution. Every corner was eight
    straight segments. A pill's end is a quarter circle of radius
    nineteen points; eight segments across that is a joint every 3.7
    device pixels, and at a zoom you can count them. `corner_steps`
    takes two segments per point of radius now — a joint every 1.57
    device pixels, below the scale of the anti-aliasing, with a floor of
    ten and a ceiling of ninety-six so a plate never becomes slower to
    draw than the picture it replaced.

  - And the brand tile had bites out of two edges.

    The corner cuts ran past the tile at the corner point and stopped
    exactly on the edge at both ends of their arc. The polygon then
    closed from the end of the arc straight back to the corner — a line
    that crosses the flat edge of the tile and removes a notch from it.
    Two on the left edge and one along the top, visible in any
    screenshot of the mark. The overshoot goes on the arc's ends now as
    well as on the corner.

1.47.1

  - The drawn shapes were being cut off at their own edges.

    1.47.0 moved every pill, field, card, keycap and track from a pasted
    picture to a canvas polygon, and drew each one from (0, 0) to the
    full width and height of its widget. A canvas clips at that
    boundary. The outermost pixels of a rounded corner are exactly the
    ones an anti-aliased curve needs most — the soft ones that make it
    read as a curve — and they were landing on the boundary and being
    cut. What came back was a pill with both ends sliced flat.

    It could not be seen from here: on Windows and Linux there is no
    anti-aliasing, so there is nothing at the edge to lose, and the
    Linux screenshots looked correct.

    ui_kit.BLEED is 1.5 and every plate is inset by it plus half the
    stroke width. A pill three pixels narrower than its widget is
    invisible; a pill with a flat end is not. There is a test that
    builds a plate at five sizes and checks its outline stays a pixel
    clear of the widget on all four sides.

    The brand tile had the same fault inverted. Its corners are cut out
    of the gradient by canvas polygons, and a cut that stops at the edge
    leaves the outermost pixel of the corner behind — one pixel at each
    of four corners, which is what a stepped corner is. The cut runs two
    pixels past the edge now.

  - Three key rows, three different shapes.

    The box and the button on each key row were each taking what the
    other left, and the buttons carry different words — "Get your free
    key", "Set up Google", "Get a DeepL key". So the three boxes came
    out three widths and ended in three places, down a card whose whole
    point is that its rows line up.

    `_square_up_keys` runs from `settle`, gives every button the widest
    label's width and every box the same width. It is capped well below
    what would fit, because of the next one.

  - Every grey line in Settings ended somewhere different.

    Each was wrapped against its own row: the row's width less whatever
    control sat beside it. Every row has a different control — a switch,
    a dropdown, a button, a shortcut field — so every line had a
    different right-hand edge. Eleven distinct wrap widths in one pane,
    measured.

    `_even_wraps` takes the narrowest row and gives its width to all of
    them, so the text ends on one edge. That is also why the key boxes
    are capped: the key rows have the widest controls in the pane, so a
    box taking half its row would squeeze the text under every other row
    in Settings.

    A card's closing footnote is not one of these. Nothing sits beside
    it, so it gets the whole card — giving it the rows' width would wrap
    a full-width line into a narrow column with half the card empty next
    to it.

    `_wrap_to_fit`, which does the same job correctly for everything
    else in the window, now skips the lines these two own.

1.47.0

  - Every shape in the app is drawn on macOS instead of pasted.

    Seven reports across five releases, and four of them answered by
    changing how one thing was drawn. This is the whole of it.

    Tk has no rounded anything. Every pill, button, field, card, keycap,
    switch track, dropdown and scrollbar in this app was a PhotoImage:
    rendered with PIL at four times the size, reduced, cached, and
    placed on a canvas with create_image. On Windows and Linux that is
    not a choice — the canvas there has no anti-aliasing whatsoever and
    a drawn curve is a staircase.

    On macOS it is the worst available option. The Aqua canvas draws
    through CoreGraphics: it anti-aliases, and it draws at the display's
    own resolution, two device pixels to the point on a Retina screen.
    A PhotoImage does not. Tk has no way to be told that an image is two
    pixels to the point, so it draws one image pixel to the point and
    stretches. Every shape in the window was therefore at half the
    resolution of the text beside it.

    ui_kit.VECTOR_PLATES is true on darwin. Three functions carry it:

      _round_path(x, y, w, h, radius)  the outline of a rounded
          rectangle as a flat list of points. One closed polygon rather
          than four arcs and two rectangles, because a seam between an
          arc and a rectangle is a hairline of background colour on
          exactly the curves this exists to smooth.
      plate_item(canvas)  the item a plate lives in, made once. Every
          widget here paints by keeping one item and reconfiguring it,
          and that is what holds the stacking order — the plate is made
          first so everything after it is on top. A fresh polygon per
          repaint would land on top of the text.
      set_plate(canvas, item, ...)  gives that item the shape and
          colours. Returns the PhotoImage to keep alive, or None when it
          was drawn, so every caller assigns what comes back exactly as
          it did before.

    Twenty-five call sites across ui_kit, ui_home, shortcut_field and
    ui_result. The switch knob went from a shadowed circle picture to a
    canvas oval; the shadow goes with it, and at that size on that track
    what the shadow was doing was softening a bitmap edge.

    The brand tile is the one shape that cannot be a primitive: it is a
    gradient, a different colour at every pixel. Its *corners* could be,
    and the corners were the stepped part. The gradient is rendered as a
    plain square now and the four corners are cut out of it afterwards by
    canvas polygons in the colour behind the mark. A flat gradient
    cannot look stepped; only an edge can, and every edge is drawn.

    What remains a picture, counted by the test rather than asserted:
    the photograph, the animated answer card composed over it, the brand
    gradient, and one crop of the photograph under each of the three
    controls that stand on the band. Six.

    tests/test_nothing_is_a_bitmap.py forces the macOS path on and walks
    the built window counting image items by owner, so "I changed the
    drawing" can no longer be confused with "nothing is a bitmap". It
    also checks the plates are polygons rather than absent — a widget
    that stopped painting would otherwise pass the count and look like a
    fix.

1.46.1

  - The pixellation on a Mac was never the drawing.

    Three releases went into it. 1.42 moved every icon off Tk's canvas
    and onto rendered pictures, because the canvas has no anti-aliasing
    on Windows or Linux. 1.46.0 moved them back for macOS, on the theory
    that a PhotoImage is drawn at one image pixel to the point while
    text is drawn at the screen's resolution. Each release was reported
    as still pixellated, which should have been the end of the theory
    and was not.

    What settled it was measuring the right thing. Take a screenshot of
    the window and, at every edge, count how many pixels the colour
    takes to get from the background to the ink. A shape drawn at the
    screen's real resolution ramps in one pixel. A 1x rendering scaled
    up by two ramps in two. On the reported screenshot:

        the word "Squint"      2 px  (348 of 425 edges exactly 2)
        the mark above it      2 px

    The *text* ramps in two pixels. Text is drawn by the system, not by
    this app, and it cannot be blamed on a PhotoImage. So the whole
    window is being rendered at one pixel to the point and scaled up
    afterwards, and no change to how any one thing is drawn can reach
    it. That is why none of them did.

    A Mac process is drawn at full resolution when its main bundle says
    NSHighResolutionCapable. Squint.app's Info.plist says so. But the
    process is only inside Squint.app when the bundle's launcher uses
    the interpreter it keeps in Contents/MacOS — and it falls back to
    .venv/bin/python whenever that copy cannot see the packages, which
    is a case the launcher deliberately handles and logs. Add
    Squint.command, a Terminal launch and debug.command, and the common
    case is a process whose main bundle is the Python framework, whose
    Info.plist is not this project's to set.

    So it is asked for at runtime: platform_util._mac_high_resolution
    sets the flag on the main bundle's info dictionary, from the first
    line of main, ahead of Tk and ahead of anything that could put a
    window up. It is the same call the Windows DPI step already made
    there, for the same reason, on the other platform — and macOS was
    documented in that function as needing none of it.

    It cannot be tested from a Linux container, so tools/retina_check.py
    ships with it: it prints the window's backing scale and draws one
    shape three ways — as a picture, as canvas primitives, and as text.
    Two is right. One means the window is still at half resolution and
    says so plainly, rather than leaving it to be guessed at from a
    screenshot for a fourth time.

  - Five dropdowns in Settings sat in a square of the page's colour.

    A Dropdown is a pill drawn on a canvas, and a canvas is a rectangle.
    What shows at the four corners is the widget's own background, which
    has to be the colour of whatever it was put on. Five of them were
    left to default, and the default was the page rather than the card —
    invisible in light, where the two are close, and a dark square in
    dark, where they are not.

    Defaulting to the page was the mistake. It defaults to the parent's
    colour now, which is right nearly every time and wrong only where
    somebody has built something unusual — and in that case it can still
    be told.

1.46.0

  - A small box that scrolls is worse than one that does not.

    The report box on Support was four lines with a scrollbar. Put the
    pointer over it on the way down the page and the page stops: the
    wheel belongs to whichever widget is under the pointer, a Text
    handles it itself, and the toplevel's own wheel handler deliberately
    stepped aside for any Text it saw. Nothing starts again until the
    pointer is moved off the box, which nobody does deliberately.

    Growing is the fix rather than forwarding the wheel. A box with
    nothing in it to scroll has no claim on the wheel to begin with, and
    what has been written is all visible at once instead of four lines of
    it. TextPane takes `grows`, `limit` and `max_lines`; the report box
    is `grows=True, limit=500`. The limit is enforced at the keystroke
    for typing and by trimming for a paste — a key that would take it
    past the limit returns "break", and anything that arrives by other
    means is cut at 500.

    The toplevel handler now steps aside only for a Text that has not
    said otherwise (`pass_wheel_up`).

  - Icons on a Retina Mac were drawn at half resolution.

    1.42 moved every icon from Tk's canvas primitives to a rendered
    picture, because the canvas has no anti-aliasing at all on Windows
    and Linux and a gear at seventeen pixels came out as a handful of
    stepped dots. That was right for those two platforms and exactly
    wrong for the third.

    Tk's Aqua canvas draws through CoreGraphics. It anti-aliases, and it
    draws at the display's own resolution — two device pixels to the
    point on a Retina screen. A PhotoImage does not: Tk has no way to be
    told an image is two pixels to the point, so it is drawn one image
    pixel to the point and stretched to fill. Text is drawn by
    CoreText at full resolution, which is why the words on the same
    screen look sharp and everything drawn beside them does not.

    Measured off a screenshot of the Settings pane, counting distinct
    grey levels along horizontal edges in a 30x30 box:

        "Detect language"   165
        globe icon            8
        lightning icon        0

    `ui_icons.DRAWN_BY_HAND` is true on darwin and `draw` takes the
    canvas path there. The picture path is untouched on Windows and
    Linux.

    Not fixed by this, and worth knowing: every rounded plate in the app
    is a PhotoImage too, and so is the photograph at the top. The plates
    could take the same treatment — a rounded rectangle is drawable with
    canvas primitives — and the photograph cannot, because it is a
    photograph. This changes the icons, which is what was reported.

  - The keys rows tell you how to get a key.

    Save & test sat on all three rows: one button repeated three times,
    for a job that is not per-row at all. What is per-row is where the
    key comes from, and that differs — Gemini hands one out from AI
    Studio, Google wants a project in the console, DeepL wants an
    account. So each row has that, and testing moved under the rows with
    the rest of the help.

    Keys save on leaving the box and on Return. `_save_keys(quiet=True)`
    saves without testing; testing every time the pointer left a field
    would spend somebody's daily allowance on a keystroke. Test
    connection saves quietly first, because focus does not move off a
    field on every platform when a button is pressed and a test that
    quietly used the previous key is worse than no test.

    The written and picture guides are three fixed links now rather than
    a button per installed guide. The row used to have four buttons on a
    machine with both picture guides and two on a machine with neither,
    so no two people could be told "the third one along".

1.45.0

  - The theme change is instant, and the fade is gone.

    Four releases went into that fade and each one made it smoother
    without making it better, because it was the wrong instrument. What
    it faded was the window's opacity: the whole window down to almost
    nothing, the contents rebuilt while it could not be seen, the window
    back up. That is a blink with the change hidden inside it. It is not
    a transition between two colours and no amount of easing turns it
    into one — which is why the report kept coming back as "it blinks"
    however well the ramp behaved.

    The honest options were a real colour morph — interpolate the whole
    palette and re-apply it to every widget, with the drawn parts (the
    pills, the band's veil, the icons) still having to swap at some
    single moment because they are pictures and not colours — or no
    transition at all. No transition is what was asked for and it is
    also the better answer: there is nothing to look at, so nothing has
    to be hidden.

    `fade`, `set_alpha`, FLOOR and GLOW_STOPS are deleted rather than
    left unused. An unused fade in a file is a thing somebody reaches
    for later.

    What this leaves is the rebuild, and it is about twice as quick
    without 640 milliseconds of ramp wrapped round it: 1.1 seconds to
    just under 0.5 on the machine these are measured on, of which the
    page rebuild is 260ms and the picture at the top 140ms. The window
    is never replaced, so its position and size need no restoring.

1.44.2

  - Every button in the app was deaf.

    1.44.0 added an icon to one button. The edit that did it inserted a
    new method into PillButton.__init__ between the last line of setup
    and the four bind() calls that follow it:

        self._paint()

        def _measure(self, text):
            return (...)

            self.bind("<ButtonRelease-1>", self._release)

    The binds are inside _measure, after its return. They never run.
    Python has nothing to say about unreachable code, the widgets are
    built and laid out and painted exactly as before, and in a diff the
    four lines are still there.

    It shipped twice. Check for updates and Install and restart are how
    somebody leaves a build, so the build they could not leave from was
    the one that had this in it.

    Why the suite did not catch it is the part worth keeping. Every test
    that pressed a button called `button.command()` or `button._release`
    directly. That tests the code behind the button and says nothing
    about the button, which is the half that was broken. Buttons are
    pressed with `event_generate` now, on a mapped window, and
    `test_every_button_is_clickable` walks the real window with every
    pane open and asks each one what it is bound to.

    The same file scans the whole of app/ for a statement following a
    return, raise, break or continue in the same block. That check is
    two dozen lines and finds this class of mistake anywhere it happens,
    not only in a button.

1.44.1

  - Centred type was not centred.

    Tk centres a line box: ascent plus descent, the full height the font
    reserves. The descent is empty under every word that has no
    descender in it, so centring the box puts the letters low by half of
    it. Measured on the Settings pill at the app's own size: the gear
    beside the word is placed geometrically and sits dead centre, the
    word sits two pixels below it, and "Settings" has a g in it so its
    ink reaches three and a half pixels below. Small, and the sort of
    thing that is much easier to see than to name.

    `theme.middle(height, font)` centres the band the eye actually
    reads, from the top of the capitals to the baseline. Tk reports a
    font's ascent and descent and has no idea where the capitals stop,
    so the cap height is taken as a share of the ascent — 0.68, measured
    off this face at the sizes the app draws it. A share rather than a
    number of pixels, so it holds at every size.

    Eight widgets had their own copy of "half the height, plus one".
    There is one rule now, and a test that fails if another copy appears.

  - The theme change, again, and this time the fade was not the problem.

    The window dimmed to 0.34 while it rebuilt itself. Everything on the
    window is legible at 0.34 — so the swap from dark widgets to light
    ones happened in plain view, in the middle of the fade that existed
    to hide it. A hard cut with a smooth ramp either side of it is still
    a hard cut, and that is what "it happens in frames" was. The floor
    is 0.07. Not zero: a window that leaves the screen entirely and
    comes back is a different effect and a worse one, because for the
    length of the rebuild there is no window there.

    The second part was the frames themselves. `fade` called `step` on
    every one of them, and `step` repaints the theme button — two
    rounded plates and two icons through the imaging library, at a new
    mixed colour each time, so the icon cache missed every frame. That
    costs more than a frame has, the frames stopped being evenly spaced,
    and unevenly spaced frames read as stepping even when the average
    rate is fine. The button crosses in sixteen stops, which at the size
    it is drawn is finer than anyone can see, and the colours repeat so
    the cache hits.

    The third was the pause between the two fades. Rebuilding the page
    hands the band four or five different widths before it settles on
    one, and each was a full repaint — resize the photograph, mix the
    veil, draw the Japanese card at twice size and put it down. Around
    sixty milliseconds each and four of them discarded. `ui_home.SETTLING`
    holds the band still for the duration and `flush(force=True)` paints
    it once at the end, before the window comes back up. Measured on a
    theme change: 1.7 seconds to 1.1, and the part of it that is a dead
    pause from 1135ms to 440.

1.44.0

  - The source language never reached three of the five readers.

    Every reader's `image_to_lines` takes a `source`. Passing it is the
    whole point: a reader that has not been told what it is looking at
    guesses the alphabet, and the guess is wrong in the one place it
    matters most. Han, kana and Hangul share a great deal of their shape
    vocabulary, and an engine unsure of a character will reach across
    those three without lowering its confidence at all — which is why a
    misreading of this kind does not look like a failure from outside.
    It looks like a translation that went bad.

    Where it was being dropped:

      * `image_to_lines` and `image_to_text` called the Tesseract path
        without it. That path then chose its script from `_last_script`,
        a module global holding whatever the previous snip resolved to.
        Snip something Korean, snip something Chinese, and the second one
        is measured with the Hangul model; if the measuring pass comes
        back confident the search never runs and the whole page is read
        as Korean. The search, when it did run, tries Japanese before
        HanS and stops at the first candidate over ninety.
      * Apple Vision ran an unconstrained pass first and returned it
        whenever the result was non-Latin, reasoning that a non-Latin
        reading proves the script was detected. It proves the page is not
        Latin, which is a different and much weaker claim. Worse, the
        constrained pass it skipped was itself a *list* with ko-KR and
        ja-JP still in it, and a list is a preference rather than a
        restriction. For the four confusable tags the list is now the one
        language named and nothing else.
      * The Gemini reader deleted the argument on the first line of both
        entry points and sent a fixed prompt. The prompt now names the
        language when there is one, and the language is part of the
        reply cache's key — the same picture read as Chinese and read
        with no hint are two different questions.
      * Cloud Vision did use it, but its hint table had no Latin
        entries, so choosing German sent the empty list that choosing
        nothing sends.

    And the translator. `translate_grouped` called `translate_lines`
    without a source, so the engine auto-detected on every snip — over
    every block of the page joined into one request, which means one
    badly-read block could carry the detection for all of it.

  - Two columns, one sentence.

    A recogniser joins the end of one column's line to the start of the
    next one's whenever they share a baseline. `split_at_gutters` exists
    to undo that: it finds the blank vertical bands in the picture and
    cuts any line that crosses one, checking first that the rectangle it
    would cut through has no ink on that line's own rows — which is what
    lets a headline cross every gutter on the page and survive.

    It was doing nothing on the pages it was written for. A gutter only
    counted if what it left either side was at least a fifth of the page
    wide, and `_wide_enough_to_be_columns` deletes gutters one at a time
    until that holds — so on a grid five or six cards across, every
    gutter went and the page was treated as prose. That floor is 0.11
    now. It cannot go lower: six cells across a table row leave columns
    about a tenth of the page wide, and a table is not six columns.

    The floor could come down at all because the check that follows it
    is the strong one. A cut needs an empty rectangle in the picture, on
    the line's own rows, every time.

    The second way columns merged was in the grouping. Two lines join
    into a paragraph if their left edges are within a quarter of a box
    width, and the box width taken was the larger of the two. A quarter
    of a page-wide line is most of the way across the page, which is far
    enough to reach a caption in the next column that happens to sit a
    little lower. It is the narrower box now — the one whose left edge
    the indent is actually a fraction of.

  - Offline translation is a list rather than a picker.

    One row a language: its name, what it takes up on disk, and a Remove
    button. One row at the bottom to add another. The card knows what is
    installed, so the list of things to add holds only things that can be
    added, and the dot that used to mark an installed language in the
    dropdown is gone with the question it answered.

    The size is measured off the disk. The package objects report where
    they live, not how big they are, and what a model costs is the
    question somebody asks before deciding whether to keep it.

1.43.0

  - The two controls in the corner sat in squares.

    They are canvas widgets standing on a photograph, and a canvas
    widget has a background. The code averaged the pixels each one
    covered and painted that average behind it, which gets the colour
    right and leaves the shape wrong: the sky in that corner is a
    gradient, so one flat colour behind a rounded pill shows as a
    rectangle at the four corners. On a screenshot at size it reads as
    pixellation, which is what it was reported as.

    Each control is now handed the crop of the composed band it covers
    and draws it underneath itself, so there is nothing behind it but
    the photograph. The average is still computed and still set as the
    widget's background, for the row of pixels a rounding error can
    leave showing.

  - Two file choosers, one on top of the other.

    The whole drop area is a click target - that was the point of
    folding the Open file button into it - and nothing stopped a second
    click while the chooser was already open. Tk puts the second dialog
    up behind the first, both are modal, and the window does not respond
    until both are gone. Only one of them is on screen to dismiss.

    It is one at a time now. This also hung a test run, which is how it
    was found: a stray click during a window rebuild, a dialog nobody
    could see, and a suite that sat there for thirty-five minutes.

  - Settings was a manual with switches in it.

    A heading, three or four sentences of explanation, then the controls
    underneath in a row of their own. Everything it said was worth
    saying and almost none of it was read, because an explanation four
    lines above the control it explains is an explanation somebody has
    to hold in their head on the way down.

    It is rows now: what the setting is called and one line saying what
    it does on the left, the control on the right, a hairline between
    rows, a name at the top of each card. The long paragraphs are one
    line each, beside the thing they are about; what genuinely belongs
    to a whole card - how the keys work, what the free allowance is -
    sits along the bottom of it.

    Two things in that are worth keeping in mind for the next row
    anybody adds. The control claims its side of the row first, because
    `pack` hands out space in the order it is asked and a column of
    words asked first takes what it wants - which is how a Save & test
    button ends up outside the card. And the wrap width of the line
    under the title is measured against the row, not against the column
    the label is in: a label wrapped to its own parent's width sets that
    width, which sets the wrap, which sets the width, and the column
    walks itself down to nothing.

    Those measurements are taken when the pane is first shown rather
    than when it is built. A pane is built while it is hidden - that is
    the point of building it on demand - and a label asked how wide its
    row is while the row is one pixel wide gets one pixel for an answer.

  - Light and dark still showed a blank window halfway through.

    The window stopped being replaced two releases ago and the contents
    were rebuilt inside it instead, which was right; what was still
    wrong is that the old contents were destroyed before the new ones
    were built. So the dimmed window had nothing in it for as long as
    the rebuild took, and the fade was fading an empty grey rectangle.

    The replacement is built first, unpacked and invisible, behind the
    window that is still showing the old one. Then one step: the old one
    goes, the new one is packed, and nothing is redrawn in between.

  - Scrolling stuck.

    It added each notch to a target and eased towards it on a
    ten-millisecond timer. Eased scrolling is a lovely thing when it
    keeps up and a terrible one when it does not: the page trails the
    hand, the trailing is worse the faster you scroll, and letting go
    leaves it still moving. A trackpad already sends a stream of small
    deltas - the easing is in the hand - and a wheel notch is a jump on
    purpose. The page moves when it is told to now and stops when the
    hand stops.


1.42.0

  - The photograph is large enough for the space it fills.

    Everything else about the picture was downstream of this. The file
    that shipped was 700 by 350 and the band is anywhere from 640 to
    1550 across, so it was always an enlargement; sharpening an
    enlargement is what made it crunchy, and not sharpening it left it
    soft. There was no third option with that file.

    The one that ships now is cut from a 5000-pixel original, cropped to
    the band's own shape with the peak already a little right of centre,
    clear of the headline. The app only ever reduces it, which is the
    one resizing that costs nothing, and the veil over it was retuned:
    the old one was mixed for a dusk shot that needed opening up, and
    this is a morning.

  - Every icon in the app was drawn with stepped edges.

    Tk's canvas has no anti-aliasing on Windows or Linux. A gear at
    seventeen pixels is a handful of hard dots, a chevron is a bent
    staircase, and there are icons in the rail, in both controls in the
    corner, on every dropdown and inside the drop area. Rendered four
    times up with the imaging library and reduced, they are smooth on
    every platform, and each one is cached by name, size and colour so
    the cost is paid once.

  - Settings and Support were set in a size that ignored the window.

    The kit sizes its own type, and that size was a fixed number of
    pixels whatever the window was doing — which is how a settings page
    ends up in headline type in a small window and in fine print in a
    large one. It goes through the page's scale now, one knob for the
    whole app. The three windows that are not the main one — first run,
    the shortcut help, the setup pages — scale up with it and never
    down: their rows have widths of their own and stop fitting.

  - And their paragraphs lost words off both ends.

    Wrap widths typed in as 720 or 680, right for the window they were
    written in. A label wider than its parent is not clipped on the
    right, it is centred, so the sentence loses its beginning and its
    end. Every wrapped label in those two panes now follows the width of
    what it is in.

  - Eleven pixels is the floor.

    The page is drawn at a scale taken from the display, and below about
    three quarters the body text stops being worth reading. So that is
    where it stops: a screen too small for the window gets the window it
    can hold, not a page it cannot read. At the other end, a 4K display
    gets about half again the window a laptop gets rather than one and a
    half times over — a screen twice as wide is a reason for more desk
    around the window, not for twice the window.


1.41.0

  - One shape is not one size.

    The window asked for 960 by 540 and got two very different windows.
    On a Retina Mac those numbers are points and the display draws two
    pixels for each of them; on a 4K screen they are pixels. The same
    request is a comfortable window on one and a quarter of the width it
    should be on the other, and no amount of fixing the layout inside it
    addresses that, because the layout was right both times.

    A share of the screen is the only measure that means the same thing
    on both, so that is what it takes: the window and the scale the page
    is drawn at move together by one number, measured against the
    display the design was drawn on. The page is the same page on every
    machine and only its size changes. Held between four fifths and
    about twice that, because below the first it stops being readable
    and above the second a window stops being a window.


1.40.0

  - The photograph was not soft. It was crunchy, which is worse.

    Sharpening it twice is what did it - once when the shipped file was
    made and again when the band was composed - on a picture already
    enlarged past its own resolution, and on a file saved as JPEG, so
    the compression blocks were enlarged and sharpened along with
    everything else. Hard stair-steps along every ridge. That reads as
    pixellation and it is not what softness looks like; the previous
    release added the second sharpening pass believing it was fixing
    the first.

    It ships lossless now, and it is resized exactly once, straight to
    the size it will be drawn at, with no sharpening at either end. The
    band is composed at its own size rather than at twice it and shrunk,
    which was a second resample of the photograph for the sake of the
    corners of one rounded rectangle; that rectangle - the card with the
    Japanese in it - is drawn at four times the area in a patch of its
    own and put down at size, so it keeps its smooth corners and the
    photograph keeps its single resample.

    What is left is that a Tk canvas draws a picture one image pixel to
    one point and a 2x display doubles it. That is not fixable here,
    and it is why the type in the same band is crisp: Tk draws text at
    the display's resolution and pictures at the window's.

  - The window is one size now, and does not resize.

    Everything that had to hold at every size held properly at none of
    them. The band is measured from its own width, so widening the
    window made it taller than the photograph could fill and the
    mountain was cropped away to a sliver; narrowing it made the band
    shorter while the type, which is drawn at one scale chosen when the
    window opens, stayed the size it was. Two releases went into
    measuring and re-measuring a page at sizes nobody had ever looked
    at, for a window that has one layout and one job.

    960 by 540, on every machine, with the only concession being a
    screen too small to hold it - which gets the same shape, smaller.

  - Light and dark still blinked.

    The last two attempts were both wrong in the same way: they were
    animating the wrong thing. A theme change built a second window and
    threw the first away, and a window is new to the system as well as
    to the person looking at it - it appears, it takes focus, it has no
    history. Fading one into the other cannot hide that there are two
    of them.

    The window stays now and rebuilds its own contents inside itself:
    dim, tear down the children, build them again in the other scheme,
    bring it back up. One window, going dark and coming back. Where the
    window is, which pane was open and what was in the two boxes all
    survive it, because the window that knows those things is the one
    that is still there.

  - Scrolling stuck.

    Tk sends a wheel event to the widget under the pointer and nothing
    bubbles up, so a scrolling page has to bind every widget inside it.
    That was done once, on a timer, after Settings was built - so
    anything built later, or rebuilt, or packed in response to a
    setting, was a patch of page the wheel did nothing over. It felt
    like sticking because that is exactly what it was.

    A toplevel's own name is in the bindtags of every widget inside it,
    so one binding on the window is every widget in it, for good,
    including the ones that do not exist yet. One notch also moves half
    again as far: the old distance was chosen against a mouse wheel and
    a trackpad sends many small events rather than a few large ones.

  - Three things the design asked for.

    The headline is one line - "Snip it, Understand it." is a sentence,
    and set as two lines it was a poster. Snip & Translate sits beside
    the heading below the picture rather than on a row of its own with
    a band of empty page next to it; the row it had goes to the boxes.
    And light and dark is one button rather than two halves of a pill
    with a thirty-point target each, for a setting with two states and
    no ambiguity about which one you want next.


1.39.0

  - The photograph was soft, and on a Retina screen it was mush.

    Two things, stacked. The picture ships at 700 by 350 and the band
    it fills is around 900 points wide, so it was being enlarged before
    anything else happened to it; and a Tk canvas draws a picture one
    image pixel to one point, which a 2x display then doubles. The
    second is not something this can fix - it is why the type in the
    same band is crisp and the mountain is not, since Tk draws text at
    the display's own resolution and pictures at the window's.

    What it can fix is the first, and the sharpening. A larger copy
    ships, so the band is a reduction of the picture rather than an
    enlargement of it, and the composed band is sharpened at the size
    it will actually be drawn at - which is the only size worth
    sharpening at, because what the eye gets is that image doubled.

  - The window opened too big, still, and could be dragged into a mess.

    Opening at a share of whatever screen it finds is how a window ends
    up a different size on every machine and larger than anyone wanted
    on most of them. It opens at 780 by 520 now, which is also its
    smallest size, on every machine.

    The scale the page is drawn at has a floor to go with it. The
    design is for a window twice this wide, and scaling it down in
    proportion puts the body text at eight pixels: a page that fits and
    cannot be read. It holds at three quarters and the page scrolls if
    it has to.

  - Dragging the window narrow printed the heading through itself.

    Every line in the band sat at a fraction of the band's height - the
    eyebrow a quarter down, the claims at nine tenths - which is right
    at one size and wrong at every other. The band is as tall as the
    window is wide; the type is the size the page is drawn at. Narrow
    the window and the band loses height while the words keep theirs,
    and the sentence ends up printed through the row of claims.

    The block is now built from its own measurements, from nothing, and
    if it comes out taller than there is room for it is rebuilt smaller
    until it fits. Nothing can overlap, because nothing is placed
    anywhere it was not measured into. The card in the picture and the
    handwritten note are measured the same way.

  - The place to drop a file had gone from the box.

    "One height for both boxes" was right when both were a box and
    nothing else. The left one now has the drop area underneath it, and
    handing it the height of a three-line translation left that area
    with nothing - not smaller, gone, because pack does not map what
    there is no room for. The caret area takes what its own text needs
    and what is left in the field belongs to the drop area.

  - The tip along the bottom ran underneath its own link.

    A Tk label does not shorten itself, and that row ends with the only
    control in it. Packed left, a sentence longer than the room left
    runs under the link and out of the card. The tail of it stands down
    when there is no room; the keys never do, because the keys are the
    tip.

  - Light and dark still blinked.

    Fading the old window out and then the new one in is two fades with
    a hole between them: the window vanishes, the desktop shows
    through, a window comes back. Both are on screen together now, one
    exactly over the other, and a single loop walks one opacity up
    while the other goes down - adding to one the whole way, so there
    is never a frame with a hole in it.

    Timed rather than counted, as well. A fixed number of steps with a
    fixed sleep between them takes as long as the steps happen to take,
    which here was twice what was asked for, because every step waits
    for two windows to redraw.

  - The line in the picture was in Russian.

    The picture is of Fuji and the line in it is the sample the app can
    draw; Japanese is the first choice and Russian the second. On a Mac
    with four Japanese faces installed it was picking Russian, because
    the font finder looks for file names and on macOS those files are
    named in Japanese. The named files are now tried first, and a
    collection is asked face by face - a .ttc holds several weights and
    the first is not always the one with the kanji.


1.38.0

  - The heading was two pages side by side.

    Words in a column on the left, a photograph in a column on the
    right, and a seam down the middle. The design has one band with the
    picture running the whole width of it and the words standing on it,
    which is not a decoration: it stops the photograph being an
    illustration of the app and makes it the screen the app is used on.

    Everything in the band is drawn on one canvas, and it has to be. A
    Tk label has an opaque background, so a word set in a widget over a
    photograph is a word in a grey box. The type, the marks, the note
    and the snip loop are all canvas items. The two controls in the
    corner are the exception - they have to be clickable, so they are
    hosted on the canvas and each is given the average colour of the
    pixels it covers, which at the corner of a rounded pill is the
    difference between invisible and a smudge nobody can name.

    The wash over the picture is the part that took the longest to get
    right. A flat veil over the whole thing reads as a stock header
    with the contrast turned down. It is the page's own background
    colour, opaque where the words are, thin by the time it reaches the
    mountain, and thicker again towards the top whatever the column -
    because the photograph is a dusk shot, its sky is nearly black, and
    the sky is where the note and the two controls are. Built as a
    small mask and enlarged rather than drawn a column at a time: two
    million calls into the imaging library on every resize is not a
    gradient, it is a pause.

    The band had to lose the page's margin to reach the edge, so the
    margin moved into the panes. The words on the band take that same
    margin in pixels rather than a share of the band's width: two left
    edges a few pixels apart is the kind of wrong that is hard to name
    and impossible to unsee.

  - The window still opened too big, and the page still scrolled.

    Fitting the screen and being the right size are not the same
    question. A window eighty points short of the full width of a
    laptop display fits, and is still a window with no desk around it.
    It now takes a share of the screen - about seven tenths of the
    width, three quarters of the height - with the design's own size as
    the ceiling and, as a floor, never smaller than the page needs at
    its smallest legible scale. A third less window on a laptop.

    The band is capped as well, at two fifths of the window's height. In
    proportion to its own width it is a wide photograph; on a screen
    that is wide and short that proportion is most of the window, and
    the page under the picture is the part that does anything. Without
    the cap the page fitted at some screen sizes and scrolled at others
    for no reason a person could see, because the band's height came
    from the window's width and the room for it came from the window's
    height.

    The scroll bar was the last strip. It already hid its thumb when
    there was nothing to scroll; what it kept was its width, and twelve
    pixels of page colour down the right of a photograph that reaches
    the edge is not nothing. It gives the gutter back now. It cannot
    oscillate: hiding it makes the view wider, and a page that fitted a
    narrower view fits a wider one.

  - Light and dark jumped, and showed the front page on the way.

    The theme is baked into each widget when it is built, so changing it
    rebuilds the window. The flash was structural rather than a race: a
    Tk toplevel is on screen the moment it exists, so the replacement
    appeared showing Home and was then switched back to the pane you
    were on. It is built withdrawn now, given its place and its pane,
    and only then shown.

    Which pane the window is on stopped being a question about which
    frame is mapped and became a fact the window records. The old
    question answers "none of them" for a window built out of sight,
    which is exactly when the answer matters.

    The transition itself: the old window dims to about a third the
    moment you click - building takes long enough to notice, and a
    window that sits at full brightness after a click looks like the
    click missed - then the rest of the way out as the new one fades
    in, eased at both ends, because a linear ramp of opacity reads as a
    flicker rather than as a fade.

  - The drop area cut off its own contents.

    It is given whatever is left of the field, which on a small window
    is less than all of it, and a block centred in a box too short for
    it loses both ends. It now drops rows until what is left fits, in a
    fixed order: the mark first, then the button, never the line that
    says the whole box is a target. A lone pill in a dashed box is the
    separate Open file button this replaced.

  - A test had been asserting pixels at a page that scales.

    `test_ui_controls` wanted controls at least forty pixels tall. That
    is a question about a 1416-point window; on a laptop the answer is
    thirty, and it is the same control. It measures against the page's
    scale now. It also looked for the swap button among the language
    picker's siblings, where it has not been since it moved onto the
    seam between the two cards.


1.37.0

  - The window was bigger than the screen it opened on.

    The front page is drawn for a 1416-point window, and that is what
    it asked for — on a laptop whose entire display is about a thousand
    points across. The window manager clamped the window; the page
    inside it, still drawn at full size, ran off the bottom. The
    minimum size was the worse half: at 1080 by 700 it was larger than
    such a screen, so the window could not be made to fit even by hand.

    The window now asks for a size the screen actually has, and the
    page is drawn at a scale to match — one knob that moves type,
    spacing, icons and radii together, which is the whole reason that
    knob exists. Below three quarters it stops shrinking and the page
    scrolls instead; past that it is small rather than compact.

  - Switching between light and dark moved the window.

    The theme is baked into each widget when it is built, so changing
    it rebuilds the window - and the new window is a new window, which
    Tk puts wherever it likes. It reads as the app losing its place
    rather than as a setting being applied. Its size, its position and
    the pane you were on are carried across now.

    There was a second thing in that path, hidden behind the first. It
    still tried to re-select a tab control that had been gone for two
    releases; the exception was swallowed by the surrounding try, and
    took the rest of the rebuild with it - including the handler that
    closes the app when there is no tray icon.

  - Changing a shortcut raised instead of applying it.

    `apply_hotkeys` set a subtitle that the new front page does not
    have. The shortcut is printed in two places now - on the button
    itself and in the tip along the bottom - and both are told.

  - The swap button was half a gutter right of the seam.

    The two cards sit in one grid row and the gap between them was hung
    entirely off the left card's padding, so the space between them was
    inside the left column and the halfway line - where the button was
    placed - was the right card's edge. The gap is split between the
    two cells now. Vertically it was low by half the difference between
    the cards and the boxes inside them, because the cards carry a
    language row above and buttons below; it is placed against the
    boxes it actually sits between.

  - Support is a page rather than a placeholder.

    The tip jar, with the button it was asked for. Then a report form:
    what went wrong, and an address to reply to. It is handed to the
    mail program with everything already in it rather than posted
    anywhere - there is no server between the two of us, and adding one
    to carry a paragraph of text would mean somebody else's machine
    holding what people write about their documents. What goes with it
    is said above the button before it is sent: the version and the
    platform, and nothing else.

  - A sentence in Settings lost a word off each end.

    The note under a switch was one long line, wider than the card it
    was in - and a label wider than its parent is centred, so "Once a
    day, in the background..." arrived as "nce a day, in the
    background" with the end cut off too. The notes wrap to the room
    the row has.

  - The front page is the design, drawn to the drawing.

    A rail with the app's mark and three places to go; a hero with the
    one button the whole thing is about; under it the other ways in, as
    a pair of boxes with the language swap sitting on the seam between
    them; and a line along the bottom saying the thing worth knowing.
    Settings and Support are panes of the same page rather than a
    separate window's worth of furniture.

    Getting it to match took three goes and two of them were wrong in
    ways worth writing down, because both were units rather than taste.

    The first was mine: I built to an HTML preview I had made rather
    than to the drawing itself, and the two had drifted. The fix was to
    stop looking and start measuring — a harness that scales the drawing
    and a screenshot of the build to one width and reports a ratio per
    element. Everything below is that harness's numbers.

    The second was Tk's. A positive font size in Tk is a size in POINTS,
    which it converts using the screen's resolution: 44 becomes 59
    pixels at the usual 96 dpi. A design is drawn in pixels. So every
    piece of type on the page was a third too large, uniformly - which
    is exactly why it looked like a scale somebody had chosen rather
    than a unit somebody had confused. Negative sizes mean pixels. One
    minus sign, and the ratios went to 0.99, 1.01, 1.02, 1.03.

    Two real faults fell out of building it.

    Fontconfig does not agree with Windows and macOS about what a font
    weight is. Both of those register a face under the family in its own
    name table, so "Plus Jakarta Sans SemiBold" is a family there;
    fontconfig reads the same table, decides the weight is a style of
    one family, and reports four faces all called "Plus Jakarta Sans".
    Asked for the SemiBold by name it has never heard of it - and the
    fallback, as written, went all the way out to the system stack. The
    headline was being drawn in the machine's default face while the
    word beside it, one weight lighter, was drawn in the right one. It
    now falls back to the base family at the nearer of Tk's two weights:
    losing a weight is a smaller loss than losing the typeface.

    And the image caches outlived their interpreter. A PhotoImage
    belongs to the Tk that made it and is meaningless to any other; hand
    a second interpreter one from the first and the canvas answers that
    the image does not exist, which is not a message anybody traces back
    to a cache. Two interpreters is not exotic - changing the theme puts
    the window up again. Every cache key now says which Tk it is for.

  - Opening a file moved into the box you would otherwise type in.

    They are the same intent - "translate this" - so they are the same
    box: the caret at the top, and under it the place to drop a file,
    which steps aside the moment there is text. A separate button beside
    it asked you to classify what you were about to do before you had
    done it.

    It also fixed something else. Inside one of the two cards, that
    button made the left card taller than the right by exactly its own
    height, and the two boxes - which are read as a pair - came out
    different heights. The pair is now one grid row with one weight, so
    the two are the same height by construction rather than as a result
    of what each happens to contain. That is a thing which cannot come
    apart later.

  - The picture beside the headline is a snip, happening, on a loop.

    A still picture of a selection box has to be read before it says
    anything. A snip being drawn, in front of you, says the whole thing
    without a caption: the pointer arrives, the rectangle is pulled out
    around a line of Japanese, the handles land, and the English appears
    under it.

    It is not a film and it is not a stack of frames. The photograph and
    everything still on it are painted once; the four things that move
    are canvas items that get moved. That is the whole reason it can run
    at twenty-five frames a second without a pile of bitmaps. It stops
    when the window is hidden and when you are on another pane, because
    Squint lives in the tray and a picture animating behind a window
    nobody can see is spending somebody's battery on nothing.

  - The app's own words are set in a face that has no symbols in it.

    Not the close mark, the tick, any arrow, the chevron, the heart, the
    gear, the sun or the moon, and none of the modifier keys. The
    obvious answer - fall back to whatever face on the machine does have
    them - means an emoji, and an emoji is full colour on macOS and a
    flat outline on Windows: two platforms that were supposed to look
    like one design, diverging on the most visible marks in it.

    So every mark on the page is drawn, at the size asked for, in the
    colour given, in the same 24-unit square the design's own drawings
    use. The one exception is the keycaps. The modifier symbols are
    exactly what is printed on the keys, the system face has them and
    the interface face does not, so those three characters come from the
    system - which is what a browser does with the same stylesheet.

  - The app now carries the typefaces its own interface is set in.

    Groundwork, and nothing in this build looks any different for it.
    The front page is being redrawn to a design, and a design drawn in
    one face and shipped in whatever the machine happens to have is not
    that design: the rail comes out a different width, the headline
    wraps a line earlier, and the difference between a label and the
    word beneath it - which was two steps of weight - collapses,
    because the system face has one weight and its bold. Windows' Segoe
    and macOS' SF are both good faces and neither is this one, so the
    two builds drift apart in exactly the places a screenshot gets
    compared. Six files, half a megabyte, under a licence that permits
    carrying them; the licences travel in the same folder.

    Registered for this process only - FR_PRIVATE on Windows, the
    process scope on macOS - so nothing is installed, nothing is left
    behind when the app quits, and no other program's font list
    changes. It happens before the first window is built, because Tk
    asks the system for its font list once and keeps the answer: a face
    registered after that is one Tk will tell you it does not have, and
    the symptom is not an error, it is an interface quietly a weight
    lighter than it was drawn. Where it cannot work - Linux, a
    locked-down Mac - it does nothing and the system face is used, so
    there is no machine this can fail on.

    macOS is reached through ctypes rather than the Python-to-AppKit
    bridge already in the app: the CoreText half of that bridge is a
    separate package from the AppKit half, and a machine that has one
    does not necessarily have the other. Every return type is declared,
    because a pointer returned into ctypes' default int is a pointer
    cut in half, which on a 64-bit build crashes rather than fails.

    Three things fell out of doing it, all of them now written down in
    tests rather than in somebody's memory.

    The face is a text face and has almost no symbols in it - not the
    close mark, the tick, any arrow, the chevron, the heart, the gear,
    the sun and moon, or the modifier keys. So every mark in the new
    front page gets drawn, on a canvas, at the size the display is.
    That was the better answer regardless: a symbol typed into a label
