    is full colour on one platform and a flat outline on the other, and
    neither of those is the one in the design.

    It cannot draw Cyrillic either - the face ships the extended block
    and not the basic one - which puts Russian in the same category as
    Korean, Chinese, Japanese, Arabic, Thai and Devanagari. None of
    that matters for translations, which have never gone through this
    path and must not start: `overlay` picks a face per script and the
    translation pane asks for the system family. It matters in one
    place nobody would look. The app can be run in a language other
    than English, and the picker that offers that lists each language
    in its own script. Only English is offered today, so nothing is
    wrong; a test now fails the moment a second one is, and says which
    call to use instead. A face with no glyph does not fail and does
    not warn. It draws a box.

  - A shop's navigation menu came back as prose. Forty-one links in seven
    columns were read as twenty blocks, whole columns run together and
    handed to the engine as a sentence:

        Sports Shoes Hiking Boots Sneakers Flip-Flops Water Shoes All
        Products

    The column beside them, laid out identically, came back correctly as
    six links. That inconsistency is the tell - the decision was sitting
    on a threshold and which side any one column fell came down to a pixel
    of recogniser noise.

    Menus, tables of contents, indexes, address blocks, verse: one shape.
    Items at one margin, one line apart, in one size, close enough
    together that every test for "these are one wrapped paragraph" says
    yes. Whether two lines belong to the same paragraph was being judged
    on the space between them, and on these there is nothing to find: a
    list's line spacing *is* a page's line spacing.

    What separates them is the right margin. Wrapping is what makes a
    paragraph - every line but the last runs to the measure and stops, so
    the right edges agree. A list has no measure; each item is as long as
    it is.

    Counted as a share of the block's lines, not measured as a spread
    between its furthest two edges, and that distinction was learned the
    hard way. A spread lets one short line anywhere decide the whole
    question, and a heading glued to the front of a paragraph is exactly
    that: twenty lines of wrapped German were declared a list the moment
    the page was small enough that the heading went undetected. A share
    does not care about one outlier.

    Measured over five pages. Every wrapped block of the contract has all
    of its lines at its margin and the Chinese newsletter's worst is 0.83;
    a shop menu's best block is 0.67 and most sit at 0.33, a page of verse
    at 0.33 and 0.57. The threshold is 0.7 and nothing sits against it.

        menu       20 blocks -> 41, which is every link
        verse       9 blocks -> 22 of 24 lines
        contract   14 -> 14, unchanged
        Chinese    51 -> 51, unchanged
        Korean    115 -> 127, its own ragged blocks

    The nine-language harness still lays out all eighty-one pairs with no
    overlap, nothing off the page and nothing shrunk. Two of the nine - the
    French and Spanish pages - lose one paragraph each to four fragments,
    which is the cost and is written down rather than rounded away.

  - The corpus was answering different things depending on what ran first.

    The Tesseract reader keeps `_last_script` - whatever script won last
    time is tried first next time, which is right, because people snip the
    same language several times in a row. It also means reading the Korean
    page changes what the next read of the German page sees: 69 lines
    where there are 56, and 33 blocks where there are 14. A corpus whose
    answers depend on the order its tests ran in is not a corpus, so each
    page now says which script it is in.

    Worth writing down separately: `source` - the language the user picked
    in the app - is passed to Apple Vision and to Windows OCR and is *not*
    passed to Tesseract, which picks for itself. On the two platforms this
    ships to that is invisible. Anywhere Tesseract leads, choosing a source
    language does nothing. Not fixed here.


  - A gallery of product artwork came back with opaque boxes painted over
    the pictures, and inside the boxes, nonsense.

    VISION was read as "V - 0 N". GLASS FLOW as "G to ASS Fに ○W".
    awportrait as awpo ke "0. A recogniser cannot read a wordmark, and
    whatever it makes of one is then translated and pasted across the
    artwork it came from.

    That is the visible half. The layout loses its footing on the same
    page for the same reason. How far a block may grow is measured as a
    run of unbroken background, and a photograph has no unbroken
    background, so the measurement is noise. One caption inside a card was
    set six times its own size, ran past the bottom of the card, and
    pushed every block beneath it hundreds of pixels down an empty page,
    leaving stranded fragments behind it.

    So the question is asked before any of that: is this line on paper?
    Not what it says and not what language it is in - what is around it.
    On a page the space above and below a line is margin, which is one
    flat colour. On a picture it is picture. Coarse colour buckets,
    because a photograph of a white cup on a white cloth is still a
    photograph and its whites are not all the same white.

    Measured: over four document pages in three scripts, no line reached
    0.43; over the gallery page the median was 0.79. The threshold sits at
    0.55 and nothing needed it chosen carefully. On the gallery, 34 of 57
    lines are now left exactly as the page had them. On the contract, the
    Korean page, the Chinese page and a page of verse: not one line.

    Held back in `held_back`, which is the one place that decides both
    "do not translate this" and "do not draw over this" - deciding those
    in two places is what broke a page once before. There is a config
    switch, translate_pictures, for anyone who would rather have the
    guess.

  - Verse is still wrong, and is not in this release.

    A page of it - 24 lines - comes back as 9 paragraphs, in an arbitrary
    pattern: 8 merged, 3 alone, 7 merged. The cause is understood. Whether
    two lines are one paragraph is judged on the space between them, and a
    poem's line spacing *is* the page's line spacing, so every verse line
    looks like the next line of a wrapped sentence, and which way each one
    falls comes down to a pixel of recogniser noise.

    The fix is the right margin: wrapped prose has one and verse does not.
    Measured, it works on the page it was written for - the contract holds
    at 14 paragraphs of 14, verse improves from 9 to 22 - and it costs the
    Korean page 11 more fragments and the Chinese page 20 per cent more,
    which is the fault two releases ago was spent removing. Four slack
    settings and a rule restricted to blocks that are ragged throughout
    were tried; none separates the two.

    So it is written down here rather than shipped. A rule that fixes
    poems by fragmenting every CJK page is not a fix.


  - A page of terms and conditions came back as six paragraphs where the
    document has fourteen, and the words in them were not the words on the
    page.

    Every heading was glued to the front of the paragraph it introduced,
    and clauses 3.8.1 to 3.8.4 arrived as one twelve-hundred-character
    block. That much is a shape problem. What it did to the text is worse:
    an engine handed that many numbered clauses in one piece stops
    translating and starts editing. It lifted the numbers out to the end -
    "... remains unaffected. 3.8 3.8.1 24 3.8.2 3.8.3 3.8.4" - and wrote
    "the initial contract term is [number] months" where the contract said
    twenty-four. A reader who cannot read the source has no way to catch
    either of those.

    Two rules were missing. Neither reads a word of any language.

    A line that begins a numbered section starts a new block. The grouper
    already knew 1., a), bullets and 一、, and deliberately refused
    multi-level decimals, because "3.5 percent" opens a sentence rather
    than a list. That single exclusion cost every contract, specification
    and standard ever written - a large share of the documents anybody
    reaches for a translator to read at all. What separates the two is the
    word after the number: a section number is followed by a capital, an
    opening quote, or nothing; a decimal is followed by its unit, and
    units are lower case.

    A line set more heavily than the page's own text is a heading. It sits
    at the same margin, in the same size, one line above the paragraph it
    introduces - every test for "these are one wrapped paragraph" says
    yes, and the only thing that says no is that it is bold. No recogniser
    reports weight; the picture carries it, because bold type puts more
    ink in the same box. Measured against this page's own median rather
    than an absolute, so a scan, a screenshot and a PDF all answer the
    same, and measured in both directions, so a page set light-on-dark is
    not read as having no ink at all. It is done in _clean_lines, which is
    the one place all five readers pass through with the picture still in
    hand.

    Measured on the page: six paragraphs to fourteen, headings on their
    own lines, "24 Monate" and "4 Wochen" still inside their own sentence,
    the page filled to 91% as against a fifth of it, and no block drawn
    over another. The nine-language harness is unchanged - 18 of 18
    paragraphs exact, no overlaps, nothing off the page - and so are the
    two document pages.

  - The corpus now holds a document that numbers itself.

    This fault was not a regression. It was there from the day the marker
    was written, and nothing ever asked, because the corpus was two
    newsletters and nine synthetic pages of running prose. "Sixteen
    hundred tests pass" meant "everything I already thought of still
    works", which is not the same claim and had been standing in for it.

    tools/legalpage.py draws the page and tests/pages keeps it, so every
    build from here is asked whether a numbered document still comes back
    in its own pieces.


  - Pinching the overlay crashed the app. Not an error dialog, not a
    traceback, nothing in the log at all - the process aborted and macOS
    put up its own report saying Python quit unexpectedly.

    A magnify event is delivered while Tk's main loop is inside
    Tcl_DoOneEvent, which means Tcl's own lock is already held by that
    very thread. The handler called self.after, which is about the
    smallest call Tkinter has, and that asks for the same lock a second
    time. It is not a recursive lock. Tcl panics, and a Tcl panic is
    abort() - no Python exception, so no except clause anywhere could
    have caught it, and nothing to write down even if there had been.

    The handler now does one thing: it appends a pair of numbers to a
    deque. No widget, no lock the run loop cares about, nothing that can
    raise. A timer on the Tk side reads that deque twenty-five times a
    second, where calling Tk is legal. Everything waiting is one movement
    of one hand, so it is compounded - magnification multiplies, it does
    not add - and asked for once.

    The general rule, which is worth more than this fix: nothing reached
    through pyobjc may call into Tk from the callback. Squint already
    does this correctly for the recogniser and the hotkey, both of which
    hand work back through a queue. This was the one place that did not,
    and it went in with no way to test it.

    There is a config switch, pinch_zoom, for turning it off without a
    release.

  - Changing the translation's language from the strip is gone.

    It worked - the list was a menu opening behind a borderless
    always-on-top window, which is why nothing appeared and why the next
    click dismissed the translation - but that is beside the point. It
    was answering a question nobody asks. A reader wants this document in
    their language; they do not want it in four languages one after
    another, and the setting in the main window already covers the case
    where the answer was the wrong language. A button nobody presses is
    still a button that has to keep working, be laid out, and be
    explained.

    The strip stays. It exists for the codes the engine drops, which is
    the one mistake a reader cannot catch.


  - The overlay still overlapped itself on a full page, and the corpus
    said it did not. Six document pages, a hundred and forty-four language
    pairs, zero overlapping pairs - measured, every one of them, at one
    pixel per point. A Mac captures at two.

    That is the whole of it. At double the capture, the type is double,
    and a block allowed to grow rightwards until it reaches the edge of
    the page is now big enough to cross the gutter into the column beside
    it; at half the size it was not. The question layout was asking was
    "how far away is the edge of the page", which is the wrong question on
    any page with more than one column. _room_beside asks how far away the
    next block is - over the block's whole depth, not one line of it,
    because a paragraph three lines tall passes three lines of the column
    beside it and has to clear all of them.

    korean.png at 2x: three overlapping pairs at 700pt and one at 1000pt,
    none at either afterwards, none anywhere in a 500-1000pt sweep. The
    nine-language harness is unchanged at 1x.

    The lesson is worth more than the fix: a test bench that models one
    display and one scale is a test bench that will keep saying yes.

  - The language strip did nothing when clicked, and took the translation
    with it. One fault, not two. It was a tk.Menu posted from the overlay,
    and on macOS a menu posted from a borderless always-on-top window
    opens behind it - nothing appears, so you click again, and that second
    click lands on the scrim and dismisses everything.

    _LanguageSheet is a window of our own, created after the overlay so it
    stacks above it, with a scrim of its own so the click that closes the
    list stops there instead of reaching the overlay's. Drawn on a canvas
    like everything else in that file, so it looks the same and a click
    lands where it appears to.

  - Zoom leapt, and on a laptop with no mouse there was no way to zoom at
    all.

    Two separate things wearing one symptom. The ladder was eleven coarse
    steps and every wheel event climbed a whole one - which is right for a
    wheel that clicks and wrong for a trackpad, where the same gesture
    arrives as forty events instead of one and sent the overlay from life
    size to three times it. The ladder is twenty rungs of six per cent
    now, a wheel notch is worth two of them - the same twelve per cent to
    the hand holding a mouse - and part-rungs are kept between events, so
    ten small movements cover the ground of one large one.

    A gesture is then paced: about one doubling per second, however many
    events arrive. Paced by the event's own timestamp, not by the clock on
    the wall, which matters more than it sounds. Laying a page out again
    takes longer than the gap between two events of one gesture, so the
    tail of a burst sits in the queue - and answered by wall clock those
    leftovers go on zooming for seconds after the fingers have stopped.
    Redrawing is throttled to one in ninety milliseconds with a guaranteed
    last one, so a fast gesture is a few redraws rather than forty.

    Pinch needed nothing on Windows: a precision touchpad turns it into a
    wheel event with ctrl held, which is the same thing a mouse sends.
    macOS sends a magnify event, a kind Tk 8.6 has no name for and never
    carries to the script, so it is read from AppKit instead through the
    pyobjc already installed for the recogniser. Two monitors, not one:
    the overlay is borderless and this application is often not the
    frontmost one while it is up, so the local monitor alone would miss
    half the gestures. Every failure in there is quiet and total - the
    wheel and the keyboard still zoom, so a machine where it cannot work
    is no worse off than before it existed.

  - The language picker was sixty names in one column, taller than the
    screen, unmarked after the first entry. Finding Portuguese meant
    reading past forty other names and the bottom of the list was off the
    display.

    Pinned choices first - "Detect language", then the reader's own
    language - a rule, then a heading per letter, in columns. Which
    entries are pinned is found rather than declared: pinned_count walks
    up the list to the point where it becomes alphabetical, so a list
    sorted throughout pins nothing and needs no special case. A letter
    never begins halfway down a full column; a column may run a few rows
    long to keep one whole, which is the better trade - a reader checking
    two columns for one letter is worse off than one looking at an uneven
    column. Grouping starts above twenty choices, so font size and theme
    are untouched.

  - Three things the overlay could not do, all now on one strip along its
    bottom edge.

    A strip rather than badges in the corners: the overlay is a picture of
    somebody else's document and a badge lands on whatever happens to be
    in that corner. It is drawn below the page, so it covers nothing; it
    is pinned, so scrolling cannot take it away; and _bar_height returns
    zero when there is nothing to put on it, so it is absent rather than
    empty.

    A code the engine dropped used to be written to the log, which is the
    one place nobody looks - the reader sees a fluent sentence with a
    reference number missing from it and no reason to doubt it. overlay
    now keeps last_lost for the translation just done, separately from the
    running list, because a running list holds what earlier pages lost
    too.

    Reading the same page in a second language meant closing the overlay,
    changing a setting and snipping again - and the second rectangle is
    never the first, so the two answers could not be put side by side.
    Everything needed was already in hand: the picture and the lines read
    out of it. Only the translation is done again; the recogniser is not
    asked twice. The zoom closure is rebuilt for the new language, since
    the old one closes over the old text and would redraw it.

    And a finished translation closed and was gone. The last five are kept
    with their blocks and put back at the position they came from.

  - zoomer() is now a named function rather than a closure defined inside
    _do_snip. Two paths need one - the snip and the language change - and
    a closure inside a method cannot be reached from the second.


  - Nine languages, both directions, and eighty-one pairs of them measured
    rather than looked at.

    Two harnesses now exist because they answer different questions.
    pagecheck.py runs a real page through the recogniser, so its score
    includes whatever the recogniser did that day. langcheck.py runs pages
    whose true lines are recorded as they are drawn (langpages.py writes
    them), so a fault in the grouping is a fault in the grouping. The
    second is why the faults below could be counted at all.

    And the old harness was lying. Its stand-in translator returned one
    word per space-separated word of the original, and Chinese is written
    without spaces - so a forty-character paragraph became one word,
    nothing ever outgrew its box, and the two pages the user could not read
    scored three overlapping pairs. Counting the way a real translation
    runs put the same six pages at 234.

  - A page in columns satisfied the definition of a table, exactly.

    cells_in looks for a line with something beside it on the same row, not
    touching. The third line of the left column of a newsletter has the
    third line of the middle column beside it. On the six corpus pages that
    called between 55% and 90% of every line a cell, and a cell is a thing
    that must not move: translated alone, so the engine never saw a
    sentence and returned "It is pos sible."; measured against its own box
    and never allowed into the space beside it; and pinned where it was
    found. A translation longer than its original then had nowhere to go
    and was drawn where it stood.

    A line is prose if it fills the width it has. Wrapping is what makes a
    paragraph reach the edge of its column; a cell holds a word or a date
    and uses a fifth of the space. The line at the end of a row is judged
    against the row's own rhythm rather than the page edge - two columns of
    prose start four hundred pixels apart and are three hundred and
    seventy-five wide, three cells of a table start three hundred apart and
    are sixty.

  - The page's line spacing was read off a percentile, and the list it was
    a percentile of holds every distance on the page: inside a paragraph,
    between paragraphs, between table rows, under a heading. On a page
    whose paragraphs run to two lines - which is what a dense script gives
    you - the paragraph spacing is the minority and the percentile landed
    on the row height. Every paragraph was joined to the next and every
    column of every table was read as prose.

    line_pitch now takes the tightest spacing the page uses more than once.
    The lines inside a paragraph are the closest together anything on a
    page gets, and there is always more than one of them; a single tight
    gap is a misread box and is stepped over.

  - Rows are placed as rows. A cell used to be nailed where it was found
    and allowed neither to move nor to move anything, on the reasoning that
    the thing above a cell in page order is usually the cell to its left.
    That is true and the conclusion was wrong: a cell whose translation
    does not fit is still drawn, at the floor size, over the row beneath
    it. A hundred and seven of the hundred and nine overlapping pairs on
    the worst corpus page had a cell in them; two were prose over prose.

    The cells of a row are now placed together, keeping their offsets, and
    the row below starts under the tallest of them. Placement is one pass
    over the page rather than one per column, because the horizontal
    overlap test already keeps columns apart and the per-column pass was
    cutting every row into three.

  - A cover was allowed to touch its neighbour. background_run samples the
    background every few pixels, which finds the edge of a column and does
    not stop on the exact one, and then the cover is drawn expand pixels
    wider so no stroke of the original shows round it. Between two columns
    that laid the cover over the first sliver of the next column's letters
    - invisibly, because the two are the same colour. COVER_GAP is the
    space now left.

    Corpus: 234 overlapping pairs to 0. The nine language pages: 120 of 144
    pairs to 0.

  - The text was set too small, and by a constant nobody had measured. A
    block that would not fit could give up a quarter of its size before the
    layout looked for more room. Across both corpora one block in four was
    set at four fifths of the document's own size or less and the worst at
    69%; that is what "the translation comes out too cramped" was.

    SHRINK_LIMIT is 0.90. Nothing on either corpus is now set below 80% of
    what the page itself uses. It costs height, and only where the page was
    cramped: the worst of the six goes from 170% of the original to 225%
    and three of the six do not move. Google's own page translation does
    not shrink the words either - the page gets longer. The overlay
    scrolls; letters at 69% do not get bigger when you scroll.

  - Cells are tiered among themselves. They used to be left out of the
    sizing tiers and each measured against its own box, so that one misread
    box could not set the size for every cell near its height. That half is
    right and the result was a table in three sizes: on a real page one
    owner's name came back at seven point and the name in the row beneath
    it at fifteen. A table is the one place a reader compares one line
    directly against another. Tiering them separately keeps what the
    exclusion protected - the body text cannot drag the cells, and the
    cells cannot drag the body text.

  - Eleven languages drew empty boxes, and nothing said so.

    The font search walked its list for a file that exists and returned it
    without asking whether it contained the script. Hebrew got a Persian
    face; Thai had no list of its own and got the system interface font;
    Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, Malayalam, Gujarati, Punjabi and Bengali shared
    a list of Devanagari faces, which are a different alphabet. Each of
    those files exists on the machines in question and each was chosen
    instantly. The last-resort scan did check, and looked only at the top of
    each font directory, never inside it - which on Linux is where every
    font lives. And one character was not proof: a Japanese face has the
    character used to test for Chinese, so it passed and then drew boxes
    for the simplified-only forms.

    A font is now chosen only if it can draw the script, every character of
    the sample, one at a time. Every sample begins with a Latin letter and
    a digit because every document does - a Chinese notice carries dates, a
    version number, a part number and an email address, all in ASCII, and
    some of the best CJK faces are fallback fonts with no ASCII in them at
    all. Chosen on the strength of their Chinese, they drew every number on
    the page as an empty box.

  - Line spacing is measured off the script rather than assumed. 1.12 of
    the font size is right for Latin, Cyrillic and CJK and was used for
    everything; Devanagari ink measures 1.21 of the size and Arabic 1.29,
    so the next line was drawn through the one above. The font is asked to
    draw a line of the script and the ink is measured, once per script and
    size. A flat script measures under the floor and nothing changes.

  - Right to left is set flush right. Arabic, Hebrew, Persian and Urdu were
    drawn from the left like English, so every line of a wrapped paragraph
    started somewhere different and the edge the reader's eye returns to
    was the ragged one.

  - Codes come back the way they went in.

    An engine is a good writer and an unreliable typist. A sentence with a
    reference number in it comes back well written with the number retyped,
    or dropped. Nothing on screen says so, and the reader cannot catch it -
    they cannot check a code against a sentence they cannot read. On a real
    page put through the engine, an address quietly became a different
    domain and a telephone number lost its spaces.

    Not masked before translating: that needs the engine to leave a marker
    alone, engines differ, and a guess that fails is silent. The answer is
    repaired instead. Already there, nothing happens. Retyped, the original
    goes back. Gone, it is named in the log rather than invented, because
    choosing a sentence to put it in is the worse mistake.

    Narrowly, on purpose: addresses, tokens mixing letters and digits, long
    account and telephone numbers. Not dates, times, amounts or bare
    quantities - a date written the source language's way is meant to
    arrive in the reader's.

    The boundaries are ASCII, and that was found by a page rather than by a
    test: \w matches a Chinese character, so a code written straight onto
    the word before it - and an address with a sentence in front of it -
    were invisible or swallowed whole.

  - The size a block wanted is recorded on it. "It came out too small"
    needed a number rather than a screenshot.

  - "Same translation, same page, different formatting every time." Three
    snips of one notice, three different layouts.

    Not the engine: both run at temperature 0 and the backend in use was
    google-api-key, which is deterministic. Different text was being sent,
    because the page was being carved up differently each time.

    Two causes.

    A gutter was accepted from a single band. The gap between two cells of
    a table row is indistinguishable from the space between two columns if
    the only evidence considered is the band it appears in - so
    single-column pages were cut mid-sentence wherever a row had a wide
    gap. _running_down_the_page now requires a gutter to continue into the
    band above or below, which is what a column does and a table row does
    not. Two adjacent bands is deliberately a low bar: a real two-column
    body clears it easily.

    And the bands were laid over the picture rather than over the writing.
    A hand-drawn snip of the same page twice is two crops a few pixels
    apart; twelve bands over a crop with a wider margin sit in twelve
    different places and see different evidence. Measured:

        inset  0 -> 4 bands with gutters
        inset  7 -> 3 bands with gutters

    Seven pixels. Bands are now laid over the ink extent, so the margin
    cannot move them.

    Corpus, before and after: false cuts on p1/p2/p3 went 4/9/9 to 0/0/0;
    the genuine three-column p4 stayed at 28-29; p5 and p6 remained 0. And
    the answer is now identical across every crop tried.

    The general point: an unstable result is worse than a wrong one. A
    consistently wrong layout is a bug report; a layout that changes each
    time is "this app is unreliable", and it cannot be reasoned about,
    because no two observations are of the same thing.

  - Blocks drawn on top of each other, and I caused it in 1.26.0.

    Letting a block use the empty space to its right changed its drawn
    width and not the width two other pieces of code asked about.
    _room_below decides which blocks are underneath which by comparing
    line["box"] - the width the recogniser found - and so does the
    placement loop. A block widened to the page edge was therefore not
    seen as being above the things it now covered: it was told it had the
    rest of the page beneath it, grew down, and nothing moved aside.

    Both now ask for w + pad_r, which is settled before either runs.

    Same shape as the day's other faults: a measurement taken of one thing
    and used as though it were another. The widening was checked against
    what it could paint over horizontally and not against what it changed
    vertically.

  - Every dead shortcut this week ended at the same three lines.

        if not self._stopping:
            self._set_error_for_all(...)
        self._ready.set()

    The watcher ends; the error is recorded; nothing else happens, ever.
    An orphan holding the registration, a watchdog shooting the listener at
    startup, a child that crashed - three different faults, one shared
    consequence, and it is the consequence that made each of them a
    multi-hour hunt rather than a blip. The app had a dead watcher and no
    intention of doing anything about it, so the only cure was a restart,
    and the user was told to run commands.

    "I don't want to run a command each time to make the shortcut work. It
    needs to work every time without fail. It's the basic." That is the
    correct standard and it had not been applied anywhere near this code.

    Now: _revive() restarts the watcher whenever it ends unexpectedly, with
    delays of 0.5, 1, 2, 5, 15 then 30 seconds repeating for ever. Never
    giving up matters more than the backoff - a machine whose display or
    permissions come back gets its shortcut back on its own. The notes stop
    after the first few attempts so an hour of failure does not bury the
    log it is written in, and a watcher that reaches READY resets the count
    so a failure hours later starts from half a second again.

    And Controller._watch_hotkeys asks every thirty seconds whether the
    watcher is alive. That is not redundant with the above: the code that
    notices a dying child is the reader thread, and if the reader thread is
    what died, nothing in the process would ever find out. A supervisor
    that lives in the same thread as the thing it supervises is not one.

    The general form, worth more than the fix: this app has several
    background pieces that can fail independently of the UI - the hotkey
    watcher, the tray icon, the update check, the reader warm-up. Only the
    ones whose failure is visible got attention. A failure the app can see
    and chooses not to act on is a decision, and here it had been made by
    default, in three lines, without anybody writing down that they had
    made it.

  - The fix for orphaned listeners killed every listener at startup.

    die_with_parent watched two things: getppid() reaching 1, and stdin
    reaching EOF. The second is only meaningful if stdin is a pipe the
    parent holds open. It was not: Popen inherited the parent's stdin, and
    a process launched from the app bundle has /dev/null there. Reading
    /dev/null returns EOF at once. So the watchdog concluded the parent had
    gone, called os._exit, and the listener was dead before it registered
    a single shortcut.

    Evidence, and it is unusually clean:

        pgrep -fl hotkey_listener   ->  nothing at all
        hotkey : backend=? ready=True window-server=?

    backend=? means the child never sent its READY line. No process, no
    READY, no shortcut. Shipped as the cure for orphaned listeners and
    strictly worse than the disease: orphans accumulated over weeks and
    only sometimes won the registration; this happened every launch.

    Two changes. The parent passes stdin=subprocess.PIPE and never writes
    to it, so EOF genuinely means "this app has ended". And the child only
    starts that watcher when stat.S_ISFIFO says stdin is really a pipe, so
    where one is not provided the signal is simply not used.

    The shape to remember: a watchdog is only as good as the thing it
    watches. EOF on an inherited descriptor is not evidence about the
    parent, and it was being read as though it were. The same mistake as
    every other one this week - a real check standing in for one that was
    never made - except this one was written while fixing an instance of
    exactly that.

  - "The translation comes out too cramped in order to fit it within the
    snapped region." First report from a Korean target, on a Chinese
    notice with half the page blank beside it. Correct, complete, and too
    small to read.

    Three caps, each an arithmetic guess sitting on top of a real
    measurement:

      want = min(int(w * 0.35), edge - (x + w))

    Reach right by at most a third of your own width. On a left-aligned
    document with the entire right half empty, a short line got a sliver.
    What actually protects the page is background_run, which walks the
    picture rightwards and stops where this block's own background stops -
    it cannot paint over anything, by construction. The 0.35 was a guess
    about politeness made without looking at the page.

      item["room"] = max(h, min(room, max(int(span * 1.35), int(h * 3.2))))

    _room_below had already measured the true distance to the next block
    that actually overlaps this one horizontally - a careful piece of work
    with its own comment about eighty overlapping pairs. The line above
    then threw the measurement away and capped it at 1.35 times the
    paragraph's own depth. A block with half a page of white space beneath
    it was told it had room for a third of a line.

      if height <= avail_h and len(rows) <= 4:

    Two conditions where one was meant. avail_h is the space; the row
    count is a second, blind limit, and it bound first - a paragraph
    needing six readable lines in the room it had was shrunk until it fit
    four. Now a guard at 16 against a pathological box, and the height
    decides.

    Chinese to Korean is where it shows because the expansion is extreme:
    a character carries a word, and Korean needs several. The same squeeze
    is in every into-English translation of Chinese and nobody had said so
    because it was merely a bit small rather than unreadable.

  - Numbered lists were being translated and laid out as single paragraphs.

    group_paragraphs joins lines that are vertically adjacent, similarly
    indented and of similar height. Two items of a numbered list are all
    three. So

        1. 入口一：...
        2. 入口二：...

    became one block, one translation, one dense box - and the numbering
    survived inside the prose while the structure did not. It compounded
    the cramping everywhere it happened, because one box then had to hold
    what the page had given two.

    A line beginning with a list marker now begins a block. The rule may
    only ever split, never merge, so a false positive costs one paragraph
    laid out as two. It covers arabic numerals and letters with a dot,
    bracket or colon, the CJK enumeration comma (一、二、), circled
    numerals and bullets - and excludes decimals, because "3.5 percent"
    begins a sentence.

  - What Google Translate does with the same page, since that is the
    benchmark: every source line stays a line at its own position, runs as
    far right as it needs, keeps the document's vertical rhythm, and
    preserves the size hierarchy between headings and body. It never
    shrinks to fit. The three caps above were the whole distance between
    that and what this produced.

  - Korean was drawn with every space taken out of it.

    _pieces had one test: is this character above 0x2E80? Everything above
    it was treated as a script with no spaces between words - split into
    characters, rejoined with nothing. That is right for Chinese and
    Japanese and wrong for Korean, which is written with spaces and whose
    syllables sit in the same numeric neighbourhood.

        연구진은 세 도시에서  ->  연구진은세도시에서

    Every overlay, every release, into Korean. Nothing in the output said
    so; it simply reads as though the app cannot write Korean.

    Why it survived this long is the part worth keeping. The corpus has a
    Korean page and it is used constantly - as a *source*. Every test on
    it translates into English. Nothing in the project ever produced
    Korean output, so a defect in Korean output could not be seen by any
    amount of testing with Korean input. The first person to set Korean as
    their target language found it in a day.

    A test suite that exercises a language in one direction proves nothing
    about the other. The same is true of every other target: only English,
    German and Chinese have ever really been on the output side.

    Fixed by asking about Hangul specifically, before the 0x2E80 question,
    in both _pieces and _joiner. A Korean word is a unit; _wrap splits one
    between syllables only when it will not fit a line on its own, which
    is what Korean typesetting does.

  - Two more, found while proving the first.

    A word wider than its box was put on a line by itself and left to
    overflow - drawn off the side, with the rest of the sentence not on
    screen and nothing to report it. It had never come up because Latin
    words are short and Chinese was already broken per character. Thai has
    no spaces at all, so a Thai sentence was one piece and ran straight off
    the page. _wrap now breaks any over-wide piece.

    And breaking Thai per character split its vowels and tone marks off
    the consonants they attach to. Same for Hindi, Arabic, Vietnamese. A
    combining mark is never a break point; it stays with its letter.

    Both are the same shape as the Korean fault: a rule written while
    looking at two scripts, applied to all of them.

  - Forty orphaned listener processes, each still holding the shortcut.
    That was the whole thing. Seven releases and eight hours were spent on
    everything except this.

    pgrep -fl hotkey_listener on the reporting machine returned 41
    processes. Every Squint force-quit, crashed or killed over several
    weeks had left its listener child running, and a running listener
    keeps its RegisterEventHotKey claim. Some were from Downloads/
    SnapTranslate - from before the app was renamed - still registered for
    an old shortcut. Some were from a session with ctrl+shift+t, from
    before the default changed. All of them were alive and claiming.

    macOS delivers a hotkey to one registrant. It was delivering to an
    orphan whose parent had been dead for a fortnight, which wrote the
    press into a pipe with nothing at the other end. The running Squint
    registered successfully, got status 0, logged ready=True, and never
    received a press.

    Every observation fits and nothing else did:

      intermittent          which orphan holds it changes as they come
                            and go, so sometimes the live app won
      1.22.1 fails too      orphans are processes; they do not care which
                            version starts next
      the probe worked      it ran while whichever orphan held the
                            registration had exited
      registered, ready     entirely true, and irrelevant
      snip pressed, once    the live app won that round; the overlay was
                            then invisible for the separate reason that
                            mac_bundle had just deleted the app's bundle
                            out from under it

    The repair is two halves. hotkey_listener now watches for its parent
    going away - getppid() reaching 1, and stdin reaching EOF, because one
    signal is not enough and the parent holds the only write end of that
    pipe - and calls os._exit, which is the only exit that works from
    inside a Carbon event loop. And the manager sweeps orphans once at
    startup, before it starts its own, because at no other moment can it
    tell the difference.

    The sweep's first version was `pgrep -f hotkey_listener.py`, which
    matches anything that mentions the name: the shell that launched it,
    an editor with the file open, a test. It killed its own terminal on
    the first run. It now matches on the full command line - the script as
    an argument, followed by the JSON shortcut map - and skips its own pid
    and its parent's.

    What this cost, and why:

    The first useful command of the entire evening was `pgrep -fl
    hotkey_listener`, and it was run at the very end, after seven releases.
    Everything before it was reasoning about mechanisms. The single
    question never asked was the simplest one available: is anything else
    already holding this shortcut? A global hotkey is a shared,
    process-level resource on a machine, and nothing in eight hours of
    investigation looked at the machine's process list.

    Three wrong answers were given to the user with confidence along the
    way - permissions, the app bundle, a dead Control key - and each one
    sent him to do work that could not have helped. The measurements that
    were added were all real improvements and none of them could see this,
    because every one of them looked inside the app.

    The correct order was available throughout: what changed (a diff), and
    what else is on this machine (a process list). Both are minutes. Both
    were last.

  - The shortcut regression was mine, from 1.23.1, and four releases went
    into explaining the symptoms of it.

    The user said: it worked until yesterday. He said it three times. The
    correct response to that sentence is to diff against the last build
    that worked, and it took him asking directly - "cant you check what
    was correct in previous codes?" - before that happened. There is no
    git here, but every release is a zip on the releases page. It took two
    minutes.

    The diff is conclusive. app/snip.py in 1.22.1 is byte-for-byte
    identical to the version that "did nothing", apart from logging added
    that same evening. trigger_snip is unchanged too - including the two
    routes that 1.24.2 unified on the theory that the asymmetry was the
    bug. It was not; the asymmetry was there while it worked.

    What was added between 1.22.1 and the first report is app/mac_bundle.py
    and its call from main(). Four seconds after launch it rebuilt any
    Squint.app whose version was behind - by renaming the bundle aside,
    building a new one, renaming that into place and deleting the old. And
    the bundle it rebuilds is the bundle the running process was launched
    from. The log said so on every single launch tonight:

        bundle : rebuilt .../Squint.app (was 1.23.1, now 1.24.0)
        bundle : rebuilt .../Squint.app (was 1.24.0, now 1.24.1)
        bundle : rebuilt .../Squint.app (was 1.24.1, now 1.24.2)

    macOS does not treat that as moving files. An application whose bundle
    is renamed and deleted underneath it stops being a well-formed
    application: activateIgnoringOtherApps_ silently stops working, and a
    borderless topmost window created afterwards is never raised above
    anything. Which is exactly the observed fault - snip fires, screen
    captured, overlay built at the right size at the right place,
    invisible - and exactly the fault that four releases of activation
    theory were written to explain.

    It reports now and does not rebuild. check.command does the same work
    from outside a running app, where it is safe, and the log line says so.

    Three things worth keeping:

    A fix for "an update can never repair the launcher" was written, tested,
    shipped, and immediately caused a worse fault than the one it fixed -
    in the same file, in the same hour, on the same machine. The
    self-healing was the whole point of it and the self-healing is what
    broke.

    "It worked yesterday" is a diff, not a hypothesis. Every hour spent
    reasoning from the symptom - permissions, bundles, window servers,
    Carbon symbols, a dead Control key, activation ordering - was an hour
    not spent reading twenty lines of diff. Three of those became confident
    wrong answers given to the user.

    And the reason the diff was not the first move: there is no version
    control in this project. Every release is a zip and nothing else. That
    is the actual root cause of the evening, and it is now the top item in
    BACKLOG.md.

  - The overlay was raised before it existed.

    1.24.2 made both routes into the snip identical and gave the
    activation 120ms to land. That was right and not sufficient.

    activateIgnoringOtherApps_ raises the windows an application owns at
    the instant it is called. Called before SnipOverlay builds anything,
    the application owns no overlay - so the overlay is created afterwards
    and ordered against whatever is in front, which when the shortcut was
    pressed over another app is that other app. The result is a window at
    the right size, at the right position, topmost within an application
    that is not frontmost. Invisible, and reported by every measurement as
    correct.

    So it is asked twice now: once before, so the windows are built by a
    frontmost app, and once after, so the built windows are raised - with
    an explicit lift and -topmost on each. Different jobs, both needed.

  - Releasing the shortcuts to fix the recording box created a way to lose
    them entirely.

    Arm a field, walk away, and Squint has no shortcuts at all and no sign
    of why - the window is showing a box that says "Press keys" and
    nothing connects that to the shortcut not working. The 30-second
    timeout was written when being armed was harmless; it is not harmless
    any more.

    Fifteen seconds, and closing the window disarms whatever is listening.
    Worth noticing as a shape: a fix that makes an existing state more
    expensive has to revisit every way of reaching and leaving that state,
    not only the one it was written for.

  - "The snip shortcut does nothing, and I cannot change it either." Two
    unrelated faults reported as one, each a single missing step.

    The shortcut fired. The log said so - hotkey: snip pressed, then a
    screen grab, then a full-screen overlay at the right size and position.
    Nothing appeared on screen.

    trigger_snip had two routes. Window open: withdraw it, update_idletasks,
    after(120, _do_snip). Window hidden: _do_snip() immediately. Pressing
    the shortcut is always the second case, because the window is always
    hidden then - so the button and the shortcut were never running the
    same code, and only one of them was ever tested by hand.

    become_foreground_app() calls activateIgnoringOtherApps_. That is a
    request to the window server, not an instruction that has taken effect
    by the following statement. The 120ms in the other branch was giving it
    exactly the time it needed, by accident: the comment there says the
    delay is for withdrawing the window. So a borderless topmost overlay
    built in the same run-loop turn as the activation is built behind the
    app that is still frontmost, and stays there.

    No reason for two routes was ever written down. There is one now.

    The instrumentation lesson repeats: describe_displays reported the
    overlay's geometry and reported it correctly, for a window nobody could
    see. Geometry is what a window was asked for. winfo_viewable is what
    became of it, and that is the question people actually ask. Logged now.

  - The shortcut box could never record the shortcut that was bound.

    A registered global shortcut consumes the keystroke - the entire point
    of registering one. So while Squint held ctrl+cmd+t, pressing ctrl+cmd+t
    into the recording box went to the registration, not the box, and fired
    an invisible snip. The one combination somebody most wants to retype is
    the one that cannot be typed, and from outside that is "the box does not
    work".

    Both faults were in the same report and reinforced each other: the box
    ate the keys and the snip it triggered was invisible, so there was no
    sign anything had happened at all.

    Fixed by letting go while the box listens - pause() on the way in,
    resume() on every way out including the 30-second timeout. Failing to
    let go costs one shortcut; failing to take them back costs all of them
    until a restart, so the resume is on the timeout path too.

    RegisterHotKey on Windows consumes the keystroke exactly the same way,
    so the same bug was there and is fixed there as well. It had never been
    reported, which says something about how many people have tried to
    change a shortcut to the one they already had.

  - The instrument built to stop the guessing produced a confident wrong
    answer within an hour of shipping.

    It told a person that CMD had never been pressed. On a Mac they had
    been using all evening. Two faults:

    It polled key code 55, kVK_Command - the LEFT command key. There is a
    right one at 54, and plenty of people hold that. Same for ctrl, alt
    and shift, all of which have a second key this never asked about.

    And it sampled every 0.2s inside a loop that was doing other work. A
    keypress is about 100ms. So the sampler could miss an entire press and
    report it as absence, and did not qualify the claim in any way.

    The output then printed "CMD never arrived at this Mac at all" and,
    four lines below, "Another app may already own CTRL+CMD+T" - two
    confident and incompatible diagnoses on one screen, from the tool
    whose whole reason for existing was that the previous output could not
    distinguish causes.

    Fixed: both keys of every pair, 50Hz, and a separate hold-these-keys
    step before the shortcut test so the measurement is not competing with
    reaction time. The cause guess is suppressed when the keyboard
    evidence contradicts it, and the wording now says what it means - "did
    not reach this Mac while you were holding it", followed by: if that
    key works elsewhere, this check is wrong, not your keyboard.

    Worth stating plainly, because it is the second time in one evening.
    An instrument that answers "I did not observe X" as "X did not happen"
    is worse than no instrument, because its answer gets acted on. The
    first version of the probe said "registered, but nothing arrived" and
    then guessed at a cause, and the guess sent everyone into permissions
    for hours. This version said a key was dead. Both were built to end a
    search, and both extended it.

  - A global shortcut could register successfully and never fire, on any
    Mac, with no error produced anywhere at any point.

    Carbon delivers hotkey presses on the Carbon event queue, and
    RunApplicationEventLoop is the only thing that drains it. The code said
    exactly that, in a comment, directly above this:

        try:
            carbon.RunApplicationEventLoop.restype = None
            carbon.RunApplicationEventLoop()
        except AttributeError:
            core.CFRunLoopRun()

    CFRunLoopRun is the loop the comment names as the one that does not
    work. So on a macOS where Apple has finally removed that symbol -
    Carbon has been hollowed out for a decade - the AttributeError fires,
    the fallback runs the wrong loop, every shortcut registers with status
    0, and nothing is ever delivered. Silent by construction: no exception,
    no log line, no refusal, and a self-test that reports the shortcut
    registered because it did.

    The comment knew. It was written by someone who had already been
    caught by this exact difference and wrote down what they learned - and
    then put the thing they had just disproved in the except branch three
    lines below it. A fallback is where reasoning goes to be forgotten.

    Now: every Carbon symbol this module calls is checked at import, so a
    missing one is a sentence in the log rather than an AttributeError
    inside a watcher process whose stderr nobody reads. AVAILABLE goes
    false, hotkey_listener drops to the pynput watcher - which needs
    Accessibility but does deliver - and the reason is reported on stdout,
    which the parent reads while the child is alive. It used to go to
    stderr, which is read only after the child has ended: the explanation
    arrived at the exact moment nobody could use it.

    Whether this is what the two reported Macs had is still unconfirmed.
    It fits every observation - registered, ready, never delivered,
    reproduced on a second machine with a fresh install, identical from
    Terminal and from the bundle - and it is a fault regardless.

  - Five hours went into that, and the reason is worth more than the fix.

    The symptom was "the shortcut does nothing". That is consistent with
    the app, the bundle, permissions, another app owning the combination,
    a dead modifier key, and macOS. Every one of those was investigated,
    three of them were wrong answers given confidently to the user, and
    two releases went out that had nothing to do with it.

    What was never asked, because nothing could answer it: did the
    keystroke reach the machine at all? CGEventSourceKeyState answers that
    in one call, needs no permission, is not an event tap, and was already
    in the codebase - platform_util.key_is_down uses it for the snip
    overlay's Escape key. The probe now polls the four modifiers while it
    waits and reports which ones arrived. "cmd yes, ctrl NEVER" ends the
    search in one line.

    The general lesson: an instrument that reports one bit - it worked, it
    did not - cannot direct a search. The probe printed "Registered, but
    nothing arrived" and then guessed at a cause, and the guess sent
    everybody looking in the wrong place. It reports what it observed now,
    and only then what that might mean.

  - hotkey_backend in the settings: auto, carbon or watcher.

    An escape hatch. When one backend stops delivering, waiting for a
    release to find out which one is at fault is the wrong shape of
    dependency for something as basic as a keyboard shortcut. Pinning
    carbon deliberately does NOT fall through to the watcher, because a
    pin that silently does something else makes the next test meaningless.

  - Setup could not find Homebrew on a Mac that had it.

    A double-clicked .command runs from a shell that has never read the
    user's profile, so /opt/homebrew/bin is not on its PATH. `command -v
    brew` therefore failed on a machine with Homebrew installed, and the
    automatic Python install was skipped in favour of sending somebody to
    a download page. It now looks in the two places Homebrew actually
    lives, and adds Homebrew's own bin to the candidate list afterwards -
    the same PATH problem, one step later.

    Homebrew itself is still not installed unattended. It wants an admin
    password, it can pull down Apple's command-line tools, and it changes
    what other software on the machine resolves to. A setup script should
    not decide that while somebody watches a progress bar. It offers.

  - The queue that every global shortcut arrives on could stop being read,
    permanently, without anything saying so.

    _pump drains the cross-thread queue and reschedules itself every 60ms.
    The reschedule was the statement after the try block, so an exception
    from any handler in the loop skipped it and the pump was never
    scheduled again. Everything queued from that moment sat there for the
    rest of the run.

    The symptom is what makes this worth the entry. The window keeps
    working - its buttons call trigger_snip and trigger_clipboard directly
    and never touch the queue - so the app looks entirely healthy and only
    the global shortcuts are dead. That is indistinguishable from macOS
    withholding the keystrokes, and it sends the search into permissions,
    Accessibility, the shortcut backend and combination conflicts. All of
    which is where two rounds of this went, on the strength of a symptom
    that pointed confidently in the wrong direction.

    Now `finally`, and the exception is logged. Restarting the pump is not
    optimism about whatever failed: a queue nobody drains is a worse state
    than a repeated complaint in the log, and there was no complaint in the
    log to weigh against it.

    Whether this is what the reported Mac had is not yet known - it has the
    right shape, and the instrumentation shipped alongside it will say. It
    is a fault either way. This is the fourth instance of one shape in this
    codebase, and the first where the shape is "a failure that disables the
    only channel that could have reported it".

  - Shortcuts reported dead on a Mac, and the log could not say why.

    Two lines were missing, and their absence is the whole entry.

    First: nothing recorded that a shortcut had been pressed. So "the
    shortcut does nothing" could not be separated from "the shortcut fires
    and the thing it does fails", which are the same experience and share
    no cause. Worse, the buttons in the window do the same work and leave
    the same trace - so a log from a session with dead shortcuts showed a
    screen grab and four clipboard reads, and none of it proved anything
    either way. `_pressed` now logs the press before queueing it.

    Second: nothing recorded which shortcut backend was running. There are
    two on macOS. One registers with the system, needs no permission, and
    can be refused because another app already owns the combination. The
    other watches the keyboard and is silent without Accessibility. They
    fail for opposite reasons and want opposite fixes, and the log named
    neither - the only hotkey line there was inside `if problems:`, so in
    the case where registration succeeds and nothing fires, which is this
    case, it said nothing at all.

    That is the shape worth remembering: the log had a line for the
    failure it anticipated and none for the failure it had.

  - A test failure carried as a "known baseline" for a whole release was
    not a failure.

    test_stale_selection asserted CLIPBOARD_TIMEOUT >= 1.0 and failed with
    0.5. The real value in app/selection.py is 1.2 and always was.
    test_selection.py and test_selection_counter.py shorten it to 0.15 to
    keep themselves quick and then restore it - to a literal 0.5, typed at
    some point when that was the value, rather than to what they found. So
    they left the module changed for everything that ran after them, and
    the number they left was below what the app needs.

    Both files now save the value and put back what they found.

    Two things worth keeping. A test that changes the code under other
    tests is worse than one that merely lies, because the failure appears
    somewhere else entirely - and it was reported against a file that had
    done nothing wrong. And "known baseline failure" is a phrase that
    should be spent carefully: it was written down as one, repeated across
    a dozen release checks, and it was a real defect the whole time. The
    baseline was 2 failures. One of them was fiction.

  - The Mac app would not start from its icon, and had not been able to
    for some time. Four faults, and only the first is a bug.

    1. The launcher's check verified the one thing that could not fail.

    Squint.app is a thin wrapper: a shell script that runs the source in
    the folder. It carries a copy of the interpreter under Contents/MacOS
    so that macOS attributes the Dock icon and the menu-bar name to the
    app rather than to Python, and it verifies that copy before using it,
    falling back to .venv/bin/python if the copy is no good.

    The verification was `import sys, tkinter`. Both come from the
    framework Python that every copy shares. The check therefore returned
    true for a copy that could not see one single installed package, which
    is the only failure mode it existed to catch. The app was handed that
    interpreter, died on app/ocr.py line 6 - from PIL import Image - and
    died silently, because a bundle has no console. From outside: the icon
    does nothing. From Terminal: works perfectly. The two facts together
    look like a Gatekeeper or permissions problem, which is where the
    first round of looking went, twice, across two sessions.

    It now asks for PIL: installed, not part of any Python, imported on
    the sixth line of the first module. If that passes and the app still
    will not start, the fault is somewhere worth looking.

    The test for this asserted `import sys, tkinter` was in the launcher.
    It did not merely fail to catch the bug - it pinned it in place, and
    would have failed anybody who fixed it. Second time this has happened
    in three releases; the other was a test that read the OCR dispatcher's
    return lines too literally. A test written from the code rather than
    from what the code is for tests that nothing changed.

    2. No update could ever have delivered the fix.

    This is the part worth keeping. An update replaces app/ and restarts.
    It does not touch Squint.app, which is written by setup.command and
    check.command and by nothing else - so the launcher on a machine is
    whatever was written the day the app was first set up, and every
    release since has been fixing code that the launcher then failed to
    load. A defect in the wrapper is permanent by construction. Twenty-one
    releases had shipped over it.

    So app/mac_bundle.py now compares the bundle's own version against the
    running one at startup and rebuilds it when it has fallen behind. Four
    seconds in, on a daemon thread, because rebuilding runs codesign and
    nothing waits on the answer. It only touches bundles whose ROOT is
    this folder: somebody with two installs has two bundles, and
    rebuilding the other one would repoint it here and quietly turn their
    second install into a second shortcut to the first.

    Built beside the old one and swapped in by rename, never in place.
    make_app.build() clears its destination first, and doing that to the
    app somebody is running - or failing partway - turns a launcher fix
    into a missing application. If the swap fails the old one goes back.

    Note what this does not do for the machine that reported it: the code
    performing an update is the old code, so 1.23.1 arrives through a
    launcher that still has the old check. That machine needs
    check.command once. From there it is self-healing.

    3. diagnose.txt carried three live API keys.

    The file exists to be sent to somebody - that is why it is written to
    disk rather than printed - and it dumped config.json whole. So "run
    this and send me the result" was, in plain terms, an instruction to
    hand over working credentials: to whoever is helping, to their mail
    provider, to whatever chat it passed through. It happened. The keys
    are now redacted to "set, hidden", which keeps the fact that matters -
    set-but-wrong and never-entered are different faults - and drops the
    value, which never mattered. Both platforms; diagnose.bat had it too.

    4. The log said "Squint 1.2.0 starting" for twenty-one releases.

    app/__init__.py held a literal __version__ that nothing kept up to
    date. Harmless right up until the moment somebody is reading a log to
    work out why a Mac will not start, at which point every line in it is
    being trusted and one of them is a lie. It reads VERSION.txt now, like
    everything else.

    tools/make_app.py had already learned this exact lesson and written it
    down in a comment - the bundle's plist "sat at 1.2.0 through eight
    releases" - and the lesson was recorded in one file while the same
    literal stood in another. Writing down what went wrong is not the same
    as fixing everywhere it went wrong.

  - A fifth of the lines on a three-column page were read straight across
    the gutter, and the reason the fix took three attempts is worth more
    than the fix.

    A recogniser handed a page in columns joins the end of one column's
    line to the start of the next one's: same baseline, no wall. One box,
    one string, two halves of two unrelated sentences. Everything
    downstream inherits it - the box is wrong so the placement is wrong,
    the text is nonsense so the translation is nonsense however good the
    engine, and the paragraph it belongs to can never be found because it
    is in two paragraphs at once.

    It cannot be found in the boxes the recogniser returned, which is
    what overlay.columns() tries. A line lying across the gutter covers
    it, so the evidence that there is a gutter at all is destroyed by the
    very fault you would use it to find. On that page columns() reports
    one column.

    So the picture is asked instead. Ink either is or is not there,
    whatever the recogniser made of it. Three attempts:

      1. One band, 25%-85% of the page. Found one of the two gutters.
         The two-column reference block at the foot sits inside the
         first one and closes it.
      2. Majority voting across eight bands. Found neither. A column
         structure occupies part of a page's height, not all of it, so
         requiring three-quarters of the page to agree is requiring the
         layout to be something no page is.
      3. Per-band gutters, used at each line's own height. Both, exactly.

    That is the shape of the whole thing: a page is not one layout. It is
    a full-width masthead, then three columns, then a two-column
    reference list, which is how every newspaper has ever been set.
    Twelve bands, each read on its own.

    Two more rules earn their place. What is left between two gutters has
    to be at least 18% of the page wide or it is not a column - without
    that, the five gaps between six table cells are five gutters, and the
    blank strip after a hanging "1.1" is one too, and a contract gets cut
    away from its own numbering. Dropping the narrowest candidate first
    and looking again matters, because removing one gap widens its
    neighbours, so a table row collapses back to no gutters rather than
    to five bad ones.

    The headline rule was the interesting mistake. First version: a line
    wider than 78% of the page is a headline, leave it alone. Reasonable,
    and it silently protected almost every merged line on the page it was
    written for - on a three-column page a line merged across all three
    IS nearly page-wide. The measurement said 12 lines split; the honest
    number was 29. A rule that fails on exactly the case it was written
    for, and looks like it works everywhere else.

    Replaced with a question that has an answer: is there ink in that
    rectangle of the page, on this line's own rows? A headline has its
    own letters in the gutter. A merged line has nothing there. No
    heuristic, no threshold on width, and it is right for the same reason
    a person is right about it.

    Averaged rather than darkest-pixel, deliberately. Asking for a
    rectangle without one dark pixel in it is asking for something real
    screenshots do not have, and the whole cut would quietly never
    happen - which is a worse failure than doing nothing, because it
    looks like doing something.

    Dark mode had to be handled or the feature was off for half the
    screenshots this app gets: inverting a dark page turns the empty page
    into solid ink and every gutter disappears. The background is
    whatever most of the picture is, so the median decides which way
    round to read it.

    Measured on all six corpus pages. Korean three-column: 29 lines cut,
    every one of them genuine. Chinese two-column: 9. Japanese forms: 9.
    German tables: 4, all in the three-column legend band under the
    table. The two single-column pages: zero, which is the number that
    matters most - a feature that cuts a page that did not need cutting
    is worse than no feature. Same results on a dark inversion and on
    JPEG at quality 40. 30ms on a 2560x1440 screenshot.

    Sits in _clean_lines, before the watermark filter, because a line
    read across two columns is two lines and either half may be the
    watermark. That also means all five readers get it, which is the
    fourth-instance lesson from 1.22.1 applied rather than relearned.

    What this does not fix: overlay.columns() still reports one column on
    all six pages. Its problem is the headline, not the merged lines, and
    it is a separate change. The reference block at the foot of the
    Korean page has no measurable gutter at all, so its lines survive.

  - Settings did not say where the user's own glossary is.

    1.22.0 shipped a file whose header says "edit the other one" and did
    not give the path to the other one. That is not a small omission - it
    makes the entire two-file arrangement pointless, because a file
    nobody can find is a file nobody edits, and people adding their own
    terms was the whole reason for it.

    Settings now has the path, an Open button, and a way to the folder.
    Open creates the file first: opening a path that does not exist does
    nothing at all on Windows and shows an error on macOS, and "nothing
    happened" is the worst answer a button marked Open can give.

  - A left click on the tray icon started a snip.

    pystray marks one menu item as the default, and that is what a left
    click runs. It was "Snip & translate", which puts the app's most
    disruptive action on the gesture people make by accident: a stray
    click dimmed the whole screen and waited for a selection over
    whatever they were doing, with Escape the only way out.

    It was also the one action already on a global shortcut, so the click
    was never how anybody reached it deliberately. The gesture cost more
    than it was worth. Opening the window is the safe default and the
    snip button is right there in it.

  - The marques added in 1.22.0 never fired on a real page, and the
    reason had nothing to do with the marques.

    Chinese, Japanese and Korean are not written with spaces between
    words, but a recogniser returns one box per glyph and has to join them
    itself. Every reader does it slightly differently, and only Tesseract's
    output went through strip_cjk_spaces. The other four - Apple Vision,
    Windows OCR, Cloud Vision, Gemini - could hand on "享 界 G9".

    Nothing about that is visible. It translates the same, it draws the
    same, it looks right on screen. What it silently breaks is every exact
    match downstream: the glossary asks whether "享界" is in the text, and
    in "享 界 G9" it is not. So forty marques were written down, tested,
    shipped, and never fired for anybody using the two readers this app
    leads with. The people on Tesseract were the only ones it ever worked
    for, and they are the minority.

    From outside it looked exactly like the glossary not being loaded,
    which is where two rounds of looking went.

    Settled in _clean_lines, which is the one place all five readers pass
    through on the way out, so no reader has to remember. And the glossary
    now retries a term that did not match allowing whitespace between its
    characters - only for terms written entirely in unspaced scripts,
    because doing it to a Latin term would let "BMW" match "B M W".

    Fourth instance of one shape: the watermark filter missing from two of
    four readers, wrapped lines joined on one path and not the other, two
    readers returning past the cleaning, and now this. tests/
    test_every_reader_agrees.py walks all five by name, so a reader added
    later fails the test until somebody has thought about it.

    Writing that test found something else, and the something else was
    that the test was wrong: it read the dispatcher's return lines
    literally and failed on the one that delegates to _tesseract_lines,
    which cleans at both of its own returns. Working code, failing test,
    and the obvious move is to change the code. Worth recording as the
    hazard it is.

  - A Chinese date came back as "2026 Year 8 Month 28 Day", and it was
    this file's fault rather than the engine's.

    年, 月 and 日 are ordinary words meaning year, month and day.
    glossary.protect masked the numbers first, one at a time, so what
    reached the engine was

        ZQXAQ年ZQXBQ月ZQXCQ日

    - three placeholders and three Chinese words, with nothing left in it
    to suggest a date. It translated precisely what it was given, and it
    was right to.

    The fix is ordering: recognise the date before the numbers inside it.
    That makes it work for every engine, needs no network, and is the
    difference between a rule the translator has to be told and a fault
    that cannot happen. Full dates, month-and-day, month-only, month
    ranges (a combined issue of anything is written that way), 号 as the
    spoken form of 日, and Korean 년월일.

    English gets the month by name, because that is what the page it is
    going onto reads like. Everything else gets 2026-08-28: unambiguous in
    every language, and the one form that cannot come back with the day
    and the month the other way round. A month outside 1-12 is left
    exactly as it was - rewriting "13月" as a month would be inventing.

  - Chinese marques were translated as the words they are made of.

    享界 is Stelato. It is written with two characters meaning "enjoy" and
    "boundary", so it came back as "Enjoy G9"; 尊界 (Maextro) came back as
    "Premium V800". There is nothing about either that looks like a name -
    not a code, not a number, not an acronym - so no pattern can catch
    them and no amount of masking helps. They have to be known.

    About forty are now in the shipped glossary. Untagged, deliberately:
    the language tag exists for terms that mean different things in
    different languages, and a name written in Chinese characters can only
    be Chinese. Tagged zh: they would be skipped whenever the source was
    left on Detect, which is nearly always, and the rule would do nothing
    for the people who need it.

    Two are deliberately absent. 钛 is the model name in 钛7 and the
    element titanium everywhere else; 汉 is a BYD model and also the word
    for Chinese. A global rule for either would turn 钛合金 into "Ti
    alloy". They want a person's judgement, and there is now a file for
    that.

    The prompt was also telling the engine the opposite of what it needed.
    "Copy brand names exactly as they appear" is right for a model code
    and wrong for 享界 - copying that exactly leaves Chinese characters in
    an English sentence. It now asks for the established name in the
    target language, says never to translate the words a name is made of,
    and asks for dates to be written as dates.

  - The glossary was being deleted by every update.

    Found while adding the marques above, and the worse of the two.
    glossary.txt lives in the app folder and updater.REPLACE_FILES names
    it, so every rule anybody ever added was thrown away by the next
    update - silently, with the terms they had fixed starting to come back
    wrong again and nothing to connect the two events. Inviting people to
    edit a file that gets deleted is worse than not having the file.

    There is now a second glossary beside the settings and the log, where
    an update is not allowed. Both are read, the user's first, and a
    stable sort keeps it first - so where both name the same term theirs
    wins, because protect replaces on the first match and the term is gone
    from the text by the time the shipped rule is tried. The shipped file
    now says at the top not to edit it and where to go instead.

  - Reported after 1.20.3: the app stopping repeatedly. Two changes, and
    only one of them is a fix.

    THE SUSPECT, REMOVED. 1.20.3 asked macOS, through
    ::tk::unsupported::MacWindowStyle, not to activate the application
    when the "Translating..." pill appeared. It was written down at the
    time as the unverifiable half of that release - it could not be tested
    anywhere but on a Mac, and it applied an undocumented window style to
    a window that is already overrideredirect, which is a combination with
    no defined behaviour. It also ran on every single translation, which
    is the right shape for a fault somebody describes as constant. So it
    is out, and the toast is built exactly as it was in 1.20.2.

    Removing it does not prove it was the cause. It removes the least
    justified thing in the newest code, which is where to start when
    something began failing and nobody can see why.

    The half of 1.20.3 that is certain stays: the card still does not call
    focus_force on macOS, so translating a selection still does not drag
    the whole application in front of what is being read.

    THE ACTUAL FIX, WHICH IS NOT A FIX. Tk's answer to an exception inside
    a callback is to print it to a console a packaged app does not have.
    Every button, every timer, every redraw in this app is a Tk callback,
    so any failure in one of them arrived as a button that did nothing and
    left nothing behind to say why. root.report_callback_exception now
    writes all of them to the log.

    That is why "it crashes a lot" could not be answered with anything
