    better than a guess. It should not happen twice.

    Not a dialog, deliberately: these arrive from timers and redraws as
    much as from buttons, so a box could appear over and over with nothing
    the user can do about it, and the one that matters is usually the
    first - which a stack of identical boxes buries.

    Also: the Escape poll added in 1.20.3 imported Quartz on every tick,
    eight times a second for as long as a card was open. The lookup is now
    done once and held, failure included.

  - A question mark beside the Gemini and Google key fields, opening a
    step-by-step picture guide for that key.

    The written guide to getting a key has been in the app since 1.14,
    behind a button at the foot of the same screen, and hardly anybody
    found it. That is not a failure of the writing, and rewriting it again
    would not have helped. What people get stuck on is not what an API key
    is - it is which of forty buttons in a console Google redesigns every
    few months, and no sentence survives that. A picture of the button
    does.

    Bundled rather than linked, on the argument ui_help_keys already makes
    about the written one: whoever is reading this is reading it because
    they are stuck, a link that opens a browser on a locked-down office
    machine is one more thing that can fail, and a page on the internet
    drifts out of step with the build. These ship with the version they
    describe.

    Three decisions in it worth keeping.

    OFFER ONLY WHAT CAN BE SHOWN. The question mark is drawn only when the
    picture is on disk and Pillow can open it. DeepL has no guide and gets
    no question mark. An affordance that opens an apology is worse than no
    affordance: whoever pressed it was already stuck, and being told the
    help is missing is the one answer that helps with nothing.

    SIZE. 735 KB for both, which roughly doubles a 588 KB release - a real
    cost, and the reason they are WebP at half the rendered resolution
    rather than PNG at full. Palette-quantised PNG was tried first because
    it is lossless in the ways that matter for text; it is half the size
    again and it turned the Google Cloud logo brown, so it was dropped.
    The WebP was compared against the original side by side before it was
    committed.

    FIT, AND THEN ZOOM. The first version of this window fitted the
    picture to the screen and left it there, which is the one thing a
    guide made of screenshots must not do: fitted to a laptop display, a
    step is a thumbnail - you can see there is a button and not read what
    it says.

    So it is a floating window that zooms and pans like a map. The wheel
    scrolls, ctrl (command on a Mac) and the wheel zooms, dragging pans,
    and there are Fit / - / + buttons with the current percentage beside
    the title. Nine steps from 35% to 300%, not a continuous zoom: a guide
    is read at a handful of useful sizes - the whole thing, a row of
    steps, one step, the writing inside a screenshot - and stepping lands
    on them where a slider hunts around them. It opens at the step nearest
    to filling the window rather than the largest that fits, because one
    notch too big and scrolling sideways beats two notches too small.

    Scaled by Pillow rather than by Tk, whose zoom does whole-number
    ratios only. Redraws are coalesced behind a 90ms timer: the wheel
    sends notches faster than a 2200-pixel picture can be resized, and
    resizing on every one of them is how a zoom comes to feel like a hang.

    And it is no longer `transient` to the settings window. Tk withdraws a
    transient window whenever its master is withdrawn, so the guide
    vanished the moment somebody closed the settings window to go and
    follow it - which is precisely when they need it.

    Two limits, neither solved. The pictures are English only where the
    written guide is translated - which is why the written one stays. And
    they are dated by their screenshots in a way words are not: the next
    time Google moves a button the pictures are wrong, and nothing in the
    app can tell.

  - Translating a selection brought the whole application forward, and
    the card was drawn on top of Squint's own window.

    Reported from a Mac: press the shortcut over a sentence in a browser
    and Squint came to the front - including a settings window left open
    two hours earlier - and only then did the translation appear. What was
    asked for was one sentence; what arrived was somebody else's window
    over the work.

    Two causes, and they are the same mistake in different clothes.

    focus_force() is how a window is given the keyboard, and on Windows
    that is all it does. On macOS it does not focus a window so much as
    activate the PROCESS that owns it, and activating a process raises
    every window it has. The card asked for the keyboard 40ms after it
    appeared.

    And simply showing a window does the same thing: mapping a toplevel
    activates the process. That one is already written down in
    main.trigger_clipboard, where it broke the copy itself - the toast
    went up, macOS made Squint frontmost, and the synthetic Cmd+C went to
    Squint, which had nothing selected. This is that fault arriving a
    second time, one window later, and the order in the report is what
    gave it away: the app came forward BEFORE the translation appeared, so
    something ahead of the card was doing it too.

    The card does not need the keyboard and never did. It has a Copy
    button and a Close button, and it is dismissed by a click anywhere
    else - which is watched through the OS rather than through focus,
    because Tk never hears a click on a browser. The single thing lost by
    not taking focus is Escape, and Escape is now read from the window
    server instead through platform_util.key_is_down. That is the route
    the snip overlay has always taken, for the same reason: a borderless
    window on macOS never becomes the key window, so a key press is never
    delivered to it however hard Tk is asked. Not bind_all, which is
    application-wide and would take Escape away from the overlay - whose
    Escape is the one that rescues somebody from a dimmed screen.

    The toast now asks macOS not to activate the app when it appears, via
    Tk's noActivates window attribute (platform_util.make_non_activating).
    It is the first window the shortcut puts on screen and so the first
    chance to raise everything else. Used only on windows nobody types
    into: noActivates on a window that needs the keyboard is a window that
    cannot be used, which is why the card does not get it.

    Half of this is certain and half of it is the best available guess.
    focus_force activating the app is not in doubt. Whether the toast was
    also doing it cannot be checked from a Linux container, so that half
    is written to degrade to exactly the previous behaviour on any Tk
    without the command - which is what happens on Linux, verified.

    Left alone on purpose: OverlayWindow still calls focus_force. A snip
    has already activated the app - the picker was full-screen and held
    focus - so there is nothing left for it to steal, and taking focus
    away from the overlay would cost its Escape. If the overlay is ever
    shown without a picker in front of it, this becomes the same bug.

  - The in-app key guide had the same three faults as the printed one.

    ui_help_keys.py told everyone to "accept the free trial if it offers
    one", which only a brand-new Google account is ever offered - anyone
    who had touched Google Cloud before was stuck on the first step. It
    never said that Google wants a payment method on any project using
    Cloud Translation or Cloud Vision, including inside the free
    allowances, which is one of the three ways to get an identical 403. It
    never mentioned restricting a key at all, nor the trap that makes
    restricting it worth explaining: a service that has not been switched
    on yet does not appear in the list to tick, so a key restricted too
    early fails exactly like a key that was never made.

    The Gemini section now says what the settings screen already said and
    the guide did not: the allowance is per day rather than per month, it
    resets at midnight in California, it belongs to the Google account
    rather than to Squint, and leaving Reading text on Automatic makes it
    last about twice as long.

    Written in the same words as the picture guides, so the two cannot
    drift apart.

  - The connection to the translation server is held open between
    requests instead of being thrown away after each one.

    Every translation is one small request to a server that is usually on
    another continent, and every one of them opened a brand-new
    connection. Before a single character of the sentence went out:

        find where the host is             one round trip, if not cached
        open the connection (TCP)          one round trip
        agree on the encryption (TLS)      one or two more

    Three or four trips across the world, on every press of the shortcut,
    for ever. Reading the screen through Cloud Vision paid it a second
    time, so a snip on that path paid it twice.

    Nothing decided to hang up. urllib.request.urlopen is the one-liner
    everybody reaches for and it does exactly one thing - connect, fetch,
    close. It has no other mode. It was reached for, it worked, and it
    stayed. http.client, also in the standard library, does support
    keep-alive; it just has to be driven by hand, which app/http_pool.py
    now does in about a hundred lines and no new dependency.

    How much this is worth is entirely a function of how far away the
    server is, and it cannot be measured from a development machine - the
    same handshake measured from a datacentre next to a cache takes five
    milliseconds and from a laptop to a distant endpoint takes several
    hundred. So the app writes one line to the log when it closes:

        network    : 14 requests, 2 dialled (avg 380ms), 12 held (avg 96ms)

    Two averages side by side, which is the only statement that means
    anything. One line at the end rather than one per request, because it
    is the comparison that says something and nobody adds up a hundred
    lines.

    Two things it has to get right, and both are ways to be worse than
    what it replaced rather than better.

    A POOLED CONNECTION CAN BE DEAD. Servers close connections that have
    sat idle and do not announce it - the socket looks fine until a
    request is written to it and nothing comes back. So a failure on a
    connection that came out of the pool is retried once on a fresh one,
    and a failure on a fresh connection is a real failure and is raised.
    The retry only happens when nothing was read back, which is what a
    dead idle socket looks like; a request that got a reply is never sent
    twice. Connections older than 45 seconds are dropped unused rather
    than risked, since 60 is the common idle timeout.

    SOME PEOPLE ARE BEHIND A PROXY. urlopen reads the system proxy
    settings and this does not, so where a proxy applies to the host this
    steps aside and hands the request back to urlopen unchanged. A tenth
    of a second is not worth being the app that stopped working at the
    office. It also steps aside for any scheme it cannot pool.

    It raises exactly what urlopen raises - urllib.error.HTTPError for a
    status the caller should see, URLError for anything below that - and
    that is a requirement rather than a nicety: translator reads
    exc.reason to spot a certificate failure and retry against a different
    trust store, and ocr_google reads exc.code. Either would break
    silently otherwise. Writing the tests found one real fault before
    release: a failure while opening the socket escaped as a bare OSError,
    which every caller in the app would have failed to catch.

  - The reading pane is removed. A snip always overlays.

    It shipped in 1.20.0 and came out the same day, on sight of the first
    real capture of a Japanese form. Worth writing down at length, because
    every measurement that justified it was true and the feature was still
    wrong.

    The idea was that past a measured density - blocks written over other
    blocks, or body text under about ten points - the app should stop
    redrawing the page and set the translation as one column to read. It
    measured well. Over the six-page corpus it removed every one of the 43
    overlapping pairs, lost no words, and came out in a sensible order on
    five of the six.

    What a Japanese form looks like coming out of it is a list: "Service
    Application Form / Phonetic spelling / Your name / date of birth /
    post code". Every word correct, every relationship gone. It is not a
    form any more, and it is not far off what the text-translation
    shortcut already returns - which is the point. That shortcut exists.
    The reason to snip something instead is to see it translated in the
    shape it was in, with the labels where the labels were. The pane threw
    that away and handed back something the app could already do, so the
    capture was worth less than not having run it.

    Which means the numbers were measuring the wrong thing. "Nothing
    overlaps" is not the goal; "the page still reads as the page" is. A
    layout that abandons the page scores perfectly on every overlap metric
    while failing the only test that matters, and a metric that cannot be
    failed by giving up is not a metric. Four releases of layout work were
    scored on overlap counts, and this is where that ran out.

    So density is not a reason to show something else, and neither is
    small text - the overlay window zooms, and re-lays the page out at the
    new size rather than magnifying what was drawn, which is the answer to
    text being small. The 43 overlapping pairs are a fault in placement
    and want fixing in placement; routing around them removed the pressure
    to do that.

    One thing survives, in tools/pagecheck.py: overlaps are counted
    between the drawn text of two blocks rather than between their covers.
    Covers abut by design, and two touching covers are not something
    anybody can see, so counting those overstated the fault.

  - A dense page is read rather than looked at. (Removed in 1.20.1 -
    see above. Kept here because the reasoning below is still the correct
    description of what the layout does badly.)

    Drawing the translation back onto the page is the whole product and it
    is right nearly every time: one paragraph, a dialog, a menu, a form
    field. It has exactly one failure mode and the failure is total.

    Translated text is a different length than the text it replaces, so
    blocks grow; grown blocks collide; colliding blocks get pushed down;
    pushed-down blocks land on the next one. Every rule in the layout
    about room, tiers, spans and push-down exists to hold that back, and
    on a page laid out in three narrow columns they run out. On the worst
    page of the test corpus 42 per cent of the blocks end up written over
    another one. The same paragraphs snipped on their own come back
    perfect, which is the whole diagnosis: nothing is wrong with the
    reading or the translation, only with putting it back.

    Four releases of this were spent adding rules to hold the collisions
    off, and each one bought a page type and lost another. So past a
    measured density the app stops trying to win the page and lays the
    translation out as one column to read. Nothing can collide, because
    there is nowhere for two blocks to be in the same place. What is given
    up is the illusion that the page itself was translated - on one
    paragraph that illusion is the product, and on a whole page of columns
    it is what makes the result unreadable.

    Four decisions in it, and each was measured rather than guessed:

    WHICH LAYOUT. Asked of the finished layout, not of the capture, so
    nothing has to recognise what a document is. Two answers send a
    capture to the pane: blocks with their writing on top of another
    block's, and body text that came out at ten points or less - no
    arrangement makes ten-point text readable. Under eight blocks it never
    fires; two lines of a tooltip set small are still two lines of a
    tooltip and belong where they were.

    READING ORDER. An XY-cut: cut the page at the widest blank band that
    runs clean across it, then ask the same question of each piece. The
    existing columns() cannot answer this. It looks for a gutter running
    the whole height of the page, and a page with a heading across the top
    has none, so it reports one column - true of the page and useless for
    reading it. The direction of each cut is whichever gap is the more
    emphatic. Cutting into rows first looks right and is wrong: two
    columns whose lines sit level with each other have a gap between every
    pair of lines that runs across both, so the page splits into rows of
    one line from each column and comes out interleaved.

    TABLES. Cells that share a row of the page share a line of the pane,
    and a cell is never treated as a heading however tall its box. Without
    that, the header row of a bill became five one-word titles running
    down the page and the figures under them another twelve.

    WHAT IS CARRIED OVER. Held-back lines - account numbers, meter
    numbers, amounts - go in as they were written. On the page these are
    deliberately left showing in the original, because a recogniser that
    turns one digit of an account number into another has produced
    something that still looks like an account number. There is no page
    behind the pane to leave them on, so leaving them out would simply
    lose them, and an invoice without its account number is not a
    translation of the invoice.

    Tab switches between the two, both ways. The measurement can be right
    about the page and wrong about what somebody wanted, and there is no
    way for the app to tell them what they are not being shown - so a
    wrong choice costs a keystroke instead of a second capture.

    Over the six-language corpus at a 900-pixel capture: no words lost and
    nothing overlapping on any of the six, against 43 overlapping pairs
    laid out in place.

    What this does not fix: the pane cannot put a page in the right order
    when the page was read wrong. On the three-column page a fifth of the
    lines come back as one box spanning two columns, and with a line
    straddling the gutter there is no clean band to cut on. Ignoring the
    wide lines to find the gutter was tried and rejected - a third of that
    page qualifies as wide, so the rule throws away the page to find its
    columns and still has to place what it threw away. That is the reader
    to fix, not the ordering.

  - A line is measured by how much room it takes, not how many
    characters it has.

    unwrap() joins the line breaks a document put in and keeps the ones
    somebody meant, and one of its guards was a floor: below 45 characters
    a break is left alone, so a poem, a list or a menu is never run
    together into one sentence.

    45 characters is a Latin assumption and it quietly broke every
    language that is not written in Latin. A full-width column of the
    Korean page in the corpus holds 25 characters - each takes two columns
    of the page and carries about as much as an English word - so no line
    in it ever reached the floor, no break was ever a candidate, and every
    Korean paragraph went to the engine pre-chopped into fragments:

        The researchers selected pairs of
        buildings
        with similar structures and
        floors across three cities.

    The identical paragraph in French measured 85 and joined perfectly.
    The text was not the difference. The ruler was.

    Lines are now measured in display width - Unicode already records
    which characters take two columns - and the floor is 40 of those.
    Forty columns is about seven English words or twenty Han characters;
    no running column of text is narrower, and a list item, a line of
    verse and a heading are all well under it.

    Two things came with it.

    A break between two characters of a script written without word spaces
    is closed with nothing in between. Chinese and Japanese use no word
    spaces at all, and a narrow Korean column breaks wherever the line runs
    out, mid-word as readily as between words. Both errors are possible
    here and they are not equally bad: a space inside a word makes two
    fragments that mean nothing where one word meant something, while two
    words run together is a spacing mistake Korean writers make constantly
    and every engine is built to absorb - the reading path has been
    handing them unspaced Korean since the beginning. The run-together is
    the mistake to prefer, and it is written down in the code as a trade
    rather than as a fact.

    And a line that opens a list item is never joined onto the line above
    it, however long that line was. Items in a list are all about the same
    length, which is the one shape the ratio test cannot tell apart from a
    wrapped column, and lowering the floor made that likelier rather than
    less likely.

  - Words cut in half in the result card, from line breaks that were
    never in the text.

    Text copied out of a PDF or a page of a book carries a break at the
    end of every *rendered* line. Those belong to the column it was set
    in. Sent to an engine they are a paragraph pre-chopped into
    fragments, and what comes back is chopped in the same places - which
    no longer fall between words, because two languages do not run out of
    column at the same point:

        ALLERGENS - Our dis
        hes may contain: gluten, eggs, milk, nuts, celery, must
        ard, sulphites, fish and crust
        a
        ceans.

    The translation is perfect and every word in it is broken. The
    reading path has always joined these - it is what ocr.clean does with
    a page of recognised lines - and the clipboard path never did, which
    is exactly why one looked broken and the other did not.

    Getting it wrong the other way is worse than doing nothing: joining
    lines somebody meant to keep apart turns a poem or a list into a
    run-on sentence, and nothing on screen says the app did it. So a
    break is closed only when the line before it was long in its own
    right - at least forty-five characters - *and* reached nearly as far
    as the longest line around it, and did not end in something that
    finishes a sentence. A blank line always ends a paragraph.

    A hyphen at the end of a line keeps its word together rather than
    being dropped. It cannot be told from a real one: "Etablissement
    non-" ends a line in a French menu and the word is genuinely
    "non-fumeur". Dropping it makes "nonfumeur", which is not a word in
    any language and cannot be translated; keeping it makes "Ver-brauch"
    out of a hyphenation, which is ugly and still reads.

  - A placeholder with nothing behind it is removed rather than printed.

    Numbers, dates and reference codes are swapped for a marker before an
    engine sees them and swapped back after. An engine sometimes invents
    an extra marker - a language model handed a paragraph full of ZQXAQ,
    ZQXBQ, ZQXCQ will occasionally produce a ZQXQQ of its own - and there
    is no value to restore, because nothing was ever taken from there.

    It used to be left where it stood. That is how a bill reached the
    screen reading "ZQXQQ kWh" in place of "3.344 kWh": the reader sees
    the app's internals and cannot tell what was lost. Removing it leaves
    a gap in a sentence, which reads as a translation that dropped a word
    - recoverable, and honest about itself.

  - Text drawn on top of other text, from two causes that are one
    mistake.

    Both come of asking "what comes next?" by vertical position alone, on
    a page the column-finder reports as one column - which is every page
    it has been shown.

    The room a block may grow into was measured down to whatever came next
    in the column's running order. On a table that is very often the cell
    to the *right* on the same row, so a block was told it had no room
    when the space under it was empty, or told it had the whole page when
    the next row began ten pixels down. Room is now measured to the next
    block that overlaps it horizontally, which is what "in the way"
    actually means.

    And a block that grew pushed everything after it down, through one
    running cursor per column. On a page laid out in columns the next
    thing in that order is usually in a different column. That is the
    contract whose clause numbers drifted away from their clauses:
    nothing was wrong with either block, they were moving each other. A
    block is now pushed only by blocks it overlaps horizontally.

    Measured over the six-language corpus at a 900-pixel capture: pairs of
    blocks drawn over one another fell from 80 to 43, and on the four
    pages that are not laid out in columns from 23 to 3. The rest are on
    the two multi-column pages and are dominated by the reader returning
    lines that span two columns, which is a reading fault rather than a
    placement one.

  - A glossary rule may name the language it belongs to.

    The words an engine gets wrong are mostly single cells of a table, one
    word with no sentence around it: Menge becomes "Crowd", Eingang
    "Entrance", PLATS "PLACE". Google's document translation makes the
    identical mistakes on the identical cells, so this is a limit of
    translating a word alone rather than a fault in any one engine.

    The mechanism for pinning a term already existed - the glossary masks
    a term before an engine sees it and puts the chosen wording back after
    - and could not be used here, because a rule applied to every
    language. That is right for a brand name and wrong for a word: "Art"
    is German for "type" and English for art.

    A rule may now be written "de: Menge = Quantity". Untagged rules are
    unchanged. Tagged rules apply only when the source language is known,
    and are skipped when it is not, so not knowing costs a term left to
    the engine rather than an English word rewritten by a German rule.
    Seeded with the terms seen failing on real documents.

  - Finding columns over bands of the page: tried, measured, backed out.

    Recorded because the idea is obviously right and will come back. The
    column-finder looks for gutters down the whole page, so a full-width
    headline closes every gutter and the page falls back to one column -
    it reports one column on all six test pages, including a three-column
    newsletter. Looking for gutters *between* the full-width lines is how
    the page is actually laid out.

    Measured, it was worse: blocks drawn over each other went from 43 back
    up to 69, and a single-column justified contract was cut into
    thirty-eight regions, a "gutter" being found between each clause
    number and its clause. Paragraph grouping runs per region, so that
    broke paragraphs that had been fine. It needs a test of whether a band
    is really in columns - several consecutive rows sharing the same
    gutter positions - rather than accepting any wide enough gap.

  - The script run-off was decided on a picture too small to show the
    script it was deciding about.

    Before a page is read, each candidate model is scored on a sample of
    it and the best is used. The sample was the whole page resized to fit
    a pixel budget. What the score measures is how well a model reads
    glyphs, so resizing the glyphs changes the score - and it changes it
    most for the scripts whose detail matters most. Measured on a page of
    Chinese at a 900-pixel capture: at full size the Chinese model scores
    92.6 against Latin's 38.9 and wins outright; inside the budget it
    scores 25.9 against Latin's 29.7 and loses. The page was then read
    with a Latin model, which does not fail - it returns confident
    nonsense, and the nonsense was translated and drawn over the page.

    Take a band of the page at its own resolution instead: the band with
    the most ink in it, because a strip of margin says nothing about any
    model. Same pixel budget, and the thing being measured survives.
    Measured over six pages in six languages the correct model now wins
    every one, and by two to three times rather than by a few points.

    Two things went with it. The measuring pass may be reused as the
    answer only when it looked at the whole page - a band and a full-size
    image have the same scale factor, which is exactly how a page
    translated from the waist up would have got in. And every candidate is
    now scored on the same sample: the seed's score used to arrive from a
    different pass over a different piece of the page at a different size,
    and the seed is always Latin, so the bias ran one way.

  - A row of a table was read as one box, and the more like a table it was
    the less likely it was to be split.

    Breaking a row into cells depends on finding a gap far wider than a
    word space. The reference was the median gap on the line - so on a row
    that is entirely columns, where every gap is a column gap, the median
    is itself a column gap and nothing on the line can beat it. Measured
    on a bill at a 900-pixel capture:

      Zaehlernummer / Zeitraum / Zaehlerstand / Verbrauch
          widest gap 254, threshold required 756    not split
      Ihre Stromkosten .....................  8,34 EUR
          widest gap 658, threshold required 2303   not split

    A word space is never wider than the line is tall, so the median is
    taken over the gaps that could be one. Prose is untouched: its gaps
    are four or five pixels either way.

  - A cell is not a line of a paragraph, and the difference had to be
    made explicit before the row fix was safe to keep.

    The first render after rows started splitting was worse than before:
    the header cells marched diagonally down the page, each one pushing
    the next as though it were the line beneath it in a paragraph. Three
    behaviours that are right for prose are wrong for a cell - growing
    downward into the next line's space, being gathered with the line
    below into one block, and reaching right into the space alongside.

    A cell is recognised by having something else beside it on the same
    row, which is visible in the geometry and needs no idea of what a
    table is. A cell is a paragraph of one, keeps its position exactly,
    takes its size from its own box rather than from a tier shared with
    the page, and reaches nowhere to its right - the space beside a cell
    is the next cell, and a cover filled with this cell's background
    painted across it is the grey block that wipes out half a table.

  - Figures are not sent to a translation engine.

    An engine cannot improve a meter number, a date or a column of
    amounts, and it can corrupt them; a corrupted number still looks like
    a number, so nothing on the page says it changed. A line counts as
    language when at least a fifth of its characters are letters.

    The obvious version of that rule is wrong and is recorded because it
    cost seventeen test failures in one run: asking for a *run* of two
    letters throws away a line of Chinese, where one character is a word,
    and a line reading "a" or "I", where one letter is one. It is a share,
    and nothing about how long the letter runs are.

  - A page can be measured instead of looked at.

    tools/pagecheck.py runs the real reading and layout code over a corpus
    and counts what has actually gone wrong on real documents: rows left
    unsplit, words lost between the engine and the page, lines still
    showing under their own translation, identifiers protected, and the
    ratio between the largest and smallest text drawn. No network - the
    translator is a stand-in that marks every word so it can be traced.

    Over the six-language corpus this release moves: rows left unsplit 22
    to 1, identifiers protected 2 to 24 of 47, correct script 3 of 6 to 6
    of 6, with words lost and lines showing staying at zero.

    One correction worth keeping: the first version of the "still showing"
    measure compared covers against the box the recogniser drew rather
    than the ink inside it, and reported 25 lines showing on pages where
    nothing was. A box is routinely taller than the writing in it.

  - Which lines belong to one paragraph is decided once, and the
    translation is no longer cut up and reassembled.

    A paragraph goes to the engine whole — that is what makes the wording
    good — and then has to be put back onto the boxes it came from. The
    grouping was worked out twice: once before translating, from the raw
    lines, and once before drawing, from boxes that had since been shrunk
    to their ink and with the held-back lines already removed. Nothing
    made the two answers agree.

    When they agreed the pieces reassembled into the sentence they came
    from and nobody could tell. When they disagreed the page filled with
    half-sentences that began nowhere. And a line held back on purpose —
    an email or web address, left in the original because a recogniser
    that turns one letter of an address into another produces something
    that still looks like an address and goes somewhere else — took its
    share of the words away with it. Measured on a four-line contact
    block: four words of eleven gone from the page, and the tail of the
    paragraph stranded as its own block starting mid-sentence.

    It read as a bad translation. It was not one. Reading was measured on
    the way to finding this and is not the weak link here: on rendered
    German at 9, 11, 13, 16 and 20 pixels, and on a page downsampled from
    twice the size, on a grey panel, in pale grey, in dark mode and after
    a resample, the reader came back between 98.4% and 100% correct every
    time. The words were being lost after they were read.

    The grouping is made in one place now and carried on the line, along
    with the whole translated paragraph, so the overlay lays out the
    sentence the engine returned rather than a reassembly of its pieces. A
    held-back line breaks the paragraph rather than being skipped over
    inside one — it has to, because a rectangle spanning the lines above
    and below it paints over the address that was deliberately left
    showing.

  - The page is checked as a page.

    Four releases in a row fixed something visible on a snipped page and
    the next snip showed something else. Each fix was right, and none of
    them was a check: a repair to a particular number says nothing about
    the page nobody has looked at yet.

    Three questions are now asked of a finished page, and they are
    properties of the page rather than of the code that made it, so they
    keep working when that code is rewritten again. Nothing was lost —
    every word the translator returned is on the page. Nothing shows
    through — every line that was translated is under a cover. What was
    held back is still readable — an address left in the original is not
    painted over.

    Asked twice: once of a perfect reading, which is the right way to test
    the layout, and once through whatever reader the machine actually has,
    which is the only way to be confident about the app rather than about
    a fixture. Every fix in this release was backed out again with these
    in place. All were caught, and the pass through the real reader
    reported "paragraph 0 lost 3 of its 14 words" rather than a diff.

  - A window position beginning with a minus does not mean a negative
    coordinate.

    It means a distance from the far edge of the screen: -1920 asks for
    the window's right edge to sit 1920 pixels in from the right of the
    display. Every monitor arranged to the left of, or above, the main one
    has a negative coordinate, so every one of those overlays was placed
    by measuring inward from the wrong end of the wrong screen — landing
    on a display that already had one and leaving another bare. One
    covered, one doubly covered, one not at all, and dragging across them
    behaving as though the desk were a single screen.

    Measured, on a screen 1280 wide: "300x200-200+0" places the window at
    x=780, and "300x200+-200+0" places it at x=-200. Both signs is how a
    negative coordinate is written.

    Why it survived being fixed once is the part worth keeping. The test
    asserted the string, and the string looked right — it required exactly
    the broken spelling. The tests now create a window, place it, and ask
    where it went, which cannot be fooled that way, and one of them keeps
    the evidence that a bare minus really does mean something else.

  - A window belongs on the display the work is on, not on the main one.

    The window system's idea of "the screen" is one display. The finished
    translation was pulled back inside those bounds, so a selection made
    on a second or third monitor had its translation dragged onto the
    first, away from the thing it was translating; one made on a monitor
    to the left was pushed to zero for the same reason. The room the
    overlay has to grow into before it starts scrolling was measured the
    same way — too small on a large second monitor, and too big on a small
    one, where too big means it hangs off the edge, which is the failure
    that measurement was added to avoid.

    Placement now asks which display a window mostly sits on — by how much
    of it lands on each, not by where its corner is — and clamps within
    that one, falling back to the old answer when the displays cannot be
    enumerated, which is correct on a single screen.

  - Windows can report a screen's size wrongly, and the app believed it.

    A program has to ask Windows for real pixels. If it does not, Windows
    reports a made-up size for any display set to a different scale from
    the main one, and every measurement made on that display is then a
    measurement of a lie: correct on one monitor and wrong on the others,
    which reads as an intermittent fault and is a setting.

    The app did ask. It could not tell whether Windows agreed. The call
    returns a result code rather than raising, the code was ignored, and
    the setting can only be made once per process and fails silently when
    something set it first — which a packaged app's own manifest can do,
    invisibly from inside. It now asks in the best way available first,
    checks the answer, falls back through the older calls, and reports
    what was actually granted.

    One place still assumed a scale rather than asking for it: how much of
    the screen was grabbed decides how large the translation is set, and
    it was counted in pixels. A 4K display at 150% draws the same document
    half again as wide, so a modest selection on it counted as a whole
    page and was set at the size a whole page gets — smaller and denser,
    and only on that monitor. It is divided by that display's own scale
    now, so it means the same thing on every screen.

  - The picker writes down what it saw.

    A selection that spreads across the desk is one of two things and a
    screenshot of it cannot tell them apart: either the screen grabber
    described the displays wrongly and the overlays went exactly where
    they were asked, or it described them correctly and they did not. Both
    numbers — what was asked for and what happened, per display — go into
    the log every time the picker opens, with the scale each display is
    drawn at and how the process is being told about pixels. It costs a
    few lines of a text file and it is the difference between fixing the
    next one of these and guessing at it a fourth time.

  - The lines inside a box are counted, so a box holding two of them is
    not read as one enormous line.

    Shrinking a box to its ink, which the last release added, cures a box
    that swallowed the blank space above a line. It cannot cure a box the
    recogniser drew round two lines of the page at once: the ink really is
    that tall, the measurement is honest, and the conclusion drawn from it
    is wrong. It passed under the three-times ceiling because two lines is
    not five, and it is why an invoice number came back in headline type
    on a page of ordinary body text.

    Two lines have blank paper between them and one line does not, so the
    question is answerable from the pixels rather than from the
    recogniser. The box is reduced to one column of averages — one number
    per row, how much ink that row holds — and the bands of writing are
    counted. The height that decides the font size is then the box divided
    by the number of lines in it.

    The trap is that a band is not a line: the umlaut over an A and the
    dot over an i sit above their letters with clear blank rows between,
    and counting those would halve the size of every accented word on the
    page. A band counts only if it is nearly as tall as the tallest band
    in the box — measured against that rather than against the box,
    because each line of a two-line box is only about a quarter of it.
    Checked against all caps, an underlined heading, a boxed cell, leader
    dots, nine-point fine print, sub-scripts and a row of hyphens: all
    still one line. Two Latin lines, three Latin lines and two CJK lines:
    counted correctly. End to end on a page of eight body lines plus one
    merged box, the merged box was set at 30 against a body of 9; it is 11
    against 11 now.

  - The box is shrunk to the ink in it before anything is measured from
    it, which is the root fix for the size problem the previous release
    only guarded against.

    The size of a translation comes off the height of the box the reader
    handed back, which trusts the reader to have drawn that box round the
    line and nothing else. Usually it has. Sometimes it has swallowed the
    blank space above, or run two lines together, and then the number being
    measured is the emptiness rather than the writing — so the translation
    is set several times too big, wraps over half a dozen lines, and
    shoulders a screenful of the page out of the way. It happens most on
    Chinese and Japanese, where characters are square, there are no
    ascenders or descenders and no spaces between words, so the vertical
    edges of a line are far less obvious than in Latin text.

    The pixels are not in any doubt about where the writing is. Anything
    differing from the background is ink and everything else is blank, and
    blank is where a line is not. A box reported as 70 pixels tall
    collapses to the 14 pixels of real writing, and moves, because it also
    began 55 pixels above where the text actually was.

    Done as a difference against a flat background and one bounding-box
    call, both single operations inside the imaging library, because this
    runs for every line on the page.

    Two things learned putting it in. The first version *dropped* any line
    where no ink was found, on the reasoning that a box over blank paper is
    a false positive — which is true and is still the wrong thing to do,
    because finding no ink is not proof there is none. Pale grey on white
    is real text a threshold can miss, and dropping on that evidence loses
    the quietest writing on a page: footnotes, disclaimers, small print. It
    tightens when the pixels agree and keeps the box as it came when they
    do not, which is the direction that cannot lose words.

    The second is that this fixes more than the size. Every number
    downstream comes off these boxes — which lines count as neighbours,
    how paragraphs group, how far a cover may reach. A box that included
    blank space was wrong in all of them at once, and only one of them was
    visible.

    The ceiling added last release, capping any line at three times the
    page's own text, stays as a backstop. It was a guess about what is
    plausible; this is a measurement.

  - The watermark filter wanted two marks against a line out of three —
    repeated, tilted, faint — and the one that prompted it was tiled
    diagonally a dozen times, so it scored all three easily. The next one
    was pale, horizontal, in a corner, twice. One mark. Kept, translated,
    and drawn several times the size of the mail it sat on, because a
    watermark is set large enough to read across a whole page.

    Rather than lower the bar, which is there to stop real sentences being
    deleted on one signal, the missing signal was added: how big the line
    is against the page's own text. That is the thing a reader notices
    about a watermark first and it was the thing not being looked at. Pale
    and 4.6 times the size of everything around it is two marks.

    Adding it immediately deleted a black heading, which is how the
    contrast measure turned out to be wrong as well. It compared the
    darkest tenth of a box with the lightest tenth; ink is a small minority
    of the pixels in its own box and a smaller one the taller the box is,
    so on a heading — mostly the space around the letters — a tenth reached
    past the glyphs into the paper and averaged the two together. Black
    type scored 51, which is squarely watermark range. Reading two per cent
    into each extreme measures the ink rather than the ink diluted by its
    margins.

  - Watermark removal was not running on two of the four ways a page can
    be read, and that was found by writing a test that walks every exit
    from the reading code rather than by seeing anything fail. The plain
    Tesseract fallback returned straight to the caller, and so did the
    path taken when a cloud reader has been pinned in Settings. So on the
    plainest install there is — no built-in reader, no cloud key — the
    feature had never run at all, and on a pinned cloud reader it stopped
    running the moment the setting was changed. A feature that works on
    some machines and not others is harder to trust than one that does not
    exist, and neither gap was visible from any single machine.

  - Paragraphs are laid out once instead of a line at a time, and that
    single change cured three separate things that had each looked like
    its own bug.

    Placing every line independently meant a line that grew nudged the one
    below it down, and the nudges accumulated. Near the top of a column
    nothing was visibly wrong; by the foot of it the text had drifted well
    away from the layout it was replacing, which is why the damage always
    looked worst on numbered lists and on label-and-value rows — those are
    where a few pixels of drift put the label beside the wrong value.
    Sizing was per line too, and so was the colour sample, so one paragraph
    came back in two or three sizes and two or three greys. That reads as
    damage even when every word is right.

    And the wording was being thrown away. The engine is handed a whole
    paragraph, precisely because a paragraph is what makes the translation
    good — then the result was cut back into per-line fragments to fit
    boxes the German had decided the shape of. A paragraph now keeps one
    rectangle, one size, one colour, and its translation is rewrapped
    inside it.

    Measured on the same page as the floor work below: 68 blocks became
    34, and the overlay went from 1.04x the height of the capture to
    exactly 1.00x. No growth at all.

    It also surfaced something that would have shipped quietly. render()
    reports how much it drew and the caller compares that against the
    number of lines the recogniser found, giving up and showing the card
    instead when less than half the page was replaced. With paragraphs,
    four blocks standing for twenty lines is four against twenty — so
    every dense page would have failed that test and silently fallen back
    to the card, which is the exact behaviour the count exists to prevent.
    Blocks carry how many lines they stand for now. Worth remembering that
    the bug was not in the new code: it was in an old assumption that the
    unit being counted would never change.

  - Zoom was working the whole time and sending the page off the screen.
    The window's position was clamped to the display and its *size* never
    was, so a zoomed full-screen capture became a window wider than the
    monitor, pinned at the left edge, with the right-hand side unreachable
    — and no sideways scrolling to reach it with. Three notches took the
    canvas to 2712 pixels on a 1280 pixel screen. From the chair that is
    indistinguishable from a zoom that does nothing.

    Both dimensions are clamped now and the content scrolls inside. Two
    more faults in the same method: the old canvas was destroyed before
    its replacement had been built, so any failure left an empty overlay
    with no way back; and both failure paths swallowed the exception, so
    "could not zoom" and "zoom was never wired up" looked identical
    everywhere including the log. The binding priority was checked first
    and was innocent — Tk does correctly prefer Control-MouseWheel over
    MouseWheel.

  - Dragging, because sideways scrolling needs hardware not everybody has.
    A tilting wheel or a trackpad is required to scroll horizontally, so
    on an ordinary two-button mouse whatever zoom had pushed past the right
    edge was simply unreachable. Press and drag moves the page both ways
    at once.

    The awkward part was that the button already meant "close". Every drag
    begins with a press, so a press that closed the overlay would make
    dragging impossible. Inside a page bigger than its window the button
    now belongs to reading it; clicking away still closes, and so do
    Escape and right-click. Where the whole translation fits there is
    nothing to drag and clicking it to dismiss is the quickest way out, so
    that is untouched.

  - The cover painted over a line reaches to the right, on the reasoning
    that the space beside a line is the same background and free to use.
    Clipping it to the column stopped it landing on the column next door;
    inside a column the reasoning is still only usually true. A line ending
    beside a shaded panel, a table cell or a rule got a cover filled with
    its own background and painted across something a different colour,
    which is the stray grey block on an otherwise white page.

    The reach is measured now — walk right until the background stops
    being the background — sampled every few pixels over three rows,
    because this runs for every line on the page and the answer does not
    improve for being exact. On a test page it drops from 52 pixels to 18
    where a panel begins, and stays full where nothing is in the way.

  - The macOS menu bar could not change the target language. The tray menu
    on Windows has carried that switcher since it was written; the menu
    bar was four flat items and its builder had no notion of a submenu at
    all, so the same application offered a one-click language change on
    one platform and a trip through Settings on the other. This had been
    reported once as "where is the batch language option on the Mac" —
    there is no batch mode anywhere in the app, and what was actually
    missing was the switcher. Both menus read from the same list now,
    which is the only way two menus in one program do not slowly acquire
    different opinions about which languages deserve a click.

  - The clipboard shortcut writes down what it did: whether Accessibility
    was granted, whether the synthetic copy was observed to land, how much
    text was on the clipboard before and after, and what was on screen when
    the card opened. Every failure mode on that path is silent by
    construction — macOS refuses a synthetic keystroke without a word, and
    falling back to the old clipboard produces a confident translation of
    the wrong text — so a report of "it translated something else" arrived
    with four candidate causes and nothing to choose between them. This
    does not fix that report. It makes the next one answerable.

  - The floor was set above the size documents are written in, and that
    one number was doing more damage than everything the previous release
    fixed. Eleven points was a guess, made without measuring anything. A
    business letter is set in nine. So the floor never protected a single
    line — it *inflated* every line on the page by a fifth, or by two
    thirds where the capture was judged small enough to deserve fifteen.

    Nothing then fitted the space it came from, so every block pushed the
    one below it down, and a page snipped whole came back three times the
    height of the thing that was snipped. The columns and the reflow added
    in the previous release were working correctly the entire time: they
    were faithfully finding room for text that never needed enlarging.
    That is the worst kind of bug to look at, because everything visibly
    wrong is downstream of the cause and behaving perfectly.

    Measured on one page, changing nothing but the floor:

        15pt (what shipped)  3.0x the height of the capture
        13pt                 2.4x
        11pt                 1.9x
         9pt (the document)  1.28x
         7pt                 1.04x

    The document was already readable at nine point. It is a document;
    that is the size documents are. Enlarging it was never a readability
    improvement, only a demolition of the layout, and the reason it looked
    like a layout problem is that by the time anything was drawn it was.

    The rule now measures against the document rather than a constant: a
    block may give up a quarter of its size to fit and no further, with an
    absolute backstop of seven points. One formula covers all three cases
    — a heading gives up at most a quarter and is still a heading, nine
    point body text stays nine point, and five point legal small print,
    being under the backstop, is the one thing that grows.

    The lesson worth keeping is not about the number. It is that the
    number was chosen by reasoning about screens in the abstract instead
    of measuring one real document, and it survived a release because
    everything it broke looked like a different problem.

  - A translated page could show both languages at once, which was mine
    from the release before. A block that grows is drawn lower than the
    line it replaces, and the covering patch was drawn at the block's new
    position rather than the original's — so the source text stayed on
    screen above its own translation. Every line that did not fit appeared
    twice. The cover now starts at whichever is higher, the line's own top
    or the block's, which covers both.

  - Chinese and Japanese could not be wrapped at all. The wrapper broke on
    whitespace and neither language uses any between words, so a paragraph
    arrived as exactly one piece, was laid out as one line, and everything
    past the edge of the box was not drawn. Nothing anywhere reported it:
    the words were simply absent. Both now break between characters, which
    is what those scripts do, keeping runs of Latin — a code, a number, a
    name — together and not stranding closing punctuation at the start of
    a line.

  - The font search returned fonts that could not draw the script it had
    been asked for. Asked for Chinese with no Chinese font installed, it
    fell through to "any scalable file in the directory" and returned the
    first in alphabetical order — a mathematics font, carrying not one
    Chinese character. Nothing downstream can tell a font from a wrong
    font, so the page came out as rows of empty boxes in silence.

    It now draws the character before offering the file, and returns
    nothing when it cannot, which the caller can act on: the PDF path
    falls back to the built-in CJK fonts, which are always present. The
    check found something on the first run that no amount of listing font
    names would have — a Japanese font that also covers Chinese, because
    the two scripts share their Han characters.

  - A PDF that contains its own text is read rather than photographed.
    The old path rendered every page to a bitmap and recognised it, which
    is right for a scan and wrong for a file that was generated: the text
    is in there with the exact box, size and colour of every word, and
    recognising a picture of it turned "E.ON Energie Deutschland GmbH ·
    Postfach" into "EON Enroe DeutscHand GiH - PastSuch" before the
    translator saw anything. The engine then translated that faithfully.

    Two things that took a second pass. The base-14 PDF fonts are Latin-1
    and Latin-1 has no euro sign, so every amount on a bill came out as
    "9,92 ?" — on a document about money. And the paragraph grouping came
    from the screen overlay, where all there is to go on is where the
    recogniser found each line; a PDF states where its blocks are, and
    ignoring that merged a heading into the paragraph above it and ran a
    column of labels into the column of values beside it.

    `_translate_pdf_text` returns None rather than raising on anything it
    cannot read honestly — no text layer, a stray page number, a broken
    character map, fewer than three pages in five carrying text — and the
    old path runs. The worst case for any document is what the app did
    before.

  - The in-place translation was rewritten, and it was one change rather
    than five. A two-column bill made every fault visible at once and
    fixing any of them alone would have left the page unreadable.

    The renderer's most load-bearing assumption was that the space to the
    right of a line is empty and free to spill into. On one column of
    prose that is true, and it is what lets a longer translation take the
    room it needs. On a letter with an address block beside the body it is
    false, and the cost was not subtle: the covering rectangles reached
    35% of a line's width to the right and painted over the column next
    door, and the vertical room each line was given was the gap to
    whichever line came next *anywhere on the page* — which in two columns
    is usually a line in the other column sitting a few pixels lower. So
    the right-hand column was measured against the left, got nonsense
    numbers, shrank hard, and then had its remainder trimmed. That is why
    the worst damage was always on the narrow column.

    Columns are now found as vertical gutters: bands of x that no line
    crosses, wide enough to not be word spacing, sized against the text's
    own height rather than the page's so a dense page and a large-print
    one are judged alike. A full-width headline crosses the gutter and
    closes it, and the page falls back to one column — the safe direction,
    because the failure of treating two columns as one is painting over
    something, and the failure of treating one as two is only a narrower
    spill.

    Trimming is gone entirely. It was defended before on the grounds that
    spilling over the line below is worse, since covered words can be
    reached by moving the eye while trimmed words are gone — the first
    half of which is right and the second half of which was the wrong
    conclusion. A translation cut off mid-sentence is the one failure a
    reader cannot detect: a document may contain an ellipsis of its own,
    and the missing words leave nothing behind to notice. Text that
    overruns now pushes the rest of its own column down. Lines that fit do
    not move by a pixel.

    The size setting was doing two jobs and one of them backwards. On the
    result card `popup_font_size` is the size of the text, which is what
    the live preview beside it shows. On the overlay it was passed as
    `min_size` — the floor below which the fitter could not shrink — so
    raising it did not enlarge anything, it forbade fitting and truncated
    the remainder. Eighteen gave you three words and an ellipsis where
    twelve gave you the paragraph. The overlay now derives its own size
    from how much of the screen was captured, because a sentence and a
    page are different requests: the first wants to be readable where it
    sits, the second wants to still look like the page, and the way to
    read that is to zoom, which the overlay already did. 16px at 500
    points across or less, 12px at 1200 or more, sliding between, and a
    hard floor of 11 that nothing crosses.

    Then the split. `layout()` places everything and returns the blocks
    and the height they need; `paint()` draws them with PIL; `render()` is
    both and keeps its signature, so a translated file — which has to come
    out as a picture — is untouched. That is what allowed the last piece.

    A bitmap handed to Tk is one image pixel to one point. On a display
    that draws two device pixels per point, a picture made at capture
    resolution is scaled down to fit the window and scaled back up to
    reach the screen, so every glyph makes the trip twice and arrives
    soft. The selection frame is canvas vectors and is sharp on the same
    screen in the same application, which is the whole argument. So the
    covers are canvas rectangles now, the translation is canvas text, and
    the mark is canvas vectors — that last one mattering more than it
    sounds, because PIL's arc rasteriser has no antialiasing whatsoever
    and the mark was stair-stepped before any resampling touched it. Only
    the page photograph stays a bitmap, and nobody is reading that; it is
    there so the translation sits on its own background. Zooming became a
    re-layout rather than a magnification of something already drawn.

    And then scrolling, which is what closed the question the rest of it
    opened. Not trimming means a dense page can produce more text than the
    space it came from, and growing the window to hold it works right up
    until the content is taller than the display — past which it stops
    helping, because the words that would not fit the page now do not fit
    the screen either, and a paragraph below the bottom edge has been lost
    as surely as a trimmed one. So the window grows to at most 92% of the
    screen and the content scrolls inside it. Trackpad deltas are used as
    they arrive rather than quantised into notches, which is the
    difference between tracking the fingers and stepping. The mark and the
    scroll thumb carry a tag and are moved back by the same amount on
    every scroll: everything on a canvas scrolls together, and the mark is
    not content — it frames the window, and a translation that scrolls out
    of its own frame is the obvious way to get this wrong.

    One piece of the original diagnosis is deliberately still open.
    Background colour is sampled from the whole line box, so a line whose
    box clips a shaded panel takes that shade for its cover, which is
    where grey bands over a white page come from. Clipping covers to the
    column took most of the sting out — a cover can no longer carry the
    wrong shade into a neighbour — and the rest wants sampling from a thin
    margin around the glyphs, which is a separate piece of work.

  - The corner marks on the selection frame had a small spike at each
    corner. The straight run out of each elbow was drawn with a round cap,
    and Tk strokes an arc with flat ends — so the cap added a semicircle
    half a stroke deep pointing back across the join, and with the arc
    curving away underneath it, that bulge escaped the curve. Worst on the
    black underlay, which is the widest stroke of the two and therefore
    the one that showed. Butt caps meet the arc's flat ends flush and
    tangentially, which is one continuous mark with no join visible.

  - The macOS menu bar said "Snip && Translate". The doubled ampersand is
    correct in the tray menu — that one is drawn by a toolkit where a
    single "&" marks the underlined access key, so a literal one has to be
    escaped — and AppKit has no such escape and printed what it was
    handed. The test covers both halves, because the obvious tidy-up is to
    make the two menus agree and that would put a stray underline in the
    tray.

  - Esc did not cancel the snip on macOS, and the reason was not the
    binding. It never was: the bindings are on the window, on the canvas
    and app-wide, there is a retry loop that calls lift() and
    focus_force() three times over half a second, and an earlier fix
    promotes the whole process to a foreground application before the
    overlay is even created. All of that was aimed at a focus problem
    that Tk cannot solve here. The overlay is borderless -
    overrideredirect(True) - and a borderless window on macOS is not
    eligible to become the key window. Mouse events reach it regardless,
    which is exactly why this looked so strange from the outside:
    dragging a selection worked perfectly, the cancel button worked,
    right-click worked, and the one thing that needed keyboard delivery
    did nothing. No amount of asking Tk for focus changes what AppKit
    considers key.

    So the key is read instead of waited for. While the overlay is up,
    Escape's state is polled from the window server every 60ms via
    CGEventSourceKeyState. That is a state query rather than an event
    tap, so unlike the synthetic copy in selection.py it needs no
    Accessibility permission - which mattered, because a fix for Esc
    that asked for a second permission would not be a fix.

    The obvious-looking fix was written first and then backed out:
    raise_to_front() sets -topmost and drops it 300ms later, so calling
    it on the overlay would have quietly removed the overlay's own
    always-on-top and let other windows come up in front of a selection
    in progress. There is a test pinning that so it does not get
    re-added by someone reaching for the same obvious thing.

  - A shortcut could take the machine hostage, and the guard against it
    only worked going forward. On macOS the shortcut is registered
    through Carbon's RegisterEventHotKey, which is the right API and has
    the property that it *consumes* the keystroke - the foreground app
    never sees it. That is what makes the shortcuts work properly over a
    browser, and it is also what turns a shortcut set to cmd+C into a
    machine where Copy does not work anywhere, including in the panel
    the user would need to fix it from.

    shortcut_field.py has refused to record a bare cmd+letter for some
    time. What it does not do - could not do - is anything about a value
    already in config.json. Nothing re-checked stored shortcuts, so a
    value saved by a build from before that guard, or typed into the
    settings file by hand, was loaded and armed on every single launch
    forever. The guard was protecting the door and not the room.

    hotkey.forbidden() is the same rule as a function, and Config.migrate
    applies it on startup alongside the existing bad-defaults repair,
    falling back to that slot's own platform default. A machine already
    in this state fixes itself on the next launch rather than needing to
    be talked through anything.

  - Pressing keys into a shortcut field was the decision itself. There
    was no gap at all between a keypress and a system-wide shortcut:
    on_change applied immediately, and the risky() warning - which
    already knew that one modifier and a letter collides with almost
    everything - was computed afterwards and returned as status text.
    A warning that arrives after the thing it warns about is a caption.

    It now asks before applying and restores the previous value on no.
    Deliberately still not a refusal: risky() has always been an opinion
    rather than a rule, some people pick these knowingly, and refusing on
    upgrade would take away a shortcut somebody has been using for
    months. Setting both shortcuts to the same combination *is* refused,
    because that is not a trade-off, it is one of the two silently not
    working with nothing on screen saying which.

  - Settings > Shortcuts has a reset button. Small, and it exists because
    of the shape of the failure above: the person who most needs to put a
    shortcut back is the one who just bound something unfortunate, and on
    macOS that can be a shortcut that stopped them copying the correct
    value from anywhere else. Asking them to know the defaults, or to
    type them, is asking the wrong person. A test checks the defaults it
    restores are not themselves contested, not forbidden, and not equal
    to each other, so the button cannot hand anybody a second problem.

  - The main window could still end up in front of the first-run wizard,
    even after the raise-to-front fix below it in this file. That fix was
    correct and not the whole story: the wizard raises itself once, at
    the moment it opens, and two things elsewhere show the main window on
    their own timer rather than in response to anything clicked - a
    missing tray backend and a shortcut conflict, both checked a second
    or so into startup. Either one calls the main window's own show(),
    which does its own raise, later, and nothing had ever told it the
    wizard might still be the only thing on screen. A fresh install is
    exactly when either check is likeliest to fire and exactly when the
    wizard is likeliest to still be open, so this was not a rare
    coincidence, it was the two features colliding by design. The
    Controller now knows whether the wizard is still open and both checks
    wait for it to close before showing anything of their own, rather
    than the two windows fighting over which one is on top.
  - The language screen's "More languages" list had nowhere to go. Turning
    it on can add thirty-odd rows, well past what any reasonable window
    height shows at once, and the body they were packed into was a plain
    frame with no scrollbar - the rest was simply clipped, and the only
    way to see it was to drag the window bigger by hand. It is a
    ScrollArea now, the same widget Settings already uses, with the wheel
    forwarded to whatever it draws and each screen starting scrolled back
    to the top rather than carrying over wherever the last one left off.
    Fixing this surfaced a small standing fault in the scrollbar widget
    itself - its resize handler took an event argument it did not use and
    had no default for one, so a resize triggered without a real Tk event
    behind it (which a rebuilt scrollable body can do) raised inside the
    callback. Harmless - Tkinter's default handler just prints it - but
    noisy, and a default argument was the entire fix.

  - Settings > Behaviour has a way back into the welcome screens - a
    "Show again" button, next to a line saying what they are. They are
    shown automatically exactly once, at first launch, and that was never
    going to change; what was missing was not a reason to see them again
    but a way to, on purpose, for someone who skipped past a screen too
    fast the first time or just wants to add another language pack
    through the friendly version of that question rather than hunting for
    the equivalent controls scattered across Settings. It opens the same
    wizard, not a second copy of it, prefilled from whatever is already
    saved, and asking for it twice while one is already open brings that
    one forward rather than stacking another on top.

  - The first-run screens could open invisibly on a fresh install. Every
    other window in the app is shown in response to a click, at which
    point the process already has focus; this one opens with nobody
    having clicked anything, and Windows can refuse to foreground a
    window from a process the user did not just interact with. Nothing
    forced it to the front, and it was transient to a window that is
    permanently withdrawn for the app's whole life, which can also leave
    a window with no taskbar entry of its own. It now asks properly -
    raised, focused, and forced to the front the same way the main window
    already does - with a check afterwards that forces it again if it
    still did not show.

  - The language screen offers an opt-in checklist of languages to read,
    off by default, and turning one on installs that language's Windows
    text-recognition pack in the background rather than blocking the
    rest of setup. The choice was between waiting for it to finish and
    letting setup carry on to the key screen while it downloads;
    background won; a permission prompt and a multi-minute download
    should not stand between someone and the two questions this already
    asks. Selecting several installs them together - one permission
    prompt and one commit to Windows covering everything picked, rather
    than one prompt per language (see win_langpack.install_many).
    Declined, refused by the machine, or left off: nothing breaks, and
    the same offer comes back later, just-in-time, from Settings - it was
    never meant to be the only place this is offered, only the earliest.
    A background install's own progress could finish and then un-finish
    itself back to "Installing…" - the result and a polling tick both
    post to the same queue, the tick's path touches a log file the result
    does not, and on a fast finish the tick can queue up after the result
    and be drawn last, overwriting the answer that had already landed.
    Caught by a test before it reached anyone: the result is authoritative
    now regardless of which one the queue hands over first.

  - The progress bar is one widget now, not two. Settings and the new
    checklist each drew their own; the settings one becomes the shared
    one, so a percentage looks the same wherever it is shown, and there
    is one place to fix it rather than two to remember.

  - The app used to go straight from "unzipped" to "running in the tray"
    with nothing in between, on the theory that fewer screens is fewer
    chances to lose someone. What that actually did was hand a new
    install two silent defaults - English out, no key - and no evident
    way to know that either one was ever a choice. Two questions on first
    launch now: which language you want translations in, and an optional
    translation key.
    The two things this deliberately does not do are the interesting
    part. It does not touch Windows, and it does not download anything.
    A text-recognition pack is minutes behind an administrator prompt,
    and on a work PC the answer to that prompt may simply be no - putting
    it in front of someone before the app has done anything at all is a
    worse first impression than not having it yet, and it stays exactly
    where it already was, an explicit choice in Settings, offered later
    for a language that actually turns out to need it rather than guessed
    at up front for every language someone might one day read. It also
    does not hold anything back until a Finish button: each screen saves
    what was on it - the language, whichever keys were filled in - the
    moment it is left, so closing the window at any point keeps whatever
    had already been entered rather than discarding it for having
    stopped one screen early. Closing early still counts as done, the
    same as pressing Finish: asked once, never nagged about again.

  - "Text works, snap doesn't", reported four times on four unrelated
    pages, and it was never the page. It was reported as a watermark
    problem, and one of the four was; the other three were not, and the
    thing they had in common was that only the overlay path was failing.
    A failed line came back as its own source text. translate_lines sends
    every line in one request and drops to one request per line when the
    engine returns a different number of lines than it was given - which a
    language model does whenever it merges two headlines or splits a long
    one. On a per-line failure it appended the original. The overlay draws
    what it is handed, so the source was painted over the source: the same
    pixels, no error, and nothing to distinguish it from a line the app
    had deliberately left alone. A page the engine faltered halfway
    through looked like a page the app had finished with.
    That is the whole reason this took four rounds to find. A partial
    failure whose symptom is a complete-looking page cannot be told from
    working, and the screenshots that came back looked like a translator
    that had declined rather than one that had fallen over.
    Failures are empty now. Nothing is drawn there, the real text shows
    through, and a gap looks like a gap. Nothing translated at all is
    still an error, as it was.
    And an overlay covering less than half of what was read is no longer
    shown at all - three lines changed out of twenty is a finished job
    done badly, because the user cannot tell an untouched line from one
    the app chose not to touch. The card is the honest answer there, and
    it is what already happens when nothing can be drawn.

  - A screen of English was kept by the Korean pack, because the seven
    characters that pack invented made its reading two characters longer
    than the longest correct one, and length was the tiebreak. It won on
    its own mistakes. From the user's PC:
        ko          362 chars,  7 script, 1.9%, score 362   <- kept
        sv-SE       360 chars,  0 script, 0.0%, score 360
        de-DE       359 chars,  0 script, 0.0%, score 359
    Nothing reached the screen wrong: the reading was doubtful, so a cloud
    reader was asked and answered correctly. It cost a network round-trip
    on every snip to undo a decision that should not have been made.
    When no reading is in a writing system of its own, they are all Latin
    and the out-of-script characters in them are not content - they are
    the engine reaching for a shape it did not know. Debris costs a
    reading now rather than earning it. Small enough to stay a tiebreak: a
    reading that found twice as much text still wins with a stray
    character in it.

  - Half a watermarked document came back untranslated, and that one is
    mine, introduced the release before by the rule meant to protect
    addresses.
    Lines carrying an email address are held back so a recognised copy is
    never drawn over a real one. Corporate documents are stamped with the
    reader's own name and email, diagonally, across every paragraph. The
    recogniser reads that stamp into every line it crosses, so line after
    line "carries an address" and the page stayed in its original
    language - protecting nothing, because nothing was at risk. The
    watermark is furniture, not a link anybody is going to click.
    Frequency tells them apart. An address on most of the page is a
    stamp; an address on a line or three of it is content. Both a count
    and a share are required, because either alone is wrong in a case
    that happens: a contact page may print the same address three times
