    and mean all three, and a two-line snip of a signature is entirely
    address and means it.
    Worth naming the shape of the mistake rather than only the fix. The
    rule was written against one page, tested against that page, and
    shipped. The input that broke it - a stamped internal PDF - is the
    single most common kind of document its user reads all day.

  - A placeholder reached the screen: "connect the ZQXBQ.ZQXAQ
    aerodynamic goals with the exterior design", with "24Cd 0" left over
    at the end of the paragraph.
    Codes and numbers are swapped for markers before translation so the
    engine cannot rewrite them, then swapped back. "0.24Cd" is masked in
    two pieces - one for the code, one for the number - which leaves the
    literal text ZQXBQ.ZQXAQ in the sentence. That is two markers joined
    by a dot: not a marker, so the guard that skips already-masked tokens
    (fullmatch) said no, and a run of capitals containing a dot, which is
    exactly what the acronym pattern looks for. So the acronym pass
    masked the pair as one acronym, and unmasking put back a value that
    was itself two markers and stopped, single-pass.
    The orphans were then appended by the rule that restores anything the
    engine dropped, which is where "24Cd 0" came from.
    Two changes. The guard now skips any token containing a marker rather
    than only a token that is one. And unmasking repeats while anything is
    still masked - the first change makes that unreachable, and it is
    there so a later one cannot quietly make it reachable again.

  - The card presented a guess in the same voice as an answer. The
    shortcut copies for you; when that fails it falls back to whatever is
    already on the clipboard, which is right - somebody who copied by
    hand and pressed the shortcut meant it - and the only sign was the
    label reading "clipboard" instead of "selection". One quiet word on a
    card that otherwise looks exactly like a result, next to fluent
    English about something the user did not select.
    1.11.1 caught the case where the clipboard held this app's own last
    answer. It did nothing about the commoner one: the clipboard holding
    something the user copied earlier, which sails through and gets
    translated.
    Windows keeps a clipboard change counter that moves whenever anything
    is put on the clipboard, even a copy of identical text. If it has not
    moved, nothing was copied - a fact, not an inference. The card now
    says "old clipboard (copy failed)" when that is what happened. Where
    there is no counter the app does not know and does not claim to.

  - The selection shortcut answered the question before last, in fluent
    prose, in a card that looked exactly like a working one.
    Two faults, and the second is what made the first invisible.
    The shortcut sends Ctrl+C for the user, but only once their own
    modifiers have come back up: a copy sent while Alt is still down
    reaches the other program as Ctrl+Alt+C and copies nothing. It waited
    0.6s for that, then allowed 0.5s for the other program to put a
    paragraph on the clipboard. Both are give-up times - the loops exit
    the instant the keys lift and the instant the clipboard changes - so
    those numbers only ever cost anything in the failure case, which is
    the case they exist for. 2.0s and 1.2s now.
    The second fault is what the timeout fell back to. Using whatever is
    already on the clipboard is right; somebody who copied something by
    hand meant it. But with auto-copy on, what is on the clipboard is
    this app's own previous answer. So the fallback translated English
    into English, got it back unchanged, and presented last time's answer
    as this time's. Three selections in a row came back as the
    translation of a fourth. The only thing on screen that said so was
    one word in the label - "clipboard" where it usually says
    "selection" - and nobody reads a label on a result that looks like a
    result.
    It now remembers what it put there and refuses to translate it back,
    saying it could not copy in time and to try again. Wrong-but-silent
    becomes visible, which is the only acceptable shape for this to fail
    in. A clipboard someone filled themselves still translates normally.

  - Recognised email addresses and web addresses were drawn over the real
    ones. Every other misreading costs a word and looks like a
    misreading; this one produces something still shaped like an address,
    typeset as neatly as the page, pointing somewhere else. There is no
    upside to weigh against it either - an address is not language, and
    translating it is a no-op at best. Lines carrying one now keep their
    own pixels. The words around it on that line stay in the original,
    which is the whole price.

  - The first read of a session was much slower than every read after it,
    and it is the read that decides whether somebody thinks the app
    works. Windows loads a recognition model per installed pack and
    Vision loads its own on first use; the same work either way, the only
    question being whether the user is watching a toast while it happens.
    They now happen on a background thread at startup, while the user is
    still deciding what to snip. Reported as "very slow to read, so slow
    it doesn't give the results" on a machine where nothing was cached,
    which is every machine on the day it is installed.

  - time-read.bat was described in 1.11.0's notes and was not in the zip.
    make_release.py ships a fixed list of files and it was never added to
    it; tools/time_read.py came along because whole directories are
    copied, so the release contained the engine and not the ignition.
    Both launchers ship now, and a test walks the folder and fails if any
    .bat or .command in it is missing from that list - along with the
    reverse, and a check that files named in the changelog exist. The
    list was never the problem. Relying on remembering it was.

  - The wrong language pack won, on a Windows PC with several installed,
    and what it produced was not garbage - it was the page, correct
    almost everywhere, with a few characters silently wrong.
    Windows recognises one language per pass and reports no confidence at
    all, so five installed packs mean five readings and nothing to rank
    them by except what they say. The rule was: a reading containing two
    or more characters in a writing system of its own wins, on the
    grounds that a misreading can only ever come out as Latin. That is
    true of a Latin engine pointed at a non-Latin page - it was written
    for exactly that case, where a page of Chinese came back as
    "iRERJXIJXFL8Y" - and it is false in the other direction, which is the
    direction nobody tested.
    A CJK engine pointed at a Latin page does not fail either. It returns
    the page, mostly right, with a couple of lookalike characters dropped
    in where a shape was not in its alphabet. Two is all the rule asked
    for.
    Caught with a timing script on a PC with five packs installed
    (de-DE, en-GB, ko, sv-SE, zh-Hans-CN), reading a screen of English
    filenames:
        ko          '[] time-read [] build (勺 check.command [] debug (勺'
        zh-Hans-CN  '[ 《 ] time-read 国 ] build check.command 国 ] debu'
        de-DE       'Siz time-read build C] check.command debug C] de'
        en-GB       'Siz time-read build C] check.command debug C] de'
    kept: zh-Hans-CN. Two characters in the 4E00 block, and the reading
    with them beat the two that were right.
    On an ordinary page of Latin text the same choice put an email
    address on screen with one letter changed, a web address with an
    extra dot in the domain, and an accented letter rendered as a digit
    - then drew all of them in clean type over the correct ones. That is
    the worst shape a bug can take here: not obviously broken, just
    quietly untrue. A wrong address that looks typeset is more dangerous
    than no translation at all. The language on screen is beside the
    point; any pair of installed packs can produce it.
    The test is a share now, not a count: at least 15% of the non-space
    characters have to be in that script before the reading is believed
    because of it. A page really in another writing system is mostly in
    it; a Latin page misread by a CJK engine is two characters in two
    hundred;
    between those is a mixed page, where the Latin reading was the safer
    of the two anyway.
    And the same signal was being read backwards one layer up. Loose CJK
    in an otherwise Latin reading is the clearest evidence there is that
    the wrong pack answered - the engine reaching outside the alphabet
    actually on screen - and might_be_misread() treated it as proof the
    reading was trustworthy, so the one case that most deserved a second
    opinion was structurally excluded from getting one. It asks now.
    The rule this replaces was right about its own case and was never
    checked against the mirror image of it. Both directions have tests,
    with the readings above kept verbatim.

  - Reading a page written in Latin letters on a Mac cost six full
    passes of Vision instead of one, which made the most common case in
    the app the slowest one in it. Anything in a Latin alphabet: six
    reads. Anything in another one: one.
    Vision will read Cyrillic as the Latin letters that look like it -
    АБВГДЕЁ came back as "AEBTAEE", confidently - so the defence is to
    read the image several ways and prefer a non-Latin answer. That
    run-off short-circuits as soon as the automatic pass returns a
    non-Latin script, because a misreading can only come out as Latin.
    Which means it never short-circuits on a page that really is written
    in Latin letters - which is most pages, in most languages.
    Those fell through to the whole ladder: the full fourteen-language
    list, then CJK, then Cyrillic, then Arabic, then Thai, each at
    accurate level with its own recognition models to load, to arrive at
    the answer the first pass had already given.
    The run-off stays - nothing but a second opinion catches a confidently
    wrong alphabet. What it does not have to be is expensive. Deciding
    whether there is Cyrillic here is a far cheaper question than reading
    it, so the alternative alphabets are probed at fast recognition level
    on a copy shrunk to 700px, and full accuracy is spent only on an
    order where a probe actually found something. A named source language
    skips the run-off entirely: the user has already said what the
    alphabet is, and nothing here knows better.
    A Latin page: one proper read. Every other alphabet: unchanged, and
    the
    winning order is still re-read at full accuracy - the small fast copy
    decides which alphabet, never what it says.

  - Every snip on Windows built about ten asyncio event loops and
    abandoned all of them. Handing the picture to WinRT is four calls and
    each installed pack is one more, and _run() reached for
    get_event_loop() on a worker thread, where there is never one to
    find, so it made a new one every time. On Windows that is a
    ProactorEventLoop: an I/O completion port and a thread pool, neither
    of which is returned by being forgotten. One loop per thread now,
    kept. Nothing visible today; it is what stops the app getting slower
    the longer it stays open.

  - Korean, Japanese and Chinese overlays drew boxes instead of
    characters. PIL takes exactly one font file and has no fallback of
    its own - a glyph that is missing is drawn as a box and reported as
    success - and all three languages shared one candidate list, so a
    machine with a Chinese font and no Korean one drew Korean as tofu.
    They have a list each now, each ending in the pan-CJK fonts that
    cover all three, so the answer to a missing font is another font
    rather than a box. This is also why the result card was right while
    the overlay was wrong on the same text: the card is Tk, and Tk gets
    the operating system's font fallback for free.

  - The shortcut fields accepted no keys on a Mac. Click one, it lights up
    and says "Press keys...", press the keys, nothing. And both fields sat
    lit at the same time, which is the clue that gives the cause away: the
    second one arming had never disarmed the first.
    The widget called focus_set() on its own canvas and bound <KeyPress> to
    it. That is the obvious way to write it and it is wrong on macOS, where
    a Tk window accepts mouse clicks while not being the key window. The
    click landed, the field lit, focus_set() quietly did nothing, and no
    keystroke was ever going to arrive - and <FocusOut> did not fire
    either, because there had been no focus to lose. Both halves of the
    symptom are the same fault seen from two sides.
    It listens at the window now, after forcing the window forward, with
    one field armed at a time and a half-minute limit on being armed and
    forgotten.
    Worth being exact about when this broke, because it was reported as
    something that used to work: this file was not touched in the run of
    changes before it. What changed around it was that two copies of the
    app were running at once - each with its own window, each registering
    the same global shortcut - so the window being clicked was often not
    the window macOS was sending keys to. The widget was always fragile in
    this way; two instances is what made it show. The single-instance guard
    in this same release removes the trigger, and this removes the
    fragility.
  - Command and a letter, with nothing else, is no longer accepted as a
    shortcut on macOS. The reporter's clipboard shortcut had become plain
    Cmd+C. That is the system's own copy: taking it globally means no
    copying in any application, and the settings panel that would undo it
    is one you can no longer paste a key into. A control that can put the
    machine into a state where the control itself cannot be used needs the
    guard in the control, not in the documentation.

  - The app raised a message and then failed to recognise it. When a model
    is busy _gemini_generate throws RuntimeError(BUSY_MESSAGE) - a sentence
    written for a person, "Gemini is busy at the moment - Google's side,
    not your key". check_keys then asks _model_is_busy, which looks for
    503, overloaded, high demand, unavailable, timed out. None of those
    words survive the rewrite into English. So a busy Gemini was filed as a
    rejected key and painted red, immediately above text saying the key was
    fine, and the other keys' results vanished because the joined line
    shows failures when there are any.
    The general fault is a one-way translation. Somewhere in the middle of
    this program an error stops being a machine fact and becomes a
    sentence, and everything downstream of that point was still written to
    read machine facts. Either the friendly message carries the original
    with it, or whatever reads it afterwards has to know the friendly
    forms too. It does now, and the check for its own message comes first.
  - macOS had no single-instance guard at all. already_running() was a
    Windows named mutex and the caller was guarded by os.name == "nt", so
    on a Mac nothing was checked - which was invisible until somebody had
    two copies of Squint.app on disk. Both launched, both drew a window,
    and both registered the same global shortcut. The two windows are the
    symptom people report; the two hotkey registrations are what makes the
    app feel broken afterwards, because quitting one does not hand the
    shortcut back.
    A lock file with flock rather than a PID file: the kernel releases it
    when the process ends however it ends, where a PID file left by a
    crash reads as "already running" for ever and checking whether the PID
    is alive is wrong in its own way, because PIDs get reused. And a
    failure inside the guard returns False - two windows is a nuisance, no
    window is the app not working.
  - The Dock icon. macOS decides an application's icon and its menu-bar
    name from the path of the running executable: a binary inside
    Something.app/Contents/MacOS belongs to that bundle and anything else
    belongs to nothing. The launcher is a shell script that execs
    .venv/bin/python, so the process everybody sees lived outside the
    bundle and showed the Python rocket - while Finder, Spotlight and
    Launchpad all showed the right icon, because those read the bundle
    rather than the process. That gap is why it looked like an icon bug
    rather than a packaging one.
    So a copy of the interpreter goes into Contents/MacOS with a pyvenv.cfg
    beside it and the venv's lib symlinked in - which is exactly the shape
    of a virtual environment, at a path macOS recognises. This is the one
    change here that cannot be tested from anywhere but a Mac, so the
    launcher runs the copy once before trusting it and falls back to
    .venv otherwise. A wrong icon is a blemish; an app that will not start
    is not, and an untestable change should not be able to cause the
    second while fixing the first.
  - CFBundleVersion had been "1.2.0" since it was written and stayed there
    through eight releases. macOS caches that value, so "which build is
    this" had a confident wrong answer. It reads VERSION.txt now. A
    constant that must be edited by hand every release is a constant that
    will be wrong.
  - Every release page carries install instructions. The description was
    the changelog and nothing else, which is the right content for
    somebody who already has the app and useless to everybody else - and a
    download link lands a stranger on exactly that page. The macOS half
    leads with `bash setup.command` rather than a double-click, which is
    not pedantry: a zip fetched through a browser is quarantined, and
    double-clicking the script is refused as "from an unidentified
    developer".

  - The first snip on a friend's PC was "so slow it doesn't give the
    results". Three faults, and all three only appear on a cold machine -
    which is the machine that decides whether somebody keeps the app.
    Mine first. Splitting the remembered Gemini model in two, one release
    earlier, gave each job its own discovery walk. So a fresh key fetched
    the model listing twice on its first snip: once for reading the
    picture, again for translating what was read. I had reasoned about the
    two caches as independent and they are - independently correct, and
    jointly twice the cold-start cost. A split that is free on a warm
    machine is not free on the only machine where it shows.
    Second, the timeouts. An image request waited seventy-five seconds
    before giving up, and it waited that on the FIRST attempt, with two
    retries behind it and three models to walk. That number came from a
    real problem - a model under load genuinely takes longer than twenty
    seconds - but it was applied to the wrong attempt. The common reason a
    request has not answered is that it is not going to, and seventy-five
    seconds of that is indistinguishable from a broken application. Short
    first, long on the retry, where the caller already knows what is
    happening.
    Third, nothing capped the walk as a whole. Every individual request
    had an opinion about how long it could take; no part of the code had
    an opinion about how long the person should be kept waiting. Those are
    different questions and only the second one is about the user. There
    is a budget now, and it clamps each attempt to what is actually left
    rather than merely checking before starting - beginning a
    seventy-five-second request with four seconds on the clock honours the
    letter of a deadline and none of its purpose.
  - And a correction inside that change. Reading was made faster by
    shrinking the picture sent to Gemini from 1600 pixels to 1100, on the
    reasoning that screen text is rendered type and survives it. That
    holds for a paragraph and breaks for a screen: somebody dragging
    across a whole 4K display is asking for 3840 pixels to become 1100,
    which turns 14px body text into 4px and reads nothing at all. The user
    caught it in one sentence - snapping should not be limited, people can
    translate their whole screen.
    He was right, and the reasoning error is worth keeping: I optimised
    the case in front of me (a paragraph on a car page) and set a constant
    that governs every case. The speed problem was never the picture, it
    was the two faults above. So the resolution stays, and a bigger
    capture gets a bigger time budget instead - up to ninety seconds,
    about ten per megapixel. A large snip is a larger job and should be
    treated as one, not failed on principle or silently degraded to fit a
    deadline chosen for something smaller.

  - The rounded snip mark had a sharp corner sticking out of every rounded
    one. The four elbows are arcs, and they were joined with a
    create_rectangle - which draws its own corners, at the true corner of
    the selection, outside the arc that was there to replace them. The
    fix is that the hairline spans only the flats: the corners belong to
    the arcs and to nothing else. Obvious once seen, and it was seen by
    the person using it rather than by the tests, because every test asked
    whether the frame was drawn and in the right place. None asked what it
    looked like.
  - The translated overlay had no edge at all. That was deliberate for a
    while - the whole aim of the renderer is to match the page it draws
    over, and a border argues with that. What it misses is that matching
    the page perfectly is exactly when you cannot tell where the
    translation stops and the original starts, so the effect is not
    seamless but ambiguous. The report was "it doesn't look bad but it
    just maybe disappears", which is the right description of a thing that
    has no boundary.
    So it gets the same mark as the snip frame, which is the same shape as
    the icon: you drag the mark, and what comes back is inside the mark.
    One object, twice. It is drawn into the image rather than added as a
    widget round it, so a zoom redraws it at the new size along with
    everything else.
  - And a margin between the mark and the text, which was the second half
    of the same report. Drawn hard against the edge the frame touches
    whatever line happens to reach the border and reads as a box squeezing
    the text rather than one holding it. The margin is filled from the
    picture's own border colour, not with white: this lands on a real
    page, and a light band round a dark article announces itself as a
    mistake before anybody reads a word of the translation. The window
    moves back by the same amount, because an in-place translation that
    does not land on the text it replaced has lost the only property it
    had.

  - A day of free Gemini allowance went on one screenshot, and the code
    that did it had been written as a safety net. translate_lines sends
    every line in one request joined by newlines and checks the same
    number came back; when they do not, it translates each line
    separately. That fallback is correct, and against a lookup service it
    costs nothing but time.
    What it did not account for is that the cost of a request is not the
    same for every backend, and that the backend most likely to trigger it
    is the one where a request is most expensive. A language model reflows
    text - it merges two short headlines, splits a long one, drops a
    placeholder - so the line count comes back wrong precisely when Gemini
    is answering. Twenty lines became twenty calls. The reporter had made
    "barely any" translations and found the allowance gone, with nothing
    on screen to say where it went.
    The general shape: a fallback written for one failure mode inherits
    the cost model of whatever engine happens to be underneath it. "Try
    harder" is free against a dictionary and ruinous against a model, and
    a retry policy that does not know which one it is talking to will be
    wrong about one of them. So the fallback now asks whether the engine
    in front charges per request, and if it does, hands the whole batch to
    a cheaper one in a single call - a plainer translation of everything,
    rather than an excellent translation of the first four lines and a
    spent allowance.
  - The key test showed one verdict in one colour over four independent
    answers. Gemini out of quota, Google working, Cloud Vision working,
    DeepL absent - all of it concatenated into a line painted red, so a
    key that had just passed its test was displayed as broken. A summary
    of several answers is not an answer; it is an average, and averaging
    verdicts loses exactly the information a verdict is for. Each key has
    a mark beside it now, and there are three states rather than two:
    working, rejected, and could-not-be-judged. Busy and out-of-allowance
    both belong in the third, because neither says anything about the key.
  - A tally for Gemini, which I had argued against and should not have.
    The argument was that the app cannot know the limit, so a counter
    would be half a fact. True - and the half it can supply was the half
    that was missing. Nobody asked "how much is left"; the question was
    "what is Squint spending", and a number would have identified the
    twenty-requests-per-snip bug in a second instead of an evening. The
    denominator was never the point. Google and Cloud Vision counters had
    been sitting three lines above the whole time.
  - Overlays use the lite model and cards use the full one. It is the
    per-job split from 1.8.0 applied one level down, and it fell out of
    the same reasoning: an overlay is headlines and button labels where
    the context a large model brings is worth least and the request count
    is highest, and a card is one passage where that context is the entire
    reason for choosing Gemini. Free-tier limits are listed per model, so
    the two jobs also draw on separate allowances. No new key - the model
    is named in the request path, not in the credential.

  - The pack list was sorted alphabetically and looked wrong, which is the
    only test a sort order has to pass. It sorted on the tag: zh-CN after
    tr-TR, sr-CYRL-RS after sl-SI. Both are in order and neither is in the
    order anybody reading the list perceives, because what is on screen is
    "Chinese" and "Serbian" and the code is the small print after the dash.
    Sorting by the key rather than by the rendering is the same class of
    mistake as sorting filenames by inode: correct, deterministic, and not
    what the list is for. This is the third pass at this one row's order,
    which is itself the lesson - ordering is not a detail you get right by
    reasoning about it once.
  - A refusal arrived as a tally. "On this computer" with no offline engine
    correctly declined to send the text anywhere, and then reported it
    through the ordinary all-backends-failed path: "No translation service
    could be reached. Tried 1 - offline: not installed". Every word true.
    The deliberate message explaining the refusal existed and was reachable
    only when the backend was missing from the table entirely, which never
    happens - so the one path that could actually occur got the generic
    text.
    What made it costly is who was reading it. He had just spent five
    releases installing a Windows language pack called Chinese, and was now
    told that Chinese could not be translated because something was "not
    installed". Two different downloads share a language name: a pack lets
    the machine READ a language, the offline engine TRANSLATES it. Nothing
    in the app had ever said that, and the error message was the place he
    was standing when he needed to know it. It says it now.

  - Two dropdowns became one, and the interesting part is what had to be
    kept when they went. Reading and Translating each offered five
    answers: twenty-five combinations, of which about four are things
    anybody wants. Worse, answering the question at all required knowing
    that Windows reads and cannot translate, that DeepL translates and
    cannot read, and that Gemini does both slowly. The control demanded
    the knowledge it was supposed to save you. Nobody thinks "Windows
    reads and DeepL translates"; they think "use DeepL".
    But the second dropdown was also a display. It was the only place in
    the app that said which engine would answer, and collapsing the pair
    would have thrown that away - which matters more than usual here,
    because the report that started this was "I set both to this computer
    and it returned a Google translation". So the resolved state is
    printed under the control: "Reads with Windows (de, en, zh).
    Translates with Gemini." A control that hides its own consequences is
    how the last three of these entries happened.
  - "On this computer" was sending text to Google, and the rule that made
    it do so was one I had written on purpose. Every chosen engine keeps
    the others behind it, because a preference is not worth a dead app -
    that came from the day Gemini's free quota ran out and the pinned
    backend answered 429 to everything. Correct, and applied one case too
    widely. Falling back from Gemini to Google is a change of supplier
    within a decision the user already made. Falling back from local to
    Google reverses the decision itself, and does it silently, for the one
    person who was most explicit about not wanting it. The generalisation
    was "a preference should not break the app"; the thing it missed is
    that some preferences are not preferences, they are policies, and the
    honest response to a policy that cannot be satisfied is to stop rather
    than to substitute. It refuses now and says why.
  - Automatic decides per job instead of once per session. An overlay is
    twenty short lines with somebody watching the screen; a card is one
    passage about to be read properly. The best engine for those is not
    the same engine, and any single answer is wrong half the time - which
    is exactly what "Gemini is noticeably slow" was. It is slow, it will
    stay slow, it is reasoning about the sentence rather than looking it
    up, and the fix is not to make it faster but to stop asking it the
    questions where speed is what was wanted.
  - A profile whose key is missing is greyed with the reason beside it,
    rather than selectable and then quietly ignored. That is the language
    pack lesson applied before it costs anything: offering a choice that
    cannot be honoured, and honouring something else instead, is worse
    than not offering it.
  - The snip frame is the app's own mark now - four thick rounded elbows
    joined by a hairline, the shape from the icon. Dark under light rather
    than the logo's black on cream, because it has to be legible over a
    black web page and a white one, and matching the brand exactly is
    worth less than being visible. The constants for it (CORNER, GRIP) had
    been sitting in snip.py unused for months.
  - Zoom moved from 40% a notch to about 12%. The first version picked
    five sizes that spanned the useful range, which is the right instinct
    for a menu and the wrong one for a wheel: a wheel is a continuous
    gesture and the steps have to be small enough that it feels like
    turning something rather than choosing from a list.

  - A setting that did not do what it was called. "Overlay text size" wrote
    popup_font_size, which the result card reads and overlay.render never
    saw - it had no size parameter at all. So the person whose complaint
    was "the writing over the page is too small" turned that dial, got a
    bigger card, and reasonably concluded the app was broken. A control
    that silently governs something other than its label is worse than a
    missing control, because it absorbs the attempt to fix the problem.
    One number for both now, and the label says Text size.
  - The overlay shrank text until it fitted, with a floor of seven pixels.
    Seven is not a size anyone reads; it is the size at which the fitting
    loop had run out of options and drew anyway. The mechanism is
    unavoidable - English is about twice the length of Chinese and the box
    was sized for the Chinese - but the response to it was the wrong one.
    Something has to give, and it should be the box rather than the type.
    So the floor is the user's setting, and below it a block spills past
    the gap under its line rather than being trimmed with an ellipsis.
    That is a deliberate trade and worth stating: some overlap with the
    line below, against words that were never drawn at all. Covered words
    can be reached by moving your eye. Trimmed words are gone, and the
    ellipsis does not tell you how much went with it - and the line
    underneath is about to be replaced by its own translation anyway.
  - Zoom, and the reason it redraws rather than magnifies. The obvious
    implementation is to scale the finished image, which makes the letters
    bigger and no clearer - a picture of small text at a larger size,
    which is precisely what somebody squinting at 11px does not want.
    Instead the original image is enlarged, the boxes are scaled with it,
    and the text is drawn again at a size that suits the new boxes. The
    type is real type; only the page behind it is interpolated, and that
    is the half nobody is reading. The user had asked for a magnifying
    button and this is that, done in the direction where it helps.
  - The note about the Gemini allowance gives no number, which took some
    deciding. A counter was the first idea and was dropped: the API does
    not report what is left, so the app could only count its own requests
    and would be blind to anything else using the same key. Google has
    also stopped publishing the free-tier figures - the docs now point at
    a per-account dashboard - and the allowance has moved more than once.
    Any number printed in that panel would be wrong on somebody's machine,
    and a wrong number is worse than no number because it gets believed
    and planned around. So the note says what spends the allowance, what
    happens when it runs out, when it resets, and where the real figure
    lives. It also says the thing that actually helps: Reading text on
    Automatic reads on this computer and only asks Gemini when the local
    reading looks wrong, which roughly halves the spend.

  - I told somebody a thing was impossible while he was watching it happen.
    The install had no percentage, and the reason given was that
    Add-WindowsCapability reports through PowerShell's progress stream,
    which is the one stream that cannot be redirected out of a hidden
    elevated shell. That is true. The conclusion drawn from it - that
    there is no progress to be had - was not, and the person waiting knew
    it was not, because he had run the same install by hand and watched
    DISM draw a bar across his console.
    The mistake is worth naming precisely, because it is not a coding
    mistake. The reasoning stopped at the first mechanism it had reached
    for. Add-WindowsCapability cannot report progress here; therefore
    progress cannot be reported. dism.exe drives the same servicing
    engine, prints a percentage, and had been sitting there the whole
    time. Establishing that one road is blocked is not the same as
    establishing there is no road, and the difference between those two
    is a thing to check rather than assume - especially when saying "this
    cannot be done" to somebody who has just seen it done.
    So the elevated command runs dism.exe with its output redirected
    through cmd - native redirection, because PowerShell's own buffers
    and a progress file that arrives all at once at the end is not
    progress - and the app tails that file. DISM redraws one line with
    backspaces, so the file accumulates every percentage it has passed
    through and the last one is the current one.
    The clock did not go. DISM is quiet for the first stretch while it
    works out what it is doing, an install on the reported machine passed
    five minutes, and if the redirect fails on some machine the reading
    never arrives at all. In both cases the clock is what is left, which
    means the worst case of this change is exactly the behaviour it
    replaced.
  - The pack list is alphabetical now, full stop. It grouped what could be
    added above what was installed, on the reasoning that adding is what
    somebody opens the row to do. The marks make that grouping redundant
    and the grouping makes the alphabet wrong: German was at the bottom
    rather than after Greek. Someone looking for a language they cannot
    find is a worse outcome than someone scrolling past three they have,
    and the person using it said so.
  - The install caption gives the size. It sat directly above a line about
    a 2 GB download - the offline translation engine, a different feature
    entirely - and the two were read as one, so a three-minute install of
    a few megabytes looked like a gigabyte arriving slowly. Neither line
    was wrong. Their being adjacent was.

  - Two lines were one line, and it cost a working install its evidence.
    The row under the pack dropdown held both the caption describing the
    highlighted pack and the answer from the last install, and
    _on_langpack_choice rewrites that caption every time the dropdown is
    touched. So the sequence was: press Install, wait, get an answer, open
    the list to look at something - and the answer is gone, replaced by
    "Free, and nothing to sign up for". The screenshot of "nothing
    happened" was exactly that. A label with two owners belongs to
    whichever one wrote last, and that is not a thing to reason about at
    the call sites; it is a thing to fix by giving the answer its own
    line.
  - The install had no feedback for the whole time it ran, which on the
    reported machine was over four minutes. There is nothing to report -
    Windows fetches the pack through its own channel and says nothing
    until it is finished - so the honest thing is not a progress bar but a
    clock: the pack being installed, the elapsed time, and that it is safe
    to leave open. It is not progress, but it is the difference between
    waiting and wondering whether to press the button again, which is what
    actually happened.
  - The amber warning was recommending the wrong thing first. It ended
    with "add one below", and the Gemini key was mentioned afterwards as
    an aside, so the pack is the path everybody took - the one needing
    administrator rights, several minutes of Windows downloading, and on a
    managed work laptop possibly a refusal. The key takes about a minute,
    needs nothing from the machine and covers every language at once.
    Reordered, with the pack's own reason stated rather than implied:
    nothing leaves the computer and it works with no network. That is a
    real reason to take the harder path, and it is who the pack row is
    for. Being the default suggestion to everybody was not.
    Worth naming the shape of this one: the feature had cost three
    releases and a dozen exchanges, and none of that was visible in the
    sentence that decided which feature people would use. Effort spent on
    a path is not a reason to keep pointing people down it.
  - The pack list marks what is installed with the same filled and hollow
    circles the offline translation list uses, rather than the word
    "(installed)" trailing the line. Both lists sit in the same panel and
    answer the same question, and answering it two ways made them look
    like unrelated controls. Noticed by the person using it, not by me,
    which is the usual way with this sort of thing.

  - The language pack feature had never once worked, and had been
    reporting success the whole time. Two faults, and neither could be
    seen past the other.
    The first was a wrong name. Windows has two vocabularies for a
    language and they are not the same: the pack is called
    Language.OCR~~~zh-CN~0.0.1.0, and zh-Hans-CN is what the OCR engine
    calls the language once that pack is installed. KNOWN_TAGS was
    written from the second one. Everything downstream was then correct
    about a capability that does not exist.
    The second is why nobody found the first. Add-WindowsCapability runs
    in a second, elevated PowerShell through Start-Process -PassThru, and
    the app read that process's exit code. powershell.exe exits 0 when a
    command inside it writes a non-terminating error, which is exactly
    what an unrecognised capability name produces. So 0 meant "the shell
    started", the app read it as "the pack installed", and every failure
    since the feature shipped was reported as a success. The reporter
    installed Chinese, restarted, installed it again, and restarted
    Windows - on the strength of a green line that was never checking
    anything.
    Three changes. The list is transcribed from Get-WindowsCapability on
    a real machine rather than reasoned out - it was also offering hi-IN,
    th-TH, vi-VN, uk-UA and he-IL, which have no OCR packs at all, and
    missing bg-BG, es-MX, fr-CA, hr-HR, pt-PT, sk-SK, sl-SI, zh-HK and
    both Serbians, which do. The elevated command checks the capability
    exists, runs with -ErrorAction Stop so a failure is catchable, reads
    the state back afterwards rather than assuming, and exits with a
    distinct code per outcome; only the code that means "Windows confirms
    the pack is on" reads as success. And it goes over as
    -EncodedCommand, because a name quoted through two shells by hand is
    a thing that works until it does not.
    The general lesson is about the exit code rather than about Windows.
    A success signal that cannot distinguish "it worked" from "the thing
    that would have told you ran" is not a success signal, and wiring one
    up is worse than having none - it converts every failure into a lie
    and stops anybody looking.
  - zh-CN and zh-Hans-CN were compared as strings in two places, so a
    pack the app had just installed did not match the language the engine
    reported. That is what made the restart advice permanent: the check
    could never see the pack, so it could never stop asking. Both places
    now share one comparison that splits a tag into language, script and
    region and requires a script or region to agree only when both sides
    give one - so zh-CN matches zh-Hans-CN, and sr-CYRL-RS still does not
    match sr-LATN-RS, because those really are two different packs.

  - A language pack installed successfully and the panel around it went on
    saying it had not been. The green line said "Installed"; the amber
    line above it still said "This PC can only read de, en", the reading
    note still listed the same two, and the dropdown still offered the
    pack that had just gone on. Every one of those is built once, when the
    settings panel is built, and _langpack_done refreshed exactly one of
    them. What the user reads is not four labels but one screen, and that
    screen said the install had failed.
    The interesting part was the restart sentence. It said Windows "may
    want a restart", every time, whether or not one was needed - which is
    the shape of a guess written into a string. It is not knowable in
    advance: nothing on the Python side caches the language list, but
    whether the OCR engine caches it for the life of the process is
    undocumented, and Windows can still be finishing the download after
    the elevated command has returned. So the app looks instead of
    guessing. resolve_tag is asked whether the new pack answers, and the
    sentence follows the answer - "Squint can read Chinese now", or a
    restart and a button that does it. A maybe that is printed every time
    carries no information and teaches people to skip the line.
  - The grey note under the row was quoting PowerShell. Windows refuses to
    list its capabilities without elevation, which is expected and
    harmless and already explained in that note; the stderr was then
    appended to it and truncated at 120 characters, so what arrived on
    screen was "(Get-WindowsCapability : The". A sentence that stops
    mid-clause reads as a crash, not as a footnote. It is kept in
    last_error, which is where a bug report can find it.
  - The App language row came back. Removing it was mine and it was the
    wrong call: "English only for now" is a fact about the list, and
    hiding the control turns it into a fact about the feature. The
    reasoning at the time - a dropdown with one item cannot do anything -
    is true and answers a question nobody asked. What somebody notices is
    that the setting they saw last week is gone. So the row stays, with
    one language in it and a line underneath saying more are coming, and
    the eleven kept translations appear there as they are finished.
    The line is beside the list rather than in it, because a greyed-out
    "Deutsch (soon)" is a language you can point at and cannot choose,
    which is a worse control than a short one.

  - The Windows pack button was installing the wrong language, and the
    mistake was in the question rather than the code. It took the language
    from the source dropdown, so somebody whose source was English pressed
    Install and was told Windows does not offer a pack for en-US: true,
    useless, and nothing to do with the Chinese they were trying to read.
    "What am I reading right now" and "which pack should this computer
    have" are different questions. The second one is the useful one, and
    the answer to it is a list.
    So it lists what Windows actually offers on this edition, marks what is
    already installed, and installs the one that is chosen. It is shown on
    Windows always rather than only after a failure - somebody who knows
    they will be reading Chinese next week should be able to add it today,
    and the row was previously hidden until the app had already got it
    wrong once. Asking Windows takes a second or two of PowerShell, so it
    happens off the main thread and the row fills itself in.
    Naming the packs needed a small map. Windows names them by script
    (zh-Hans-CN) and the app's language list is by region (zh-CN); without
    joining those up every Chinese pack came out labelled "zh-Hans-CN" with
    no name, and with a naive join every Chinese pack came out labelled
    "Chinese" and the list was unusable.
  - Twelve app languages, each named in its own language. A list of
    languages written in English is no use to somebody who cannot read
    English, which is the entire audience for the setting.
    Changing it offers a Restart now button rather than an instruction.
    Every label is built when the window is built, so nothing on screen
    changes until the app starts again; "restart Squint to see the change",
    left sitting under the dropdown, makes the setting look as though it
    did nothing, and people press it again.
    The long explanatory paragraphs are still English. Translating a
    heading is safe and translating a paragraph of advice badly is worse
    than leaving it - a mistranslated instruction about API keys sends
    somebody to the wrong page. That pass is worth doing properly rather
    than quickly.
  - The selection shortcut knows whether anything was actually copied.
    Reported from a German classifieds page: text selected, shortcut
    pressed, and back came Squint's own release notes - copied ten minutes
    earlier to paste into GitHub - labelled "selection".
    Comparing the clipboard before and after cannot catch that, and the
    reason is in the first half of the comparison rather than the second.
    It asks "is the clipboard different from what I read a moment ago", so
    if that read comes back empty - another program holding the clipboard
    open, a format Tk cannot see, a moment's bad luck - then whatever was
    already there is different from "" and reads as a fresh copy.
    Windows keeps a counter that increments whenever anything is put on the
    clipboard by anyone. It needs no handle, opens nothing and cannot be
    fooled by the contents: if it has not moved, nothing was copied. It
    also fixes the opposite mistake, which nobody had noticed yet -
    selecting the same sentence twice was reported as "nothing selected"
    the second time, because the text had not changed.
    There is no such counter on macOS or Linux, so the comparison is still
    what runs there. Worth being explicit about rather than assuming a fix
    applies everywhere.
  - An engine that cannot run no longer looks like it is running. With
    "Offline, on this computer" chosen and no offline model downloaded,
    every translation went to the free online service - the dropdown said
    one thing, the result card said another, and nothing joined them up.
    Settings says which and why now, in amber, and the same for a keyed
    engine chosen with no key saved.
    The falling back stays. It is what keeps the app working when a service
    is down, and refusing to translate because the preferred engine is
    missing would be worse. Doing it silently was the fault.
  - The Windows text-recognition pack can be installed from inside the app.
    This is the rung below every API key and the only one that cannot run
    out. Windows recognises exactly the languages it has packs for, so a PC
    in an English office pointed at Chinese does not fail - it returns
    confident nonsense, which is then translated and drawn over the page.
    Cloud Vision fixes that and wants a credit card. Gemini fixes it and
    wants an account and has a daily allowance this project has already
    managed to exhaust. A Windows pack is free, needs no account, no card
    and no quota, and once it is on the machine it stays.
    What existed before was a sentence describing where the setting is:
    Settings > Time & language > Language & region > Add a language, then
    that language's options, then Optical character recognition. Six steps
    in somebody else's app, handed to a person who has just been told the
    thing they tried does not work. Most people stop at step two.
    Two things are said before it starts rather than found out afterwards.
    It needs administrator rights, because Add-WindowsCapability is a
    system change and Windows asks; on a work laptop the answer may be no,
    and that is reported as an answer and not as a fault, since nothing an
    app can do gets round a policy. And it takes minutes, because Windows
    downloads it - through its own update channel. Squint fetches nothing
    itself, which is also why this does not trip the defences that once
    blocked the Python installer and stopped anybody installing anything.
    The capability name is checked against the shape Windows uses before it
    goes anywhere near a PowerShell command, and a test tries to get a
    semicolon through it.
  - The window uses the space it is given. Three screenshots of the same
    window at three sizes: the controls and both text boxes in exactly the
    same place each time, with a growing band of empty grey beneath them.
    At the largest size more than half the window was nothing, which reads
    as a window that has not finished drawing rather than as a design.
    The boxes asking for the height their content needs is right - it is
    what keeps the window small when there are two words in it. The mistake
    was that asking was the whole story: the requested height was also the
    ceiling, so a window somebody had deliberately enlarged had nowhere to
    put the extra. The request still follows the content, and the grid is
    now allowed to stretch them past it.
    An older test asserted the opposite - that the boxes are never
    stretched - because that was the rule when it was written. It is
    rewritten rather than deleted: the rule it was really protecting is
    that an empty box asks to be small, which is still true and is what it
    checks now.
  - A shortcut of one modifier and one letter is called out. The reporting
    machine had Cmd+E, which registers perfectly and then loses to
    whichever app is frontmost: it works on the desktop, it works in
    Finder, and it does nothing in the browser the user was trying to read,
    because Cmd+E is Use Selection for Find across macOS. Ctrl+E is the
    same story in Office and in every browser address bar.
    A warning rather than a refusal. The shortcut does work, some people
    will have chosen it deliberately, and refusing on upgrade would take
    away a shortcut somebody has been using for months - a worse thing to
    do to them than the collision. Function keys are not flagged: almost
    nothing binds Cmd+F5, and a warning that fires on safe combinations is
    one people learn to ignore.
  - Everything the OS holds is given back on the way out, including when
    the app is killed rather than quit - a global shortcut still registered
    by a dead process does nothing until the machine is restarted. The
    hotkeys and the tray icon are also released independently now: they
    shared one try block, so a hotkey manager that threw on the way out
    left the tray icon on screen.
  - Testing a key was spending the key. The retry added two builds ago made
    three attempts per model across up to three models, and Save & test
    runs that twice - once for translating and once for reading. Eighteen
    requests, worst case, to answer "does this key work". A request that
    times out on this side has usually still been counted on Google's, so
    chasing a timeout by pressing the button repeatedly is a way to spend a
    day's free allowance on tests. It asks once now.
    This is a straightforward own goal: the retry was added to stop a busy
    minute looking like a broken key, and it made testing the key expensive
    enough to break it a different way.
  - A spent allowance is believed for fifteen minutes. Otherwise every snip
    pays a round-trip to be refused before this computer's own reader takes
    over, which makes the app slower at the moment it has stopped being
    able to use the thing that was making it slow. A rest rather than a
    write-off, because the limits are per minute as well as per day.
  - Settings > Test connection says which layer is refusing.
    Three different faults have now reached the user as the same red line
    beside their API key - the service was busy, the service was slow, and
    something in between - and each time the app's honest position was "I
    do not know yet". That is not a thing to make somebody rediscover by
    making another key, which is what happens because the key is the only
    part of the picture they can change.
    So it asks the four questions in the order they can fail: does the name
    resolve, does a connection open, does the encrypted handshake finish,
    does Google answer. No key is needed - being refused by Google with a
    401 proves every layer underneath it works.
    The third question is the one that matters on a company network. A
    proxy that opens encrypted traffic to inspect it presents its own
    certificate; Windows has been told to trust it, so the browser is
    content, and Python has not, so the app fails. That is exactly the
    shape of "generativelanguage.googleapis.com opens in Brave but Squint
    times out", and nothing in the app could previously say so.
    The same report is in diagnose.bat and diagnose.command, and goes to
    the log on every press, because the useful version of this is the one
    that can be sent to somebody.
  - "The read operation timed out" was being shown as a rejected key.
    urllib does not wrap a read timeout the way it wraps other network
    failures - it comes back as a bare socket error partway through reading
    the reply, so it went past every handler in the module and arrived in
    the user's face as six words in red beside a key that was fine.
    It is caught now, and treated as the same kind of thing as a 503: the
    service did not answer this time and may well answer next time. So it
    retries, then tries the next model, then hands over to another engine.
    The wait was 20 seconds, inherited from the 5-second default meant for
    small REST calls. Generating waits 45 now and reading a picture 75.
    And the message names the host. Gemini is on
    generativelanguage.googleapis.com, which is a different domain from the
    Cloud APIs - so a corporate network can allow translation and vision
    while blocking this one, and every symptom of that points at the key.
    Somebody whose office network does that would otherwise spend an
    afternoon making new keys.
  - Screenshots are scaled down before they are sent. A whole 4K monitor is
    several megabytes as PNG and a third larger again once base64 has had
    it, and on an office uplink that upload was most of the wait. The
    reading is no better for the extra pixels: text stays legible well
    below native resolution. The boxes are unaffected because they come
    back on a 0-1000 scale of the picture, and are mapped onto the original
    size rather than the shrunken one - which is a thing to get wrong once
    and is covered by a test.
---
  - "This model is currently experiencing high demand" is not a broken key.
    Gemini's free tier answers 503 often. The code treated every error that
    was not a missing model as fatal, so one 503 ended the call - and
    Settings then printed it in red next to the key, which is how a key
    made thirty seconds earlier was reported as rejected on the day it was
    made. Nothing was wrong with it and nothing the user could do would
    have helped; the reasonable conclusion was "Gemini always looks busy".
    A busy model is asked again after a short pause, because a spike is
    usually seconds long. If it stays busy the next model is tried, because
    capacity is per model and "flash is busy" says nothing about
    flash-lite. Only when everything is busy does it give up, and then it
    says which side the problem is on.
    The retry is bounded on purpose. A shortcut that does nothing for ten
    seconds is its own bug, so it is two short waits per model and then
    somebody else's turn - translation falls through to another engine and
    reading falls back to this computer.
  - A busy model is not forgotten. The remembered model name was cleared
    whenever it failed to answer, which is right when the model has been
    retired and wrong when it is merely busy: every busy minute then cost
    an extra request to list the models again, on the tier where requests
    are the thing being rationed.
---
  - Gemini reads the screen, which is the rung that has no credit card on
    it. Cloud Vision is the better reader and stays the default where it is
    configured - it measures where text sits instead of estimating, and its
    terms keep the picture out of anyone's training data. It has a problem
    that is nothing to do with quality: Google Cloud wants card details
    before it will let anyone touch even the free tier. The card is
    identity checking rather than payment, but from the outside it is a
    stranger's app asking for card details, and four testers correctly said
    no. A Gemini key is a Google account and a minute.
    The boxes are the interesting part. Vision returns a quadrilateral it
    measured; a language model returns four numbers it estimated. Where
    fewer than most of the lines come back with a box that describes a real
    rectangle on the picture, nothing is drawn over the page at all and the
    card is used instead - a translation printed on top of the sentence it
    is translating is worse than one beside it.
    One snip is one request, not two: the overlay asks for placed lines and
    the card asks for the text, and the reply is cached by the picture. The
    allowance being spent is a daily request count.
  - Settings > Get a free key opens the page and then fills the key in.
    Google shows a new key once. What happens otherwise is that people
    press copy, get distracted by the browser, come back to an empty field
    with no way to tell whether they copied it, and make another one - and
    end up with four keys and no idea which is in use. So Squint watches
    its own clipboard while that tab is open and types the key in itself.
    Deliberately narrow, because a clipboard watcher that runs the rest of
    the time is spyware however kindly meant: only after that button, only
    the two shapes Google issues, only until the first match, only for a
    few minutes. A test walks the syntax tree to prove nothing else starts
    one.
  - The Mac copies the selection. This was never the permission.
    Squint showed a "Reading selection..." pill before sending the copy,
    because a shortcut that does nothing visible for half a second reads as
    a shortcut that did not work. On macOS, showing a window brings the
    application to the front: Tk maps the toplevel and the system activates
    the process that owns it. The copy was then delivered to whatever was
    frontmost - Squint, and Squint's own pill, which has nothing selected
    in it. The browser one window behind never saw the keystroke.
    From outside it was indistinguishable from the Accessibility failure,
    and it had the same workaround: press Cmd+C by hand and the shortcut
    works. It survived the permission being granted, which is the only
    reason it was found rather than blamed on Apple.
    Nothing is drawn before the copy on macOS now. The pill still appears,
    for the translation, which is the slower half anyway. Windows keeps it
    first - there is no such rule there, and the wait feels dead without
    something on screen.
---
  - The Mac permission explains itself where you are looking. (ay) stopped
    the card lying about where its text came from - it says "clipboard"
    when it fell back to the clipboard - but the reason went to a tray
    notification, and on macOS that goes through pystray and on at least
    one Mac arrives nowhere. So the shortcut over a German paragraph
    returned a GitHub URL, correctly labelled and entirely unexplained.
    The explanation is the card now, at the pointer, once per run, and the
    stale clipboard is not translated alongside it - a translation next to
    the explanation is what buries the explanation.
  - Squint asks macOS for the permission rather than describing where to
    find it. "Switch on the entry for Squint under Accessibility" is advice
    about a row that does not exist: macOS lists an app only once it has
    asked. Without that, the instruction ends in someone pressing + and
    hunting for a Python binary inside a .venv, which is not a reasonable
    thing to ask of anybody.
  - Settings > Shortcuts says whether copying is allowed, with a button to
    fix it. Before it fails rather than after: Apple's refusal is silent by
    design, and the way it otherwise reaches the user is a translation of
    something they copied earlier, which reads as the app being broken.
  - The release zip is called Squint.zip. A release went out where the zip
    arrived on GitHub as Squint2026.08.31ay.zip while version.json pointed
    at Squint-2026.08.31-ay.zip - byte-identical file, correct checksum,
    404 on every attempt to install it, and an hour to find because
    everything else looked right. The build number in the filename bought
    nothing the tag did not already carry; all it did was give the name
    four more characters to lose.
  - A missing zip no longer says "No release published yet, or the update
    address is wrong" - shown, in that case, by a window that was at the
    same moment displaying that release's notes. The two 404s have
    different causes and different fixes, and now different sentences.
  - make_release.py --verify checks a published release the way the app
    does: fetch version.json, download the zip it names, compare the
    checksum. Publishing is four manual steps in a browser, every one of
    them can half-succeed, and all the ways it can fail produce the same
    symptom - the update is offered and will not install.
---
  - Selection translation works on a Mac, and stops lying when it does not.
    macOS will not let a program press Cmd+C on the user's behalf unless it
    has been ticked under Privacy & Security > Accessibility, and it refuses
    silently: the keystroke is discarded, nothing errors, nothing is logged.
    From the app's side that is identical to "nothing was selected", so the
    shortcut fell through to its fallback, translated whatever was already on
    the clipboard, and labelled the result "selection".
    That label is what made it dangerous rather than annoying. Pressing the
    shortcut over a paragraph returned an API key copied ten minutes earlier,
    presented as the thing being pointed at - and the only way to find out
    was to screenshot it, which put the key in a picture too. It also
    explains "the same translation keeps coming back": it was the same
    clipboard every time.
    So the permission is asked about before the copy is attempted, the reason
    is shown once instead of being swallowed, and the fallback says
    "clipboard" because that is where the text came from.
    Asking is done through the system framework directly rather than pyobjc:
    AXIsProcessTrusted lives in ApplicationServices, which is a separate
    pyobjc package from the one Squint installs, so the obvious import fails
    on exactly the Macs this is for - and a failed check means "assume
    permission", which is the bug again.
  - A snip works on every monitor. Each display gets its own overlay, and
    the first one was taking a Tk grab. A grab routes every mouse event in
    the whole application to the window holding it, which is right when one
    window covers everything and wrong now that there are three: clicks on
    the second and third monitors went to the first one, which was not under
    the pointer, so those screens could not be snipped at all.
---
  - Points at github.com/ratanpranay/squint for updates.
  - Squint looks for a new build once a day, on its own. An Update button
    in Settings is a button nobody presses: most people never open Settings
    again after the first day, so a release would sit there unnoticed and
    the whole update mechanism would be theatre.
    It fetches one small file and, if there is something new, puts a line
    under the title. It never downloads or installs anything by itself, it
    says it once per build - having been told and not installed is a
    decision, not an oversight - and it is not a dialog, because an update
    is never urgent enough to interrupt what someone is in the middle of.
    A box over the document you were reading is what teaches people to
    dismiss update prompts unread. Switch it off in Settings > Updates.
  - A "Buy me a coffee" button in the footer, as quiet as the copyright
    line beside it. The address is one line in the code or a donate_url
    setting, because the link that actually works can only be confirmed by
    the person whose account it is.
---
  - The clipboard shortcut copies for you. Select text, press it, read the
    translation. It used to translate whatever you had already copied,
    which made reading one sentence three actions - select, Ctrl+C,
    shortcut - and forgetting the middle one produced "copy something
    first": true, and beside the point. Nobody wants to copy anything.
    Two things make this less trivial than "send Ctrl+C". The shortcut is
    Ctrl+Alt+C, so at the moment it fires those modifiers are physically
    down, and sending Ctrl+C then delivers Ctrl+Alt+C to the other program
    - the shortcut it just ignored. So it waits for the keys to come up.
    And a copy can silently do nothing: an image, a locked PDF, a window
    with no selection. The clipboard is compared before and after, because
    without that the app would confidently translate whatever you copied an
    hour ago and present it as the answer.
    Text you copied yourself still works exactly as before, and your own
    clipboard is put back afterwards unless the app was going to fill it
    with the translation anyway.
---
  - Clipboard translation reads Chinese. Copying Chinese from a browser and
    pressing the shortcut said "Clipboard has no text" about a clipboard
    that plainly had text in it.
    Tk's clipboard_get() asks the system for the STRING target, which on
    Windows is CF_TEXT - the ANSI clipboard format, from before Unicode,
    holding text in the machine's legacy code page. On an English-locale PC
    no code page can hold 前不久, so CF_TEXT is either absent or full of
    question marks and the call fails. Windows had the real text in
    CF_UNICODETEXT the whole time; that is what is read now, with Tk as the
    fallback rather than the other way round. Elsewhere Tk is fine, as long
    as UTF8_STRING is asked for before STRING - STRING is Latin-1 by the
    ICCCM, so asking for it first gets mojibake.
    The same fix applies to the paste button in the Translate pane.
  - When the clipboard really is empty it now says what to do about it
    rather than only stating the fact.
---
  - Settings > Updates. Check, download, install, restart - no re-download
    and no second setup. It works because the app runs from source: a build
    is the code, a few hundred kilobytes, while the slow part of an install
    is the .venv, which does not change between builds. So an update
    replaces code and never touches the environment - which is also the
    only way this can be done at all on Windows, where a running python.exe
    cannot be replaced. Settings, keys and downloaded languages live
    outside the app folder and are never in the blast radius.
    It refuses anything it cannot verify: a download whose checksum does
    not match what the release published, an archive that does not contain
    app/main.py, a zip that tries to write outside the folder. The previous
    code is kept for one generation, so a bad update can be rolled back
    rather than reinstalled, and a failure partway through puts everything
    back rather than leaving half a build installed.
  - tools/make_release.py builds the release zip and the version.json that
    points at it, with the checksum filled in. Doing that by hand is how a
    release goes out whose checksum does not match its zip - which the app
    then correctly refuses, in a way that looks like the updater being
    broken.
    It refuses to build if it finds anything shaped like an API key in any
    file it is about to publish. Keys live in the user's data folder and
    have no business in a release; the way it would happen anyway is one
    pasted into a source file to test something and forgotten.
  - Settings > Learn how to add APIs. What a key is, which one you want,
    what to click - for Google, DeepL and Gemini, with the cost of each and
    a button that opens the right page. It opens with "you may not need any
    of this", because the app does work without keys and a wall of setup
    instructions implies otherwise.
---
  - setup.bat installs Python again, through winget. On two test machines
    this was the step people gave up at, so it needed to come back - but
    not the way it was done before. The old version fetched python.exe
    into %TEMP% with a PowerShell policy override and ran it /quiet, which
    is the exact shape of a malware dropper; Defender blocked the whole
    download and the zip never reached anyone. winget is Microsoft's own
    package manager, already on the machine: it does the downloading and
    installing, so nothing in this script fetches or executes anything and
    there is no heuristic to trip.
    It also puts the new Python on this window's PATH itself. PATH is read
    when a console opens, so a Python installed thirty seconds ago is not
    on it - and "close this and run setup again" is where people give up
    the second time. Falls back to python.org if winget is missing or a
    policy blocks it.
  - The selection box is back. The multi-monitor rewrite gave every display
    its own overlay, screenshot and selection state, which is the right fix
    and is kept - but one attribute did not come across with it. `_bright`,
    the undimmed picture the bright selection patch is cut out of, was
    still read on every mouse-move and set nowhere.
    The way it failed is worth knowing: Tk catches an exception raised
    inside a callback, prints it to a console nobody is watching, and
    carries on. So the drag handler died on its first line, before it drew
    anything, while the release handler - which never touches that picture
    - went on working. A snip that translated correctly with no visible
    box at all: a dead code path wearing the costume of a cosmetic bug.
  - A drag now stays on the display it started on. Moving the pointer onto
    another monitor fires <Enter> there, which would have swapped the start
    point and the canvas out from under the stroke in progress.
  - The four older snip tests were still feeding their fake desktop to the
    old whole-desktop capture, which the picker no longer calls - so they
    were quietly running against the real screen and proving nothing. They
    supply monitors now.
---
  - The selection box is back. The multi-monitor rewrite gave every display
    its own overlay, screenshot and selection state, which is the right fix
    and is kept - but one attribute did not come across with it. `_bright`,
    the undimmed picture the bright selection patch is cut out of, was
    still read on every mouse-move and set nowhere.
    The way it failed is worth knowing: Tk catches an exception raised
    inside a callback, prints it to a console nobody is watching, and
    carries on. So the drag handler died on its first line, before it drew
    anything, while the release handler - which never touches that picture
    - went on working. A snip that translated correctly with no visible
    box at all: a dead code path wearing the costume of a cosmetic bug.
  - A drag now stays on the display it started on. Moving the pointer onto
    another monitor fires <Enter> there, which would have swapped the start
    point and the canvas out from under the stroke in progress.
  - The four older snip tests were still feeding their fake desktop to the
    old whole-desktop capture, which the picker no longer calls - so they
    were quietly running against the real screen and proving nothing. They
    supply monitors now.
  - New test: a real drag through the real handlers, checking what actually
    ends up on the canvas, plus a check that every attribute the drag path
    reads is one the monitor switch sets. Both fail without the fix.
  - Settings > Set up Google. A checklist of the three things that have to
    be true - a key, Cloud Translation switched on, Cloud Vision switched
    on - each with a button that opens the exact console page that fixes
    it, and a dot that says whether it is actually done. The dots are not
    assumed: it asks Google.
    This exists because all four ways it can fail come back as the same
    bare 403, and the fixes are in different places. The checklist tells
    apart "the API is off", "the key does not include this API", "the
    project has no billing account" and "that is not a valid key", and
    points at the right page for each. It saves the key only once the key
    has actually done something - a rejected key left in the box makes the
    app look configured while it quietly uses the free services.
  - The card closes when you click somewhere else. It did not, and the
    reason was that Tk's app-wide click binding is app-wide in the literal
    sense: a click on Outlook or a browser goes to that program and Tk
    never hears about it. The button is now read from the OS directly,
    eight times a second, which is the only way to know.
  - The card no longer floats above every other window. It opens on top,
    so it cannot appear behind the page it just translated, and becomes an
    ordinary window a second and a half later.
  - Watermarks are left out of the translation. A document tiled with a
    name and address in pale grey on a diagonal was being read correctly,
    translated, and drawn back across the sentences underneath - and
    because a diagonal box is tall, in enormous type. A line is now
    ignored when two of three things are true: it repeats three or more
    times, it is tilted more than six degrees, or it is too faint to be
    ink. Two signals, not one, because a repeated line might be a table
    heading and a pale one might be a caption.
  - Automatic asks Google sooner. A Chinese page read as four lines, only
    one of them obviously nonsense, passed the old 60%-junk test and got
    translated. Any jumbled word on a page with no Chinese, Japanese,
    Korean, Cyrillic or Arabic in it is now enough to get a second
    opinion - it costs one unit in a thousand, and being wrong costs the
    whole snip.
  - Windows no longer reads Chinese with an English recogniser. Windows
    does no language detection and reports no confidence: it reads one
    language per pass, and with none chosen the app was letting the PC's
    own display language decide - English. Pointed at Chinese it does not
    fail, it returns nonsense ("探店东风风神L8Y" came back as
    "iRERJXIJXFL8Y"), which was then translated and drawn over the page.
    Every pack the PC has is now tried and the readings compared, with
    real script beating a Latin misreading.
  - Reading text (OCR) is a dropdown now, beside Translating. It was a
    plain label with the cloud reader hidden behind a switch in another
    card, so a reader that had just been added could not be found at all -
    the only list on screen was a list of translators. Three choices:
    automatic (this computer, Google behind it), this computer only, or
    Google Cloud Vision for everything.
  - Google Cloud Vision reads the screen when the computer cannot. It uses
    the Google key already saved for translation, needs nothing installed,
    and is only ever reached after the machine's own reader has failed or
    come back with nonsense. 1,000 screenshots a month are free; the count
    is in Settings beside the character count.
    One snip costs one request, not two, even when the overlay falls back
    to the card.
  - Save & test now tests the key for reading as well as translating. A new
    project has Cloud Vision switched off and a restricted key excludes it;
    both arrive as a bare 403, and both are now explained with the page to
    fix them on.
  - A reading that is mostly jumble is never drawn over your screen.
  - Settings > Engines says which languages this PC can read, and
    diagnose.bat reports the same list.
  - The UI work of build (al) is included: lighter type in the buttons, one
    height for every control, labels that cannot overflow their boxes, and
    text boxes that follow their contents.
